) reduces to the indices
q=p
,
\ell_{1}=\dots=\ell_{q+1}=0
. As a consequence

F_{p}^{\tau}(Z)=\int_{0}^{\tau}G^{\tau^{\prime}}_{p}(Z)\,{\rm d}\tau^{\prime}

proving (
2.124
).
∎

2.4
Pluri-homogeneous differential geometry

In this section we introduce pluri-homogeneous
k
-forms.
We revisit the classical identities of differential geometry (
{\rm d}^{2}=0
, Cartan’s magic formula) for
k=0,1,2
which are the only cases needed in this paper.

Definition 2.27
.

(
r
-homogeneous
k
-form)

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

k=0,1,2
and set
r:=p+2-k
.
A
r
-homogeneous
k
-form is a
(r+k)
-linear map from
(\dot{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}^{2}))^{r}\times(\dot{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}^{2}))^{k}
to
\mathbb{C}
of the form
\Lambda(U_{1},\dots,U_{r})[V_{1},\dots,V_{k}]
, symmetric in the variables

{\mathcal{U}}:=(U_{1},\dots,U_{r})
and antisymmetric in the entries
{\mathcal{V}}:=(V_{1},\dots,V_{k})
, satisfying the following:
there are constants
C>0
and
m\geq 0
such that

\displaystyle|\Lambda

\displaystyle(\Pi_{n_{1}}U_{1},\dots,\Pi_{n_{r}}U_{r})[\Pi_{n_{r+1}}V_{1},\dots,\Pi_{n_{r+k}}V_{k}]|

\displaystyle\leq C{\rm max}\{n_{1},\dots,n_{r+k}\}^{m}\prod_{j=1}^{r}\|\Pi_{n_{j}}U_{j}\|_{L^{2}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\|\Pi_{n_{r+\ell}}V_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}}

(2.126)

for any
\mathcal{U}\in(\dot{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}^{2}))^{r}
, any

\mathcal{V}\in(\dot{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}^{2}))^{k}
, and any

(n_{1},\ldots,n_{r+k})
in
\mathbb{N}^{r+k}
.
Moreover, if

\Lambda(\Pi_{n_{1}}U_{1},\dots,\Pi_{n_{r}}U_{r})[\Pi_{n_{r+1}}V_{1},\dots,\Pi_{n_{r+k}}V_{k}]\neq 0\,,

then there is a choice of signs
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{r+k}\in\{\pm 1\}
such that

\sum_{j=1}^{r+k}\sigma_{j}n_{j}=0
.
In addition we require the translation invariant property:

\Lambda(\tau_{\varsigma}\mathcal{U})[\tau_{\varsigma}{\mathcal{V}}]=\Lambda(\mathcal{U})[{\mathcal{V}}]\,,\quad\forall\varsigma\in\mathbb{R}\,.

(2.127)

We also require that
\Lambda({\mathcal{U}})[{\mathcal{V}}]
is real valued for any

{\mathcal{U}}\in(L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}))^{r}
and
{\mathcal{V}}\in(L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}))^{k}
, cfr. (
2.13
).
We denote by
\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{k}_{r}
the space of
r
-homogeneous
k
-forms
and by
\Sigma_{r}^{N}\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{k}_{q}
the space of pluri–homogeneous
k
-forms.
We set
\Sigma_{r}\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{k}_{q}:=\cup_{N\geq r}\Sigma_{r}^{N}\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{k}_{q}
.

\bullet
A
r
-homogeneous
0
-form is also called a homogeneous
Hamiltonian
.

\bullet

Fourier representation of
(p+2)
-homogeneous
0
-forms:

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. A
(p+2)
-homogeneous
0
-form can be expressed in Fourier as
(recall (
2.10
))

H(U)=\frac{1}{p+2}\sum_{(\vec{\jmath}_{p+2},\vec{\sigma}_{p+2})\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p+2}}H_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+2}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+2}}u_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+2}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+2}}\,.

(2.128)

The reality condition
H(U)\in\mathbb{R}
for any
U\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
amounts to

\overline{H_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+2}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+2}}}=H_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+2}}^{-\vec{\sigma}_{p+2}}\,.

(2.129)

Moreover the scalar coefficients
H_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+2}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+2}}\equiv H_{\ j_{1},\dots,j_{p+2}}^{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{p+2}}\in\mathbb{C}

satisfy the symmetric condition: for any permutation
\pi
of
\{1,\ldots,p+2\}

H_{\ j_{\pi(1)},\dots,j_{\pi(p+2)}}^{\sigma_{\pi(1)},\dots,\sigma_{\pi(p+2)}}=H_{\ j_{1},\dots,j_{p+2}}^{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{p+2}}\,

(2.130)

and, for some
m\geq 0
, the bound

|H_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+2}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+2}}|\lesssim\max(|j_{1}|,\ldots,|j_{p+2}|)^{m}\,.

(2.131)

\bullet

Fourier representation of
(p+1)
-homogeneous
1
-forms:

A
(p+1)
-homogeneous
1
-form can be expressed in Fourier as

\theta(U)[V]=\sum_{(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k,\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},\sigma)\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p+2}}\Theta_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},\sigma}u_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1}}v_{k}^{\sigma}\,.

(2.132)

The reality condition
\theta(U)[V]\in\mathbb{R}
for any
U,V\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
amounts to

\overline{\Theta_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},\sigma}}=\Theta_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{-\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},-\sigma}\,.

(2.133)

Moreover the coefficients satisfy, for some
m\geq 0
,

|\Theta_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},\sigma}|\lesssim\max(|j_{1}|,\ldots,|j_{p+1}|)^{m}\,.

(2.134)

\bullet

Fourier representation of
p
-homogeneous
2
-forms:

A
p
-homogeneous
2
-form can be expressed in Fourier as

\Lambda(U)[V_{1},V_{2}]=\sum_{(\vec{\jmath}_{p},j,k,\vec{\sigma}_{p},\sigma^{\prime},\sigma)\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p+2}}\Lambda_{\vec{\jmath}_{p},j,k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p},\sigma^{\prime},\sigma}u_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}(v_{1})_{j}^{\sigma^{\prime}}(v_{2})_{k}^{\sigma}\,.

(2.135)

The antisymmetry condition
\Lambda(U)[V_{1},V_{2}]=-\Lambda(U)[V_{2},V_{1}]
amounts to

\Lambda_{\vec{\jmath}_{p},j,k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p},\sigma^{\prime},\sigma}=-\Lambda_{\vec{\jmath}_{p},k,j}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p},\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}\,.

(2.136)

The reality condition
\Lambda(U)[V_{1},V_{2}]\in\mathbb{R}
for any
U,V_{1},V_{2}\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
amounts to

\overline{\Lambda_{\vec{\jmath}_{p},j,k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p},\sigma^{\prime},\sigma}}=\Lambda_{\vec{\jmath}_{p},j,k}^{-\vec{\sigma}_{p},-\sigma^{\prime},-\sigma}\,.

(2.137)

Moreover the coefficients satisfy, for some
m\geq 0
,

|\Lambda_{\vec{\jmath}_{p},j,k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p},\sigma^{\prime},\sigma}|\lesssim\max(|\vec{\jmath}_{p}|,|j|)^{m}\,.

(2.138)

Definition 2.28

(
Homogeneous vector fields).

Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
and
p,N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. We denote by
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{p+1}^{m}
the space of
(p+1)
-homogeneous vector fields of the form
X(U)=M(U)U
where
M(U)
is a matrix of
p
-homogeneous
m
-operators in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}_{p}^{m}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
We denote
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{p+1}:=\cup_{m\geq 0}\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{p+1}^{m}

and

\Sigma_{p+1}^{N+1}\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{m}_{q}
the class of pluri-homogeneous vector fields.
We also set
\Sigma_{p+1}\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{q}:=\bigcup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\Sigma_{p+1}^{N+1}\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{q}
.
The vector fields in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{p+1}^{-\varrho}
,
\varrho\geq 0
, are called smoothing.

Note that
X(U)=M(U)U
is real-to-real in the sense of (
2.14
) if and only if the operator
M(U)
is real-to-real in the sense of (
2.12
).

\bullet

Fourier representation of
(p+1)
-homogeneous vector fields:
A
(p+1)
-homogeneous vector field can be expressed in Fourier as: for any
\sigma=\pm
,

X(U)^{\sigma}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}X(U)^{\sigma}_{k}\,\frac{e^{{\rm i}\sigma kx}}{\sqrt{2\pi}},\qquad X(U)^{\sigma}_{k}=\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k,\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},-\sigma)\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p+2}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!X_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},\sigma}u^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1}}_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1}}\,,

(2.139)

the last sum being in
(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},\vec{\sigma}_{p+1})
,
with coefficients
X_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},\sigma}\in\mathbb{C}
given by

X_{\ j_{1},\ldots,j_{p},j_{p+1},k}^{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{p},\sigma_{p+1},\sigma}=\frac{1}{p+1}\left(M_{\ j_{1},\ldots,j_{p},j_{p+1},k}^{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{p},\sigma_{p+1},\sigma}+M_{\ j_{p+1},\ldots,j_{p},j_{1},k}^{\sigma_{p+1},\ldots,\sigma_{p},\sigma_{1},\sigma}+\cdots+M_{\ j_{1},\ldots,j_{p+1},j_{p},k}^{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{p+1},\sigma_{p},\sigma}\right)\,,

(2.140)

namely they are obtained symmetrizing with respect to the last index the coefficients
M_{\ \vec{\jmath}_{p},j,k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p},\sigma^{\prime},\sigma}
of
M(U)
.

In particular they satisfy
the symmetry condition: for any permutation
\pi
of
\{1,\ldots,p+1\}
,

X_{\ j_{\pi(1)},\ldots,j_{\pi(p+1)},k}^{\sigma_{\pi(1)},\ldots,\sigma_{\pi(p+1)},\sigma}=X_{\ j_{1},\ldots,j_{p+1},k}^{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{p+1},\sigma}\,.

(2.141)

In addition, if
X(U)
is real-to-real, see (
2.14
), then one has

\overline{X(U)^{+}_{k}}=X(U)^{-}_{k}\quad{\mathrm{i}.e.}\quad\overline{X_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},+}}=X_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{-\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},-}\,.

(2.142)

By Lemma
2.9
we obtain the following characterization of vector fields.

Lemma 2.29
.

(Characterization of vector fields in Fourier basis)

Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
. A real-to-real vector field

X(U)
belongs to
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{p+1}^{m}

if and only if its coefficients

X_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},\sigma}
(defined as in
(
2.139
))
fulfill the symmetric and real-to-real conditions (
2.141
), (
2.142
) and:
there exist
\mu\geq 0
and
C>0
such that

|X_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},\sigma}|\leq C{\rm max}_{2}\{|j_{1}|,\dots,|j_{p+1}|\}^{\mu}\ \max\{|j_{1}|,\dots,|j_{p+1}|\}^{m}

(2.143)

for any

(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k,\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},-\sigma)\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p+2}
(cfr. (
2.10
)).

The following lemma characterizes
0
,
1
and
2
forms.

Lemma 2.30
.

(Operatorial characterization of Hamiltonians and
1
-
2
forms)

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then

(i)

A
0
-form
H(U)
belongs to

\widetilde{\Uplambda}_{p+2}^{0}
if and only if there exists a matrix of
p
-homogeneous
real-to-real operators
M(U)
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
,
such that,

H(U)=\langle M(U)U,U\rangle_{r},\quad\forall U\in\dot{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}^{2})\,.

(2.144)

(ii)

A
1
-form
\theta(U)
belongs to

\widetilde{\Uplambda}_{p+1}^{1}
if and only if there exists a matrix of pluri–homogeneous
real-to-real operators
M(U)
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
,
such that,

\theta(U)[V]:=\langle M(U)U,V\rangle_{r}\,,\quad\forall V\in\dot{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}^{2})\,.

(2.145)

(iii)

A
2
-form

\Lambda(U)
belongs to
\widetilde{\Uplambda}_{p}^{2}

if and only if there exists a matrix of pluri–homogeneous real-to-real operators

M(U)
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
,
satisfying
M(U)^{\top}=-M(U)
, such that

\Lambda(U)[V_{1},V_{2}]:=\langle M(U)V_{1},V_{2}\rangle_{r}\,,\quad\forall(V_{1},V_{2})\in(\dot{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}^{2}))^{2}\,.

(2.146)

Proof.

Proof of
(i)
:
Identity (
2.144
) follows with an operator
M(U)
which has Fourier entries

M_{\vec{\jmath}_{p},j,k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p},\sigma^{\prime},\sigma}=\frac{1}{p+2}H_{\vec{\jmath}_{p},j,-k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p},\sigma^{\prime},\sigma}

(2.147)

where the Fourier coefficients of
H
are defined in (
2.128
).
By (
2.131
) and Lemma
2.9
the operator
M(U)
defined by (
2.147
) is a matrix of
m
-operators in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{m}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Note that, in view of (
2.147
), the entries of the operator
M(U)
satisfy the corresponding momentum condition
\vec{\sigma}_{p}\cdot\vec{\jmath}_{p}+\sigma^{\prime}j=\sigma k
thanks to the restriction in (
2.128
). The reality condition (
2.129
) is equivalent to (
2.46
).

Proof of
(ii)
:
Identity (
2.145
) follows with an operator
M(U)
which has Fourier entries

M_{\vec{\jmath}_{p},j,k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p},\sigma^{\prime},\sigma}=\Theta_{\vec{\jmath}_{p},j,-k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p},\sigma^{\prime},\sigma}

(2.148)

where
the Fourier coefficients of
\Theta
are defined in (
2.132
).
By (
2.134
) and Lemma
2.9
the operator
M(U)
defined by (
2.148
) is a matrix of
m
-operators in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{m}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Note that, in view of (
2.149
), the entries of the operator
M(U)
satisfy the corresponding momentum condition
\vec{\sigma}_{p}\cdot\vec{\jmath}_{p}+\sigma^{\prime}j=\sigma k
thanks to the restriction in (
2.129
). The reality condition (
2.133
) comes from (
2.46
).

Proof of
(iii)
:
Identity (
2.146
) follows with an operator
M(U)
which has Fourier entries (cfr. (
2.43
))

M_{\vec{\jmath}_{p},j,k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p},\sigma^{\prime},\sigma}=\Lambda_{\vec{\jmath}_{p},j,-k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p},\sigma^{\prime},\sigma}

(2.149)

where the Fourier coefficients of
\Lambda
are defined in (
2.135
).
By (
2.138
) and Lemma
2.9
the operator
M(U)
defined by (
2.149
) is a matrix of
m
-operators in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{m}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Note that, in view of (
2.149
), the entries of the operator
M(U)
satisfy the corresponding momentum condition
\vec{\sigma}_{p}\cdot\vec{\jmath}_{p}+\sigma^{\prime}j=\sigma k
thanks to the restriction in (
2.135
).
The antisymmetry of
\Lambda(U)
amounts to
M(U)^{\top}=-M(U)
and the reality condition (
2.137
) comes from (
2.46
).
∎

We now extend to pluri-homogeneous
k
-forms the typical “operations” of differential geometry.

Definition 2.31
.

(Exterior derivative)

We define the exterior derivative
of a
r
-homogeneous
k
-form
\Lambda(U)
in
\widetilde{\Uplambda}_{r}^{k}
as

{\rm d}\Lambda(U)[V_{1},\dots,V_{k+1}]:=\sum_{j=1}^{k+1}(-1)^{j-1}{\rm d}_{U}\big(\Lambda(U)[V_{1},\dots,\widehat{V}_{j},\dots,V_{k+1}]\big)[V_{j}]

(2.150)

where the notation
[V_{1},\dots,\widehat{V}_{j},\dots,V_{k+1}]
denotes the
k
-tuple obtained excluding the
j
-th component.

\bullet

If
H(U)
is a
p+2
-homogeneous
0
-form in
\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{0}_{p+2}
then
its exterior differential coincides
with the usual differential of functions, namely
{\rm d}H(U)[V]={\rm d}_{U}H(U)[V]
. Moreover

{\rm d}H(U)
is a
1
-form in
\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{1}_{p+1}

and we define the gradient
\nabla H(U):=\nabla_{U}H(U)
as the vector field in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{p+1}

such that, cfr. (
2.145
),

{\rm d}H(U)[V]:=\langle\nabla H(U),V\rangle_{r}\,,\quad\forall V\in\dot{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}^{2})\,.

(2.151)

\bullet

If
\theta(U)
is a
(p+1)
-homogeneous
1
-form in
\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{1}_{p+1}

written as in (
2.145
)
then its exterior differential is

{\rm d}\theta(U)[V_{1},V_{2}]=\Big\langle\big({\rm d}_{U}X(U)-{\rm d}_{U}X(U)^{\top}\big)V_{1},V_{2}\Big\rangle_{r},\quad X(U):=M(U)U

(2.152)

where
{\rm d}_{U}X(U)
and
{\rm d}_{U}X(U)^{\top}
are, by the first remark below Definition
2.5
and Lemma
2.10
,
matrices of operators in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}_{p}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Moreover

{\rm d}\theta(U)

belongs to
\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{2}_{p}
.

Definition 2.32
.

Let
p,p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and set
r:=p+2-k
.
Given a
r
-homogeneous
k
-form
\Lambda(U)
in
\widetilde{\Uplambda}_{r}^{k}

and a matrix of homogeneous operators
M(U)
in

{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}_{p^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
we define the

\bullet

Pull back
of
\Lambda(U)
via the map
\varphi(U):=M(U)U
as

(\varphi^{*}\Lambda)(U)[V_{1},\dots,V_{k}]:=\Lambda(\varphi(U))[{\rm d}_{U}\varphi(U)V_{1},\dots,{\rm d}_{U}\varphi(U)V_{k}]\,.

(2.153)

\bullet

Lie derivative
of
\Lambda(U)
along the vector field
X(U):=M(U)U
as

(\mathcal{L}_{X}\Lambda)\,(U)[V_{1},\dots,V_{k}]:={\rm d}_{U}\Lambda(U)[X(U)][V_{1},\dots,V_{k}]+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\Lambda(U)[V_{1},\dots,{\rm d}_{U}X(U)[V_{j}],\dots,V_{k}].

(2.154)

\bullet

Contraction
of
\Lambda(U)
with the vector field
X(U)=M(U)U
as

(i_{X}\Lambda)(U)[V_{1},\dots,V_{k-1}]:=\Lambda(U)[X(U),V_{1},\dots,V_{k-1}]\,.

(2.155)

Let
\Lambda(U)
is a
r
-homogeneous
k
-form in
\widetilde{\Uplambda}_{r}^{k}
,

k=0,1,2
. Thanks to the first bullet below Definition
2.5
,
Lemma
2.10
and
(i)
and

(ii)
of Proposition
2.15
(see also
(
2.160
)-(
2.161
)), one has the following:

\bullet

if
\varphi(U)=M(U)U
is a map where
M(U)
is a pluri-homogeneous operator in
\Sigma_{0}^{N}{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

then
(\varphi^{*}\Lambda)(U)
defined in (
2.153
) belongs to
\Sigma_{r}\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{k}_{q}
;

\bullet

if
X(U)
is a homogeneous vector field
in
\Sigma_{p^{\prime}+1}\mathfrak{\widetilde{X}}_{q}
for some
p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, then

(\mathcal{L}_{X}\Lambda)\,(U)
defined in (
2.154
) belongs to

\Sigma_{r+p^{\prime}}\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{k}_{q}
;

\bullet

if
k=1,2
then
(i_{X}\Lambda)(U)
defined in (
2.155
) belongs to
\Sigma_{r+p^{\prime}+1}\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{k-1}_{q}
;

\bullet

the basic identities of differential geometry are directly verified
for pluri-homogeneous
k
-forms:
Let
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
k=0,1,2
.
Then for any
\Lambda
in
\widetilde{\Uplambda}_{p+2}^{k}
it results

{\rm d}^{2}\Lambda=0\,.

(2.156)

Given
\varphi(U):=M(U)U
with

M(U)
in
\Sigma_{0}{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, it results

{\rm d}(\varphi^{*}\Lambda)\,=(\varphi^{*}{\rm d}\Lambda)\,\,.

(2.157)

Given also
\phi(U):=M^{\prime}(U)U
with
M^{\prime}(U)\in\Sigma_{0}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{C})
, it results

\phi^{*}\varphi^{*}\Lambda=(\varphi\circ\phi)^{*}\Lambda\ .

(2.158)

Given
X(U)
in
\Sigma_{1}\mathfrak{\widetilde{X}}_{q}
then

\mathcal{L}_{X}\Lambda={\rm d}\circ i_{X}\Lambda+i_{X}\circ{\rm d}\Lambda\,;

(2.159)

\bullet

if
\varphi(U)=M^{\prime}(U)U
is a map where
M^{\prime}(U)
is a pluri-homogeneous operator in
\Sigma_{0}{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
\theta(U)\in\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{1}_{p+1}
and

\Lambda(U)\in\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{2}_{p}
are represented as in
(
2.145
), (
2.146
) then

\displaystyle(\varphi^{*}\theta)(U)[V]=\langle{\rm d}_{U}\varphi(U)^{\top}\,\,M(\varphi(U))\,\varphi(U),V\rangle\,;

(2.160)

\displaystyle(\varphi^{*}\Lambda)(U)[V_{1},V_{2}]=\langle{\rm d}_{U}\varphi(U)^{\top}\,\,M(\varphi(U))\,\,{\rm d}_{U}\varphi(U)V_{1},V_{2}\rangle\,.

(2.161)

In Section
4
we shall use the following
result about Lie derivatives and approximate flows.

Lemma 2.33
.

Let
p,N\in\mathbb{N}
with
p\leq N
.
Let
\theta^{\tau}
be a
\tau
-dependent family of
1
-forms in
\Sigma_{1}\widetilde{\Uplambda}_{q}^{1}
defined for
\tau\in[0,1]
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau}
be the approximate flow generated
by a pluri–homogeneous,
\tau
–dependent
smoothing vector field
Y^{\tau}(U)
in
\Sigma_{p+1}\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{q}^{-\varrho}
, defined for
\tau\in[0,1]
and some
\varrho\geq 0

(cfr. Lemma
2.26
). Then

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau})^{*}\theta^{\tau}=(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau})^{*}\big[\mathcal{L}_{Y^{\tau}}\theta^{\tau}+\partial_{\tau}\theta^{\tau}\big]+\theta_{>N+1}^{\tau}

(2.162)

where
\theta_{>N+1}^{\tau}

is a pluri–homogeneous
1
–form in
\Sigma_{N+2}\widetilde{\Uplambda}_{q}^{1}
, with estimates uniform in
\tau\in[0,1]
.

Proof.

Recalling the definition of pullback (
2.153
) and using that
\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau}
fulfills the approximate equation (
2.123
) (with
Y^{\tau}
replacing
X^{\tau}
) we get

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau})^{*}\theta^{\tau}\,(U)[\widehat{U}]=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}\big(\theta^{\tau}(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau}(U))[{\rm d}_{U}\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau}(U)\widehat{U}]\big)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\tau}\theta^{\tau}(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau}(U))[{\rm d}_{U}\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau}(U)\widehat{U}]+{\rm d}_{V}\theta^{\tau}(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau}(U))[Y^{\tau}(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau}(U))+R_{>N}^{\tau}(U)U][{\rm d}_{U}\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau}(U)\widehat{U}]

\displaystyle+\theta^{\tau}(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau}(U))[{\rm d}_{V}Y^{\tau}(\mathcal{F}^{\tau}_{\leq N}(U)){\rm d}_{U}\mathcal{F}^{\tau}_{\leq N}(U)\widehat{U}+{\rm d}_{U}(R^{\tau}_{>N}(U)U)\widehat{U}]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Lie},\eqref{pullback}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau})^{*}\big[\mathcal{L}_{Y^{\tau}}\theta^{\tau}+\partial_{\tau}\theta^{\tau}\big](U)[\widehat{U}]+\theta_{>N+1}^{\tau}(U)[\widehat{U}]

where

\theta_{>N+1}^{\tau}(U)[\widehat{U}]:={\rm d}_{V}\theta^{\tau}(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau}(U))[R_{>N}^{\tau}(U)U][{\rm d}_{U}\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau}(U)\widehat{U}]+\theta^{\tau}(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau}(U))[{\rm d}_{U}(R^{\tau}_{>N}(U)U)\widehat{U}]\,.

(2.163)

We now verify that

\theta_{>N+1}^{\tau}
is a
1
-form in
\Sigma_{N+2}\widetilde{\Uplambda}_{q}^{1}
.
Representing, by Lemma
2.30
,

\theta^{\tau}(U)[\widehat{U}]=\langle M^{\tau}(U)U,\widehat{U}\rangle_{r}\quad\text{with}\quad M^{\tau}(U)\in\Sigma_{0}{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\,,

and since

\mathcal{F}^{\tau}_{\leq N}(U)=({\rm Id}+R^{\tau}_{\leq N}(U))U
with
R^{\tau}_{\leq N}(U)
in

\Sigma_{p}^{N}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
,
the
1
-form
\theta_{>N+1}^{\tau}
in (
2.163
) reads

\displaystyle\theta^{\tau}_{>N+1}(U)[\widehat{U}]=\langle M^{\prime}(U)U,\widehat{U}\rangle_{r}\quad\text{where}\quad M^{\prime}(U):=

\displaystyle\left[{\rm d}_{U}\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau}(U)\right]^{\top}\,\left.{\rm d}_{V}(M^{\tau}(V)V)\right|_{V=\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau}(U)}\,R_{>N}^{\tau}(U)

\displaystyle\!\!\!+[{\rm d}_{U}(R^{\tau}_{>N}(U)U)]^{\top}M^{\tau}(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau}(U))({\rm Id}+R^{\tau}_{\leq N}(U))

is a
\tau
-dependent
matrix of operators in
\Sigma_{N+1}{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, with estimates uniform
in
\tau\in[0,1]
,
because
R^{\tau}_{>N}(U)
are smoothing operators in
\Sigma_{N+1}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, and using
(i)
,
(ii)
of Proposition
2.15
and Lemma
2.10
. Thus
\theta_{>N+1}^{\tau}
is a
1
-form in
\Sigma_{N+2}\widetilde{\Uplambda}_{q}^{1}
,
with estimates uniform
in
\tau\in[0,1]
.
∎

3
Hamiltonian formalism

Along the paper we consider real Hamiltonian systems and their symplectic structures
in real, complex and Fourier coordinates, that we describe in Section
3.1
.
In Section
3.2

we introduce the notion of vector fields which are Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
and we prove
that the classical
Hamiltonian theory is preserved “up to homogeneity
N
”.
In Section
3.3
we present results about linear symplectic flows. In Section
3.4

discuss Hamiltonian systems with a paradifferential structure.

3.1
Hamiltonian and symplectic structures

Real Hamiltonian systems.

We equip the real phase
space
\dot{L}^{2}_{r}\times\dot{L}^{2}_{r}

with the scalar product in (
2.4
) and the symplectic form

\Omega_{0}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\eta_{1}\\
\zeta_{1}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\eta_{2}\\
\zeta_{2}\end{pmatrix}\right):=\Big\langle E_{0}\begin{pmatrix}\eta_{1}\\
\zeta_{1}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\eta_{2}\\
\zeta_{2}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle_{r}=-\langle\zeta_{1},\eta_{2}\rangle_{\dot{L}_{r}}+\langle\eta_{1},\zeta_{2}\rangle_{\dot{L}_{r}}

(3.1)

where
E_{0}
is the symplectic operator acting on
\dot{L}^{2}_{r}\times\dot{L}^{2}_{r}
defined by

E_{0}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&-{\rm Id}\\
{\rm Id}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(3.2)

The Hamiltonian vector field
X_{H}
associated to a (densely defined)
Hamiltonian function

H:\dot{L}^{2}_{r}\times\dot{L}^{2}_{r}\to\mathbb{R}
is characterized as the unique
vector field satisfying

\Omega_{0}\left(X_{H}(\eta,\zeta),\begin{pmatrix}\breve{\eta}\\
\breve{\zeta}\end{pmatrix}\right)={\rm d}H(\eta,\zeta)\left[\begin{pmatrix}\breve{\eta}\\
\breve{\zeta}\end{pmatrix}\right],\quad\forall\begin{pmatrix}\breve{\eta}\\
\breve{\zeta}\end{pmatrix}\in\dot{L}^{2}_{r}\times\dot{L}^{2}_{r}\,.

(3.3)

As

\displaystyle{\rm d}H(\eta,\zeta)\left[\begin{pmatrix}\breve{\eta}\\
\breve{\zeta}\end{pmatrix}\right]

\displaystyle={\rm d}_{\eta}H(\eta,\zeta)[\breve{\eta}]+{\rm d}_{\zeta}H(\eta,\zeta)[\breve{\zeta}]=\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{\eta}H\\
\nabla_{\zeta}H\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\breve{\eta}\\
\breve{\zeta}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle_{r}

(3.4)

where
(\nabla_{\eta}H,\nabla_{\zeta}H)\in\dot{L}^{2}_{r}\times\dot{L}^{2}_{r}

denote the
\dot{L}^{2}_{r}
- gradients,
the Hamiltonian vector field is given by

X_{H}=J\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{\eta}H\\
\nabla_{\zeta}H\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{\zeta}H\\
-\nabla_{\eta}H\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{where}\quad J:=E_{0}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&{\rm Id}\\
-{\rm Id}&0\end{pmatrix}.

(3.5)

We also denote by

\theta_{0}(\eta,\zeta)\Big[\begin{pmatrix}\breve{\eta}\\
\breve{\zeta}\end{pmatrix}\Big]:=\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle E_{0}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\breve{\eta}\\
\breve{\zeta}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle_{r}

(3.6)

the Liouville
1
–form. Note that
{\rm d}\theta_{0}=\Omega_{0}
, where the
exterior differential is recalled in Section
2.4
.

Real linear Hamiltonian systems.

We now consider the most general quadratic real Hamiltonian

H(\eta,\zeta)=\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle{\bf A}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle_{r},\quad{\bf A}:=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
B^{\top}&D\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad{\bf A}^{\top}={\bf A}\,,

(3.7)

where
A,B,D
are linear real operators acting on
\dot{L}^{2}_{r}

and the operators
A,D
are symmetric, i.e.
A^{\top}=A
,
D^{\top}=D
, where
A^{\top}
denotes the transpose operator with respect to the
real scalar product
\langle\ ,\ \rangle_{\dot{L}^{2}_{r}}
.

{\bf A}^{\top}
is the transpose with respect to the scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{r}
in (
2.4
).

Definition 3.1
.

(Linear Hamiltonian operator)

A linear operator
\mathcal{A}
acting on (a dense subspace) of
\dot{L}^{2}_{r}\times\dot{L}^{2}_{r}

is Hamiltonian if it
has the form

\mathcal{A}=J{\bf A}=J\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
B^{\top}&D\end{pmatrix},\qquad{\bf A}={\bf A}^{\top}\,,

(3.8)

with
A,B,D
real operators satisfying
A=A^{\top}
and
D=D^{\top}
; equivalently if

E_{0}\mathcal{A}={\bf A}
is symmetric with respect to the
real scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{r}
defined in (
2.4
).

We now provide the characterization of a real linear Hamiltonian
paradifferential operator.
In view of (
3.8
) and (
2.26
) a
matrix of paradifferential operators is Hamiltonian if it has the form

J{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}a(x,\xi)&b(x,\xi)\\
b(x,-\xi)&d(x,\xi)\end{bmatrix}\right)}={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}b(x,-\xi)&d(x,\xi)\\
-a(x,\xi)&-b(x,\xi)\end{bmatrix}\right)}

(3.9)

with

\displaystyle a(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R},\ a(x,\xi)=a(x,-\xi)\,,\quad d(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R},\ d(x,\xi)=d(x,-\xi)\,,\quad b(x,-\xi)=\overline{b(x,\xi)}\,.

(3.10)

Real Hamiltonian systems in complex coordinates.

We now describe the above real Hamiltonian systems
in the complex coordinates defined by the change of variables

\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix}=\mathcal{C}\begin{pmatrix}u\\
\overline{u}\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad\mathcal{C}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}{\rm Id}&{\rm Id}\\
-{\rm i}&{\rm i}\end{matrix}\right)\ ,\quad\mathcal{C}^{-1}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}{\rm Id}&{\rm i}\\
{\rm Id}&-{\rm i}\end{matrix}\right)\,,\quad\mathcal{C}^{\top}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}{\rm Id}&-{\rm i}\\
{\rm Id}&{\rm i}\end{matrix}\right)\,.

(3.11)

Note that
\mathcal{C}
is a map between the real subspace of vector functions

\dot{L}^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
into

\dot{L}^{2}_{r}\times\dot{L}^{2}_{r}
.
In the sequel to save space we denote
\begin{pmatrix}u\\
\overline{u}\end{pmatrix}
also as
(u,\overline{u})
.

The pull-back
\Omega_{c}:=\mathcal{C}^{*}\Omega_{0}

of the symplectic form
\Omega_{0}
in (
3.1
) is

\Omega_{c}\left(\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
\overline{u}_{1}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}u_{2}\\
\overline{u}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\right)=\Big\langle\mathcal{C}^{\top}E_{0}\mathcal{C}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
\overline{u}_{1}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}u_{2}\\
\overline{u}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle_{r}=\Big\langle E_{c}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
\overline{u}_{1}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}u_{2}\\
\overline{u}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle_{r}\

(3.12)

where
E_{c}
is the symplectic operator acting on
\dot{L}^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})

E_{c}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&-{\rm i}\\
{\rm i}&0\end{pmatrix}={\rm i}E_{0}\,.

(3.13)

Remark that

E_{c}^{\top}=-E_{c}
and
E_{c}^{2}={\rm Id}
. Similarly the Liouville
1
-form
\theta_{0}
in (
3.6
) is transformed into the symplectic form
\theta_{c}:=\mathcal{C}^{*}\theta_{0}
given by

\theta_{c}(U)\left[V\right]=\frac{1}{2}\langle E_{c}U,V\rangle_{r}\,,\quad\forall\ U=\begin{pmatrix}u\\
\overline{u}\end{pmatrix}\,,\ V=\begin{pmatrix}v\\
\overline{v}\end{pmatrix}\,

(3.14)

and it results

{\rm d}\theta_{c}=\Omega_{c}\,.

(3.15)

Next we show how the differential and gradient of a Hamiltonian transform under the complex change of coordinates.
The pull-back under
\mathcal{C}
of the

1
-form (cfr. (
3.4
))

{\rm d}H(\eta,\zeta)[\cdot]=\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{\eta}H\\
\nabla_{\zeta}H\end{pmatrix},\cdot\Big\rangle_{r}

is

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C}^{*}{\rm d}H)(u,\overline{u})[(v,\overline{v})]=\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{u}H\\
\nabla_{\overline{u}}H\end{pmatrix}\Big|_{\mathcal{C}(u,\overline{u})},\ \begin{pmatrix}v\\
\overline{v}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle_{r}

(3.16)

where

\nabla_{u}H:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\nabla_{\eta}H-{\rm i}\nabla_{\zeta}H\right)|_{\mathcal{C}(u,\overline{u})}\,,\quad\nabla_{\overline{u}}H:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\nabla_{\eta}H+{\rm i}\nabla_{\zeta}H\right)|_{\mathcal{C}(u,\overline{u})}\,.

(3.17)

Furthermore, by (
3.11
),

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C}^{*}{\rm d}H)(u,\overline{u})[(v,\overline{v})]={\rm d}_{u}H|_{\mathcal{C}(u,\overline{u})}[v]+{\rm d}_{\overline{u}}H|_{\mathcal{C}(u,\overline{u})}[\overline{v}]

having defined

{\rm d}_{u}H:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left({\rm d}_{\eta}H-{\rm i}{\rm d}_{\zeta}H\right)|_{\mathcal{C}(u,\overline{u})}\,,\quad{\rm d}_{\overline{u}}H:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left({\rm d}_{\eta}H+{\rm i}{\rm d}_{\zeta}H\right)|_{\mathcal{C}(u,\overline{u})}\,.

In the sequel we also use the compact notation,
given
U=(u,\overline{u})
,

{\rm d}_{U}H(U)[\widehat{U}]:={\rm d}_{u}H(U)[\widehat{u}]+{\rm d}_{\overline{u}}H(U)[\overline{\widehat{u}}]\,,\quad\forall\widehat{U}=\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{u}\\
\overline{\widehat{u}}\end{pmatrix}\,.

Real Hamiltonian vector fields in complex coordinates.

Given a real valued Hamiltonian
H(\eta,\zeta)
, consider the Hamiltonian in complex coordinates
H_{c}:=H\circ{\mathcal{C}}
which is a function of
(u,\overline{u})
.
Recalling the characterization (
3.3
) of the Hamiltonian vector field and (
3.16
),
the associated Hamiltonian vector field is

X_{H_{c}}(U):=\mathcal{C}^{-1}(X_{H})_{|\mathcal{C}(U)}=J_{c}\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{u}H_{c}\\
\nabla_{\overline{u}}H_{c}\end{pmatrix}=J_{c}\nabla H_{c}(U)=\begin{pmatrix}-{\rm i}\nabla_{\overline{u}}H_{c}\\
{\rm i}\nabla_{u}H_{c}\end{pmatrix}

(3.18)

where
J_{c}:=E_{c}^{-1}=E_{c}
is the Poisson tensor in (
1.22
).
One has also the characterization

\Omega_{c}(X_{H_{c}},\cdot)={\rm d}_{U}H_{c}(U)[\cdot]\,.

(3.19)

In case
H
is the quadratic form (
3.7
),
the transformed Hamiltonian
H_{c}=H\circ\mathcal{C}
is given by

\displaystyle H_{c}(u,\overline{u})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle{\bf R}\begin{pmatrix}u\\
\overline{u}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}u\\
\overline{u}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle_{r},\quad{\bf R}:=\mathcal{C}^{\top}{\bf A}\mathcal{C}:=\begin{pmatrix}R_{1}&R_{2}\\
\overline{R}_{2}&\overline{R}_{1}\end{pmatrix}

(3.20)

where
R_{1}:=(A-D)-{\rm i}(B+B^{\top})
,
R_{2}:=(A+D)+{\rm i}(B-B^{\top})
.
The operator
{\bf R}
is real-to-real according to (
2.12
).
In addition, since
{\bf A}
is symmetric, cfr. (
3.7
),
the operator
{\bf R}
is symmetric with respect to
the real non-degenerate bilinear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{r}
, namely

{\bf R}={\bf R}^{\top}\,,\quad\text{i.e.}\ \ R_{1}=R_{1}^{\top}\,,\ \ R_{2}^{*}=R_{2}\,.

(3.21)

Definition 3.2
.

(Linear Hamiltonian operator in complex coordinates)

A real-to-real linear operator
J_{c}{\bf M}
is
linearly Hamiltonian if

{\bf M}={\bf M}^{\top}
is symmetric with respect to
the non-degenerate bilinear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{r}
, cfr. (
3.21
).

In view of (
2.26
) and (
3.21
)
a matrix of paradifferential real-to-real complex operators is linearly
Hamiltonian if

J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{matrix}b_{1}(U;t,x,\xi)&b_{2}(U;t,x,\xi)\\
\overline{b_{2}(U;t,x,-\xi)}&\overline{b_{1}(U;t,x,-\xi)}\end{matrix}\right)}\,,\quad\begin{cases}b_{1}(U;t,x,-\xi)=b_{1}(U;t,x,\xi)\,,\\
b_{2}(U;t,x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}\,,\end{cases}

(3.22)

namely
b_{1}
is even in
\xi
and
b_{2}
is real valued.

Definition 3.3
.

(Linearly symplectic map)

A real-to-real linear transformation

\mathcal{A}
is linearly symplectic if
\mathcal{A}^{*}\Omega_{c}=\Omega_{c}

where
\Omega_{c}
is defined in (
3.12
), namely

\mathcal{A}^{\top}\,E_{c}\,\mathcal{A}=E_{c}
,
where
E_{c}
is the symplectic operator defined in (
3.13
).

Hamiltonian systems in Fourier basis.

Given a Hamiltonian
H(U)
expanded as in (
2.128
) we characterize its Hamiltonian vector field.
We decompose each Fourier coefficients as

u_{j}=\frac{x_{j}+{\rm i}y_{j}}{\sqrt{2}}
,
\overline{u_{j}}=\frac{x_{j}-{\rm i}y_{j}}{\sqrt{2}}
,
where
x_{j}:=\sqrt{2}\mathrm{Re}\,(u_{j})
and
y_{j}:=\sqrt{2}\mathrm{Im}\,(u_{j})
and we define

\partial_{u_{j}}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big(\partial_{x_{j}}-{\rm i}\partial_{y_{j}}\big)\,,\quad\partial_{\overline{u_{j}}}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big(\partial_{x_{j}}+{\rm i}\partial_{y_{j}}\big)\,,

(3.23)

so that

\partial_{u_{j}^{\sigma}}u_{j}^{\sigma}=1
, for any
\sigma=\pm
, and

\partial_{u_{j}^{\sigma}}u_{j}^{\sigma^{\prime}}=0
, for any
\sigma\sigma^{\prime}=-1
.
For a real valued Hamiltonian
H
it results

\overline{\partial_{u_{j}}H}=\partial_{\overline{u_{j}}}H\,.

(3.24)

We now write a Hamiltonian vector field (
3.18
) in the coordinates

(u_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}
.
For notational simplicity we also denote
u\equiv(u_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}
.
We first note that, by (
3.12
) and (
2.5
),
the symplectic form (
3.12
) reads, for any

U=(u,\overline{u})
,
V=(v,\overline{v})
,

\Omega_{c}\big(U,V\big)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}-{\rm i}\overline{u_{j}}v_{j}+{\rm i}u_{j}\overline{v_{j}}=-{\rm i}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\atop\sigma\in\{\pm\}}\sigma u_{j}^{-\sigma}v_{j}^{\sigma}\,.

(3.25)

Lemma 3.4
.

(Fourier expansion of a Hamiltonian vector field)

The Fourier
components of the Hamiltonian vector field associated to a real Hamiltonian
H(U)

are, for any
\sigma=\pm
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
,

\big(J_{c}\nabla H(U)\big)^{\sigma}_{k}=-{\rm i}\sigma\partial_{u_{k}^{-\sigma}}H(U)\,.

(3.26)

In particular, if the Hamiltonian
H
is expanded as in (
2.128
), then

\big(J_{c}\nabla H(U)\big)^{\sigma}_{k}=-{\rm i}\sigma\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k,\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},-\sigma)\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p+2}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!H_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},-\sigma}u_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1}}\,.

(3.27)

Proof.

The expression (
3.26
) is a consequence of (
3.25
)
and the definition (
3.19
) of a Hamiltonian vector field, using
(
3.24
).
Finally (
3.27
) is a consequence of (
3.26
), (
2.129
), (
2.130
).
∎

By (
3.27
)
and (
2.139
) we deduce the following characterization of Hamiltonian vector fields:

Lemma 3.5
.

(Characterization of
(p+1)
–homogeneous Hamiltonian vector fields)

A
(p+1)
–homogeneous
real-to-real vector field
X(U)\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{p+1}
of the form (
2.139
)
is Hamiltonian if and only if the coefficients

H_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},\sigma}:=-{\rm i}\sigma X_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},-\sigma},\qquad\forall(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k,\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},\sigma)\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p+2}\,,

(3.28)

satisfy (
2.130
), (
2.129
) and (
2.131
).
In such a case
X(U)
is the Hamiltonian vector field generated by

H(U)=\frac{1}{p+2}\sum_{(\vec{\jmath}_{p+2},\vec{\sigma}_{p+2})\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p+2}}H_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+2}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+2}}u_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+2}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+2}}\,,\quad H_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+2}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+2}}:=-{\rm i}\sigma_{p+2}X_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},j_{p+2}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},-\sigma_{p+2}}\,.

The Poisson bracket between two real functions
F,G
is, by
(
3.19
), (
3.25
), (
3.26
), (
3.24
),

\{F,G\}:={\rm d}F(X_{G})=\Omega_{c}(X_{F},X_{G})=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}{\rm i}\Big(\partial_{\overline{u_{j}}}F\partial_{u_{j}}G-\partial_{u_{j}}F\partial_{\overline{u_{j}}}G\Big)\,.

(3.29)

Note that the right hand side of (
3.29
) is well–defined also for
complex valued functions
F
and
G
and,
with a small abuse of notation, we shall still refer to it as the Poisson bracket
between
F
and
G
.

3.2
Hamiltonian systems up to homogeneity
N

Along the paper we encounter vector fields which are Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
. We distinguish between linear and nonlinear ones.

Linear Hamiltonian operators.

In the sequel let
p,N,K,K^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
K^{\prime}\leq K
,
r>0
.

Definition 3.6
.

(Linearly Hamiltonian operator up to homogeneity
N
)

A real-to-real matrix of spectrally localized maps

J_{c}{\bf B}(U;t)
in
\Sigma{\mathcal{S}}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

is linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
if
the pluri-homogeneous component

\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}({\bf B}(U;t))
(defined in (
2.36
)) is symmetric, namely

\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}({\bf B}(U;t))=\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}({\bf B}(U;t)^{\top})\,.

(3.30)

In particular, a matrix of paradifferential real-to-real complex operators is
linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
if it has the form (cfr. (
3.22
))

\!\!\!J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{matrix}b_{1}(U;t,x,\xi)&b_{2}(U;t,x,\xi)\\
\overline{b_{2}(U;t,x,-\xi)}&\overline{b_{1}(U;t,x,-\xi)}\end{matrix}\right)}\,,\ \begin{cases}b_{1}(U;t,x,-\xi)-b_{1}(U;t,x,\xi)\in\Gamma^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[r]\\
\mathrm{Im}\,b_{2}(U;t,x,\xi)\in\Gamma^{m^{\prime}}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[r]\end{cases}

(3.31)

for some
m,m^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 3.7
.

(Linearly symplectic map up to homogeneity
N
)

A real-to-real matrix of spectrally localized maps
{\bf S}(U;t)
in
\Sigma\mathcal{S}_{K,K^{\prime},0}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

is linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
if

{\bf S}(U;t)^{\top}\,E_{c}\,{\bf S}(U;t)=E_{c}+S_{>N}(U;t)

(3.32)

where
E_{c}
is the symplectic operator defined in (
3.13
) and

S_{>N}(U;t)
is a matrix of spectrally localized maps
in
\mathcal{S}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

The approximate inverse up to homogeneity
N
of a linearly symplectic map up to homogeneity
N
is still
linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
p,N\in\mathbb{N}
with
p\leq N
.
Let
\Phi_{\leq N}(U):={\bf B}_{\leq N}(U)U
be such that

{\bf B}_{\leq N}(U)-{\rm Id}\in\Sigma_{p}^{N}\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

and

{\bf B}_{\leq N}(U)
is linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
(Definition
3.7
).
Then its approximate inverse
\Psi_{\leq N}(V)
, constructed in Lemma
2.24
, has the form

\Psi_{\leq N}(V)={\bf A}_{\leq N}(V)V

where

{\bf A}_{{\leq N}}(V)-{\rm Id}
is in
\Sigma_{p}^{N}{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and

{\bf A}_{{\leq N}}(V)
is linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
,
more precisely

{\bf A}_{{\leq N}}(V)^{\top}\,E_{c}\,{\bf A}_{{\leq N}}(V)=E_{c}+S_{>N}(V)

(3.33)

where
S_{>N}(V)
is a matrix of pluri–homogeneous spectrally localized maps in

\Sigma_{N+1}{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

As
{\bf B}_{\leq N}(U)
is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
, one has

{\bf B}_{\leq N}(U)^{\top}\,E_{c}\,{\bf B}_{\leq N}(U)=E_{c}+S_{>N}(U)

(3.34)

where
S_{>N}(U)
is a pluri-homogeneous operator in
\Sigma_{N+1}{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, being the left hand side above a pluri-homogeneous operator).
Then we evaluate (
3.34
) at
U=\Psi_{\leq N}(V)
, apply
{\bf A}_{\leq N}(V)
to the right and
{\bf A}_{\leq N}(V)^{\top}
to the left and use (
2.110
) and the composition properties in Proposition
2.19
.
The operator
S_{>N}(V)
is pluri-homogeneous as the left-hand side of (
3.33
).
∎

The class of linearly Hamiltonian operators up to homogeneity
N
is closed under conjugation
under a linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
map.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
J_{c}{\bf B}(U;t)
be a linearly Hamiltonian operator up to homogeneity
N

(Definition
3.6
)
and
\mathcal{G}(U;t)
be an invertible map, linearly symplectic to homogeneity
N
(Definition
3.7
).
Then the operators

\mathcal{G}(U;t)J_{c}{\bf B}(U;t)\mathcal{G}(U;t)^{-1}
and

(\partial_{t}\mathcal{G}(U;t))\mathcal{G}^{-1}(U;t)

are linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
.

Proof.

Set
{\bf B}:={\bf B}(U;t)
and
\mathcal{G}:=\mathcal{G}(U;t)
for brevity.
As
\mathcal{G}
is invertible, we deduce from (
3.32
) that
\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}\left(\mathcal{G}J_{c}\right)=\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}\left(J_{c}[\mathcal{G}^{-1}]^{\top}\right)
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}\left(\mathcal{G}J_{c}{\bf B}\mathcal{G}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}\left(\mathcal{G}J_{c}\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}[{\bf B}]\mathcal{G}^{-1}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{A:sym23}}}{{=}}\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}\left(J_{c}[\mathcal{G}^{-1}]^{\top}\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}[{\bf B}]\mathcal{G}^{-1}\right)=J_{c}{\bf M}

where

{\bf M}:=\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}\left([\mathcal{G}^{-1}]^{\top}\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}[{\bf B}]\mathcal{G}^{-1}\right)

is symmetric since
\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}[{\bf B}^{\top}]=\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}[{\bf B}]
.
This proves that
\mathcal{G}J_{c}{\bf B}\mathcal{G}^{-1}
is linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
.

Next, differentiating
(
3.32
) (with
\mathcal{G}(U;t)
replacing
{\bf S}(U;t)
), we get

\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}\left[E_{c}(\partial_{t}\mathcal{G})\mathcal{G}^{-1}\right]=-\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}\left[(\mathcal{G}^{-1})^{\top}(\partial_{t}\mathcal{G})^{\top}E_{c}\right]=\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}\left[\big(E_{c}(\partial_{t}\mathcal{G})\mathcal{G}^{-1}\big)^{\top}\right]

showing that
(\partial_{t}\mathcal{G})\mathcal{G}^{-1}
is linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
.
∎

Nonlinear Hamiltonian systems up to homogeneity
N
.

Let
K,K^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
K^{\prime}\leq K
,
r>0
and
U\in B_{s_{0}}^{K}(I;r)
.
Let

Z:={\bf M}_{0}(U;t)U\quad\text{ with }\quad{\bf M}_{0}(U;t)\in\mathcal{M}^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},0}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\,.

(3.35)

Definition 3.10
.

(Hamiltonian system up to homogeneity
N
)

Let
N,K,K^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
K\geq K^{\prime}+1
and
assume (
3.35
).
A
U
–dependent system

\partial_{t}Z=J_{c}\nabla H(Z)+M_{>N}(U;t)[U]

(3.36)

is Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
if

\bullet

H(Z)
is a pluri-homogeneous Hamiltonian
in
\Sigma_{2}^{N+2}\widetilde{\Uplambda}_{q}^{0}
;

\bullet

M_{>N}(U;t)
is a matrix of
non-homogeneous operators in
\mathcal{M}_{K,K^{\prime}+1,N+1}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

In view of the first bullet after Definition
2.31

the Hamiltonian vector field
J_{c}\nabla H(Z)
is in
\Sigma_{1}^{N+1}\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{q}
.

We shall perform nonlinear changes of variables which are symplectic up to homogeneity
N
according to the following definition.

Definition 3.11
.

(Symplectic map up to homogeneity
N
)

Let
p,N\in\mathbb{N}
with
p\leq N
. We say that

\mathcal{D}(Z;t)=M(Z;t)Z\qquad\text{with}\qquad M(Z;t)-{\rm Id}\in\Sigma\mathcal{M}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\,,

(3.37)

is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
, if its pluri-homogeneous component
\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z):=\big(\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}M(Z;t)\big)Z
satisfies

(\mathcal{D}_{\leq N})^{*}\Omega_{c}\,=\Omega_{c}+\Omega_{>N}

(3.38)

where
\Omega_{>N}
is a pluri-homogeneous 2-form in

\Sigma_{N+1}\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{2}_{q}
.

Equivalently,
by (
3.12
) and
the operatorial representation (
2.146
) of
2
-forms,
the nonlinear map

\mathcal{D}(Z;t)
is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
,
if

\left[{\rm d}_{Z}\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)\right]^{\top}\,E_{c}\,{\rm d}_{Z}\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)=E_{c}+E_{>N}(Z)\quad\text{with}\quad E_{>N}(Z)\in\Sigma_{N+1}{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\,.

(3.39)

Remark 3.12
.

In the real setting we say that a map
\mathcal{D}(\eta,\zeta)
is symplectic up to homogeneity if its pluri-homogeneous component
\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(\eta,\zeta)
satisfies

\big[{\rm d}_{(\eta,\zeta)}\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(\eta,\zeta)\big]^{\top}\,E_{0}\,\big[{\rm d}_{(\eta,\zeta)}\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(\eta,\zeta)\big]=E_{0}+E_{>N}(\eta,\zeta)

(3.40)

where
E_{0}
is the real symplectic tensor defined in (
3.2
) and
E_{>N}

is matrix of real operators in
\Sigma_{N+1}{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

We now show that the usual properties
of symplectic maps still hold, up to homogeneity
N
.
For example the approximate inverse of a symplectic up to homogeneity
N

map is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
as well.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
p,N\in\mathbb{N}
with
p\leq N
. Let
\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)=Z+M_{\leq N}(Z)Z
as in
(
2.108
) be symplectic up to homogeneity
N
.
Then its approximate inverse
\mathcal{E}_{\leq N}(V)=V+\breve{M}_{\leq N}(V)V

up to homogeneity
N
as in (
2.109
)
(provided by Lemma
2.24
)
is symplectic up to homogeneity
N

as well.
Moreover

\big[{\rm d}_{Z}\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)\big]J_{c}\big[{\rm d}_{Z}\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)\big]^{\top}=J_{c}+J_{>N}(Z),\quad J_{>N}(Z)\in\Sigma_{N+1}{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\ .

(3.41)

Proof.

As
\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)
is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
, we get that,
using also the first bullet after Definition
2.32
,

(\mathcal{E}_{\leq N})^{*}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq N})^{*}\Omega_{c}=(\mathcal{E}_{\leq N})^{*}\left[\Omega_{c}+\Omega_{>N}\right]=(\mathcal{E}_{\leq N})^{*}\Omega_{c}+\widetilde{\Omega}_{>N}

(3.42)

for some pluri-homogeneous 2-forms
\Omega_{>N},\widetilde{\Omega}_{>N}
in

\Sigma_{N+1}\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{2}_{q}
.
Now recall that, being
\mathcal{E}_{\leq N}(V)
the approximate inverse of
\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)

up to homogeneity
N
, by (
2.111
) one has
\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}\circ\mathcal{E}_{\leq N}={\rm Id}+F_{>N}
for some
F_{>N}(V)=M_{>N}(V)V
with

M_{>N}(V)
in
\Sigma_{N+1}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Thus we can also write

(\mathcal{E}_{\leq N})^{*}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq N})^{*}\Omega_{c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{pullback2}}}{{=}}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}\circ\mathcal{E}_{\leq N}\right)^{*}\Omega_{c}=\left({\rm Id}+F_{>N}\right)^{*}\Omega_{c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{pullback.form2}}}{{=}}\Omega_{c}+\Omega_{>N}^{\prime}(V)

(3.43)

for some pluri-homogeneous 2-form
\Omega_{>N}^{\prime}
in

\Sigma_{N+1}\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{2}_{q}

(by the first bullet below Definition
2.5
,
Lemma
2.10
and Proposition
2.15
).
Then (
3.42
)-(
3.43
) prove that
\mathcal{E}_{\leq N}
is symplectic
up to homogeneity
N
.

Next we prove (
3.41
). We start from (
3.39
) for
\mathcal{E}_{\leq N}
evaluated at
\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)
, i.e.

\big[{\rm d}_{V}\mathcal{E}_{\leq N}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z))\big]^{\top}\,E_{c}\,{\rm d}_{V}\mathcal{E}_{\leq N}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z))=E_{c}+E_{>N}(Z)

(3.44)

with

E_{>N}(Z)
in
\Sigma_{N+1}{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, by Proposition
2.15
and Lemma
2.10
.
Then apply
J_{c}
to the left of (
3.44
) and

\big[{\rm d}_{Z}\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)\big]J_{c}\big[{\rm d}_{Z}\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)\big]^{\top}
to the right of it
and use the first and last of (
2.112
),
and Proposition
2.15

to deduce (
3.41
).
∎

The approximate flow of a
Hamiltonian smoothing vector field
is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
p,N\in\mathbb{N}
with
p\leq N
.
Let
Y(U)
be a homogeneous
Hamiltonian smoothing vector field in

\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{p+1}^{-\varrho}
for some
\varrho\geq 0
. Then its approximate flow

\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau}
(provided by Lemma
2.26
)
is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
(Definition
3.11
).

Proof.

Recalling that
\Omega_{c}={\rm d}\theta_{c}
we have

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau})^{*}\Omega_{c}\,

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{commu}}}{{=}}{\rm d}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau})^{*}\theta_{c}\,

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{deriv:pullback}}}{{=}}{\rm d}(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau})^{*}\mathcal{L}_{Y}\theta_{c}\,+{\rm d}\theta_{>N+1}^{\tau}\,

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{cartan},\eqref{commu},\eqref{d2=0}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau})^{*}{\rm d}(i_{Y}\Omega_{c})\,+{\rm d}\theta_{>N+1}^{\tau}\,

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{complexvecham}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau})^{*}{\rm d}^{2}H_{p+2}\,+{\rm d}\theta_{>N+1}^{\tau}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{d2=0}}}{{=}}{\rm d}\theta_{>N+1}^{\tau}\,

(3.45)

where
H_{p+2}
is the Hamiltonian of
Y(U)
and

\theta_{>N+1}^{\tau}
is a pluri-homogeneous
1
-form
in
\Sigma_{N+2}\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{1}_{q}
.
Integrating (
3.45
) from
0
to
\tau
,
and using that
\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{0}={\rm Id}
, we get

(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau})^{*}\Omega_{c}\,=\Omega_{c}+\Omega_{>N}^{\tau}\,,\quad\Omega_{>N}^{\tau}:=\int_{0}^{\tau}{\rm d}\theta_{>N+1}^{t}\,{\rm d}t

where
\Omega_{>N}
is in
\Sigma_{N+1}\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{2}_{q}
.
This proves that
\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau}
is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
.
∎

A symplectic map up to homogeneity
N

transforms a Hamiltonian system up to homogeneity
N
into
another Hamiltonian system up to homogeneity
N
.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
p,N\in\mathbb{N}
with
p\leq N
,
K,K^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
K\geq K^{\prime}+1
.
Let
Z:={\bf M}_{0}(U;t)U
as in (
3.35
).
Assume
\mathcal{D}(Z;t)=M(Z;t)Z
is a symplectic map up to homogeneity
N
(Definition
3.11
) such that

M(Z;t)-{\rm Id}\in\begin{cases}\Sigma\mathcal{M}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\qquad\qquad\text{if}\quad{\bf M}_{0}(U;t)={\rm Id}\,,\\
\Sigma\mathcal{M}_{K,0,p}[\breve{r},N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\,,\,\forall\breve{r}>0\quad\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

(3.46)

If
Z(t)

solves a
U
-dependent Hamiltonian system up to homogeneity
N

(Definition
3.10
),
then the variable

W:=\mathcal{D}(Z;t)

solves another
U
-dependent Hamiltonian system up to homogeneity
N

(generated by the transformed Hamiltonian).

Proof.

Decompose
\mathcal{D}(Z;t)=\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)+M_{>N}^{\mathcal{D}}(Z;t)Z

where
\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z):=\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}[M(Z;t)]Z
is
its pluri-homogeneous component and

M_{>N}^{\mathcal{D}}(Z;t)\in\begin{cases}\mathcal{M}_{K,{K^{\prime}},N+1}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\qquad\qquad\text{if}\quad{\bf M}_{0}(U;t)={\rm Id}\,,\\
\mathcal{M}_{K,0,N+1}[\breve{r}]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\,,\,\forall\breve{r}>0\quad\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

By Definition
3.11

the map
\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)
satisfies (
3.38
).
If
Z(t)
solves (
3.36
) then
W=\mathcal{D}(Z;t)
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}W

\displaystyle=\big({\rm d}_{Z}\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)+M_{>N}^{\mathcal{D}}(Z;t)\big)\left[J_{c}\nabla_{Z}H(Z)+M_{>N}(U;t)U\right]+(\partial_{t}M_{>N}^{\mathcal{D}}(Z;t))Z

\displaystyle={\rm d}_{Z}\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)J_{c}\nabla_{Z}H(Z)+M^{\prime}_{>N}(U;t)U

(3.47)

where, by the first bullet below Definition
2.5
and Proposition
2.15
,

M^{\prime}_{>N}(U;t)\in\mathcal{M}_{K,{K^{\prime}+1},N+1}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\ .

(3.48)

Denote by
\breve{\mathcal{D}}_{\leq N}(W)
the approximate inverse up
to homogeneity
N
of
\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)
(see Lemma
2.24
).
Then

\breve{\mathcal{D}}_{\leq N}(W)=\breve{\mathcal{D}}_{\leq N}\big(\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)+M_{>N}^{\mathcal{D}}(Z;t)Z\big)=Z+\breve{M}^{\prime}_{>N}(Z;t)Z

where, by (
2.111
) and
Proposition
2.15
,

\breve{M}^{\prime}_{>N}(Z;t)\in\begin{cases}\mathcal{M}_{K,{K^{\prime}},N+1}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\qquad\qquad\text{if}\quad{\bf M}_{0}(U;t)={\rm Id}\,,\\
\mathcal{M}_{K,0,N+1}[\breve{r}]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\,,\,\forall\breve{r}>0\quad\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

Finally we substitute
Z={\bf M}_{0}(U;t)U
, cfr. (
3.35
),
in the non–homogeneous term
\breve{M}^{\prime}_{>N}(Z;t)Z

and using
(iii)
and
(i)
Proposition
2.15
we
get

Z=\breve{\mathcal{D}}_{\leq N}(W)+\breve{M}_{>N}(U;t)U

(3.49)

with

\breve{M}_{>N}(U;t)\in\mathcal{M}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
We substitute (
3.49
) in the term
\nabla_{Z}H(Z)
in (
3.47
) to obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}W

\displaystyle={\rm d}_{Z}\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)J_{c}\nabla_{Z}H(\breve{\mathcal{D}}_{\leq N}(W))+M^{\prime\prime}_{>N}(U;t)U

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{diinv2},\eqref{Z.M0}}}{{=}}{\rm d}_{Z}\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)J_{c}[{\rm d}_{Z}\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)]^{\top}[{\rm d}_{W}\breve{\mathcal{D}}_{\leq N}(\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z))]^{\top}\nabla_{Z}H(\breve{\mathcal{D}}_{\leq N}(W))+M^{\prime\prime\prime}_{>N}(U;t)U

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{lasolita},\eqref{Z.M0}}}{{=}}J_{c}[{\rm d}_{W}\breve{\mathcal{D}}_{\leq N}(W)]^{\top}\nabla_{Z}H(\breve{\mathcal{D}}_{\leq N}(W))+M^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}_{>N}(U;t)U

\displaystyle=J_{c}\nabla_{W}(H\circ\breve{\mathcal{D}}_{\leq N})(W)+M^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}_{>N}(U;t)U

(3.50)

where
M^{\prime\prime}_{>N}(U;t)
,
M^{\prime\prime\prime}_{>N}(U;t),

M^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}_{>N}(U;t)
are matrices
of operators as in (
3.48
).
Note that in the very last passage we also substituted
\mathcal{D}_{\leq N}(Z)=W+M_{>N}(U;t)U
where
M_{>N}(U;t)
is a matrix of operators as in
(
3.48
).
This proves that system (
3.50
) is Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
.
∎

3.3
Linear symplectic flows

We consider the flow of a linearly
Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
paradifferential operator.

Lemma 3.16
.

(Linear symplectic flow)

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}
,
N,K,K^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
K^{\prime}\leq K
,
m\leq 1
,
r>0
. Let

J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(B\right)}
be a linearly Hamiltonian operator up to homogeneity
N

(Definition
3.6
)
where
B(\tau,U;t,x,\xi)
is a matrix of symbols

B(\tau,U;t,x,\xi):=\begin{pmatrix}b_{1}(\tau,U;t,x,\xi)&b_{2}(\tau,U;t,x,\xi)\\
\overline{b_{2}(\tau,U;t,x,-\xi)}&\overline{b_{1}(\tau,U;t,x,-\xi)}\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{cases}b_{1}\in\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r,N]\\
b_{2}\in\Sigma\Gamma^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r,N]\,,\end{cases}

(3.51)

with

b_{1}^{\vee}-b_{1}
in
\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[r]
and the imaginary part

\textup{ Im }b_{2}
in
\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[r]
(cfr. (
3.31
)) uniformly in
|\tau|\leq 1
.
Then there exists
s_{0}>0
such that, for any
U\in B_{s_{0},\mathbb{R}}^{K}(I;r)
,
the system

\begin{cases}\partial_{\tau}\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{B}(U;t)=J_{c}\,{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\big(B(\tau,U;t,x,\xi)\big)\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{B}(U;t)\\
\mathcal{G}_{B}^{0}(U;t)={\rm Id}\,,\end{cases}

(3.52)

has a unique solution
\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{B}(U)
defined for all
|\tau|\leq 1
,
satisfying the following properties:

(i)

Boundedness:
For any
s\in\mathbb{R}
the linear map
\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{B}(U;t)
is invertible and there is
r(s)\in]0,r[
such that for any
U\in B_{s_{0},\mathbb{R}}^{K}(I;r(s))
for any
0\leq k\leq K-K^{\prime}
,
V\in C_{*}^{K-K^{\prime}}(I;\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}))
,

\|\partial_{t}^{k}(\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{B}(U;t)V)\|_{\dot{H}^{s-\frac{3}{2}k}}+\|\partial_{t}^{k}(\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{B}(U;t)^{-1}V)\|_{\dot{H}^{s-\frac{3}{2}k}}\leq\big(1+C_{s,k}\|U\|_{k+K^{\prime},s_{0}}\big)\|V\|_{k,s}

(3.53)

uniformly in
|\tau|\leq 1
.

In particular
\mathcal{G}_{B}^{\tau}(U;t)
and
\mathcal{G}_{B}^{\tau}(U;t)^{-1}

are non–homogeneous spectrally localized maps in
\mathcal{S}_{K,K^{\prime},0}^{0}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
according to Definition
2.16
.

(ii)

Linear symplecticity:
The map
\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{B}(U;t)
is linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
(Definition
3.7
).
If
J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(B\right)}
is linearly Hamiltonian (Definition
3.2
), then
\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{B}(U;t)
is linearly symplectic (Definition
3.3
).

(iii)

Homogeneous expansion:

\mathcal{G}_{B}^{\tau}(U;t)
and its inverse are spectrally localized maps and
\mathcal{G}_{B}^{\tau}(U;t)^{\pm}-{\rm Id}
belong to

\Sigma\mathcal{S}_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{(N+1)m_{0}}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
with
m_{0}:=\max(m,0)
, uniformly in
|\tau|\leq 1
.

Proof.

Since the symbols

b_{1}
and
\textup{ Im }b_{2}
have order
0
and
\textup{ Re }b_{2}
has order

m\leq 1
, the
existence of the flow
\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{B}(U;t)
and the estimates (
3.53
)
(actually with loss of
k
derivatives instead of
\tfrac{3}{2}k
)
are classical and follow as in Lemma 3.22 of
[
16
]
. In view of (
2.72
), the bounds (
3.53
) imply that
\mathcal{G}_{B}^{\tau}(U;t)

is in
\mathcal{S}_{K,K^{\prime},0}^{0}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
The inverse
\mathcal{G}_{B}^{\tau}(U;t)^{-1}
satisfies the same estimates regarding it
as the time
\tau
-flow

\mathcal{G}^{\tau^{\prime}}_{B^{-}}(U;t)|_{\tau^{\prime}=\tau}
of the system

\partial_{\tau^{\prime}}\mathcal{G}_{B^{-}}^{\tau^{\prime}}(U;t)=J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(B^{-}(\tau,\tau^{\prime},U;t,x,\xi))\mathcal{G}_{B^{-}}^{\tau^{\prime}}(U;t)\,,\quad\mathcal{G}_{B^{-}}^{0}(U;t)={\rm Id}\,,

(3.54)

where
B^{-}(\tau,U;t,x,\xi):=-B(\tau-\tau^{\prime},U;t,x,\xi)
.

Let us prove item
(ii)
. Set
{\bf B}:={{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}{(B(\tau,U;t,x,\xi))}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\tau}:=\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{B}(U;t)
for brevity.
By (
3.52
) we get, for any
\tau
,

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}(\mathcal{G}^{\tau})^{\top}\,E_{c}\,\mathcal{G}^{\tau}

\displaystyle=-(\mathcal{G}^{\tau})^{\top}{\bf B}^{\top}J_{c}E_{c}\mathcal{G}^{\tau}+(\mathcal{G}^{\tau})^{\top}E_{c}J_{c}{\bf B}\mathcal{G}^{\tau}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle J_{c}E_{c}={\rm Id},\eqref{Ecci}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{G}^{\tau})^{\top}({\bf B}-{\bf B}^{\top})\mathcal{G}^{\tau}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{ipoBnBtN}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{G}^{\tau})^{\top}({\bf B}_{>N}-{\bf B}_{>N}^{\top})\mathcal{G}^{\tau}\,.

Therefore

(\mathcal{G}^{\tau})^{\top}\,E_{c}\,\mathcal{G}^{\tau}=E_{c}+S_{>N}\quad\text{where}\quad S_{>N}:=\int_{0}^{\tau}(\mathcal{G}^{\tau^{\prime}})^{\top}({\bf B}_{>N}-{\bf B}_{>N}^{\top})\mathcal{G}^{\tau^{\prime}}\,{\rm d}\tau^{\prime}

is
a matrix of spectrally localized maps in
\mathcal{S}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
because

\mathcal{G}_{B}^{\tau}(U;t)
and
\mathcal{G}_{B}^{\tau}(U;t)^{-1}

are in
\mathcal{S}_{K,K^{\prime},0}^{0}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
,
the paradifferential operator

{\bf B}
belongs to
\Sigma\mathcal{S}^{m_{0}}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

(see the fourth bullet after Definition
2.16
),
and
(ii)
of Proposition
2.19
.
This proves that the
\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{B}(U;t)
is linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
according to Definition
3.7
.
The same proof shows that, if
J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(B\right)}
is linearly Hamiltonian, then

\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{B}(U;t)
is linearly symplectic.

Let us prove item
(iii)
. By (
3.52
),
iterating
N
–times the fundamental theorem of calculus
we get the expansion

\mathcal{G}_{B}^{\tau}(U;t)={\rm Id}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}S_{j}^{\tau}(U)+S_{>(pN)}^{\tau}(U;t)

(3.55)

where

S_{j}^{\tau}(U):=\int_{0}^{\tau}\!\int_{0}^{\tau_{1}}\!\!\!\cdots\!\!\int_{0}^{\tau_{j-1}}J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(B(\tau_{1},U;t,x,\xi))\cdots J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(B(\tau_{j},U;t,x,\xi))\,{\rm d}\tau_{1}\cdots{\rm d}\tau_{j}

and, writing for brevity
{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(B(\tau_{j},U)):={{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(B(\tau_{j},U;t,x,\xi))
,

S_{>(pN)}^{\tau}(U;t)=\int_{0}^{\tau}\!\int_{0}^{\tau_{1}}\!\!\!\cdots\!\!\int_{0}^{\tau_{N}}J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(B(\tau_{1},U))\cdots J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(B(\tau_{N+1},U))\mathcal{G}_{B}^{\tau_{N+1}}(U;t)\,{\rm d}\tau_{1}\cdots{\rm d}\tau_{N+1}.

Since each
{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(B(\tau_{j},U))

belongs to
\Sigma\mathcal{S}^{m_{0}}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

and
\mathcal{G}_{B}^{\tau_{N+1}}(U)
is in
\mathcal{S}_{K,K^{\prime},0}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

we deduce, by
(ii)
of Proposition
2.19
, that

\mathcal{G}_{B}^{\tau}(U;t)-{\rm Id}
in (
3.55
) is a matrix of spectrally localized maps in
\Sigma\mathcal{S}_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{(N+1)m_{0}}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, uniformly in
|\tau|\leq 1
.
The analogous statement for
\mathcal{G}_{B}^{\tau}(U;t)^{-1}-{\rm Id}
follows by (
3.54
).
∎

The flow generated by a Fourier multiplier satisfies similar properties.

Lemma 3.17
.

(Flow of a Fourier multiplier)

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}
and
g_{p}(Z;\xi)
be a
p
–homogeneous,

x
-independent, real symbol in
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{p}
. Then the flow

\mathcal{G}_{g_{p}}^{\tau}(Z)
defined by

\partial_{\tau}\mathcal{G}_{g_{p}}^{\tau}(Z)={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}g_{p}(Z;\xi)\right)}\mathcal{G}_{g_{p}}^{\tau}(Z)\,,\quad\mathcal{G}_{g_{p}}^{0}(Z)={\rm Id}\,,

(3.56)

is well defined for any
|\tau|\leq 1
and satisfies the following properties:

(i)

Boundedness:

For any
K\in\mathbb{N}
and
r>0
the flow
\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)
is a real-to-real diagonal matrix of spectrally localized
maps in
\mathcal{S}_{K,0,0}^{0}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. Moreover there is
s_{0}>0
such that for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
there is
r(s)\in(0,r)
such that for any
functions
Z\in B^{K}_{s_{0},\mathbb{R}}(I;r(s))

and

W\in C^{K}_{*}(I;\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}))
, it results, for any
0\leq k\leq K
,

\|\partial_{t}^{k}(\mathcal{G}_{g_{p}}^{\tau}(Z)W)\|_{\dot{H}^{s-\frac{3}{2}k}}+\|\partial_{t}^{k}(\mathcal{G}_{g_{p}}^{\tau}(Z)^{-1}W)\|_{\dot{H}^{s-\frac{3}{2}k}}\leq\big(1+C_{s,k}\|Z\|_{k,s_{0}}\big)\|W\|_{k,s}

(3.57)

uniformly in
|\tau|\leq 1
.

(ii)

Linear symplecticity:

The flow map
\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)
is linearly symplectic (Definition
3.3
).

(iii)

Homogeneous expansion:
The flow map
\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)
and its inverse
\mathcal{G}^{-\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)
are matrices of spectrally localized maps
such that
\mathcal{G}^{\pm\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)-{\rm Id}
belong to
\Sigma\mathcal{S}^{\frac{3}{2}(N+1)}_{K,0,p}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
,
uniformly in
|\tau|\leq 1
.

Proof.

Since
g_{p}(Z;\xi)
is real and independent of
x
, then the flow
\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)
is well defined in
\dot{H}^{s}
and it is unitary, namely
\|\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)W\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}=\|W\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}
. Moreover, since
g_{p}
is a Fourier multiplier of order
\frac{3}{2}
,
we have

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}(\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)W)\|_{\dot{H}^{s-\frac{3}{2}}}

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)\partial_{t}W\|_{\dot{H}^{s-\frac{3}{2}}}+\|{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\partial_{t}g_{p}(Z;\xi)\right)}\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)W\|_{\dot{H}^{s-\frac{3}{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq\|W\|_{1,s}+C\|Z\|_{1,s_{0}}^{p}\|W\|_{0,s}\,.

The estimates for the
k
–th derivative follow similarly using also that
\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)^{-1}=\mathcal{G}^{-\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)
.

To prove
(ii)
we use that, in view of (
2.24
), (
3.22
)
and since
g_{p}(Z;\xi)
is real valued,
the operator
{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}g_{p}(Z;\xi)\right)}
is linearly Hamiltonian, according to Definition
3.2
.
Then, as
for item (
ii
) of Lemma
3.16
,
the flow
\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)
is linearly symplectic.
Finally also item
(iii)
follows as for item (
iii
) of Lemma
3.16
,
since
{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}g_{p}(Z;\xi)\right)}^{k}
is in

\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{kp}^{\frac{3}{2}k}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and

\mathcal{G}^{\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)
is in
\mathcal{S}_{K,0,0}^{0}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, uniformly in
|\tau|\leq 1
.
∎

3.4
Paradifferential Hamiltonian structure

In order to compute the Hamiltonian vector field associated to a paradifferential Hamiltonian we provide the following result.

Lemma 3.18
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathbb{R}
.
Let
S(U)
be a real-to-real symmetric matrix of
p
-homogeneous spectrally localized maps in
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{p}^{m}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and define the Hamiltonian function

H(U):=\frac{1}{2}\big\langle S(U)U,U\big\rangle_{r}\ .

(3.58)

Then its gradient

\nabla H(U)=S(U)U+R(U)U

(3.59)

where
R(U)
is a real-to-real matrix of homogeneous smoothing operators in

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{p}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
for any
\varrho\geq 0
.

Proof.

By the definition (
2.151
), the gradient
\nabla H(U)
is the vector field

\nabla H(U)=S(U)U+L(U)^{\top}U\quad\text{where}\quad L(U)W:=\frac{1}{2}{\rm d}_{U}S(U)[W]U\,.

(3.60)

As
S(U)
is a spectrally localized map in
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{p}^{m}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, by Lemma
2.21
the transposed of its internal differential, namely
L(U)^{\top}
,
is a smoothing operator in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{p}^{-\varrho}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
for any
\varrho\geq 0
. Then (
3.59
) follows from (
3.60
).
∎

As a corollary we obtain the Hamiltonian vector field associated to a paradifferential Hamiltonian.

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathbb{R}
and

a(U;x,\xi)
a real valued homogeneous
symbol in
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{p}
.
Then the Hamiltonian vector field generated
by the Hamiltonian

H(U):=\mathrm{Re}\,\langle A(U)u,\overline{u}\rangle_{\dot{L}^{2}_{r}}=\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}0&\overline{A(U)}\\
A(U)&0\end{pmatrix}U,U\Big\rangle_{r},\quad A(U):={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(a(U;x,\xi)\right)}\,,

is

J_{c}\nabla H(U)={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}a(U;x,\xi)\right)}U+R(U)U

where
R(U)
is a real-to-real matrix of homogeneous smoothing operators in

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{p}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
for any
\varrho\geq 0
.

We now prove that if a homogeneous
Hamiltonian vector field
X(U)=J_{c}\nabla H(U)
can be written in paradifferential form

X(U)=J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A(U))U+R(U)U

where
A(U)
is a matrix of symbols and
R(U)
is a smoothing operator,
then
{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A(U)\right)}={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A(U)\right)}^{\top}
up to a smoothing operator.
As a consequence we may always assume, up to modifying the smoothing operator,
that the paradifferential operator
{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A(U))
is symmetric, namely that

J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A(U))
is linearly Hamiltonian.

Lemma 3.20
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varrho\geq 0
. Let

X(U)=J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A(U;x,\xi)\right)}U+R(U)U=J_{c}\nabla H(U)

(3.61)

be a
(p+1)
-homogeneous Hamiltonian vector field, where (cfr. (
2.28
))

A(U;x,\xi)=\begin{pmatrix}a(U;x,\xi)&b(U;x,\xi)\\
\overline{b(U;x,-\xi)}&\overline{a(U;x,-\xi)}\end{pmatrix}

(3.62)

is matrix of symbols in
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{p}^{m}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

and
R(U)
is a real-to-real
matrix of smoothing operators in

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{p}^{-\varrho}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Then we may write

X(U)=J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A_{1}(U;x,\xi))U+R_{1}(U)U

(3.63)

where the matrix of paradifferential operators
{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{1}(U;x,\xi)\right)}
is symmetric,
with matrix of symbols

A_{1}(U;x,\xi)=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}a+a^{\vee}&b+\overline{b}\\
\overline{b}^{\vee}+b^{\vee}&\overline{a+a^{\vee}}\end{pmatrix}

(3.64)

and
R_{1}(U)
is another real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators in

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{p}^{-\varrho}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

The linear vector field
{\rm d}_{U}X(U)
, obtained linearizing
a Hamiltonian vector field
X(U)=J_{c}\nabla H(U)
, is Hamiltonian, namely

{\rm d}_{U}X(U)=J_{c}S(U)\quad\text{where}\quad S(U)=S(U)^{\top}\quad\text{is symmetric}\,.

On the other hand, by linearizing (
3.61
),

S(U)={{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A(U))+{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}({\rm d}_{U}A(U)[\cdot])U+J_{c}\breve{R}(U)

where
\breve{R}(U):=R(U)+{\rm d}_{U}R(U)[\cdot]U
is a
matrix of smoothing operators in

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{p}^{-\varrho}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

(see the remark after Definition
2.5
).
Then, since
S(U)
is symmetric, it results, writing for brevity
A:=A(U)
,

{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A)-{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A)^{\top}=\big((J_{c}\breve{R}(U))^{\top}-{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}({\rm d}_{U}A(U)[\cdot])U\big)+\big(\big[{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}({\rm d}_{U}A(U)[\cdot])U\big]^{\top}-J_{c}\breve{R}(U)\big).

We now apply Lemma
2.22
to the spectrally localized map

{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A(U))-{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A(U))^{\top}
which has the form (
2.106
)
with

L:=\big((J_{c}\breve{R}(U))^{\top}-{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}({\rm d}_{U}A(U)[\cdot])U\big)\,,\quad R:=\big(\big[{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}({\rm d}_{U}A(U)[\cdot])U\big]^{\top}-J_{c}\breve{R}(U)\big)\,.

(3.65)

By Lemma
2.21
, the operator

\big[{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}({\rm d}_{U}A(U)[\cdot])U\big]^{\top}
is in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{p}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
,
and therefore both
L^{\top}
and
R
in (
3.65
) are
p
-homogeneous
smoothing operators in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{p}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
The assumptions of
Lemma
2.22
are satisfied, implying that

{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A(U))-{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A(U))^{\top}=:R^{\prime}(U)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{p}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\,.

In conclusion we deduce
(
3.63
) with

{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}{(A_{1}(U))}:=\frac{1}{2}\Big({{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}{(A(U))}+{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}{(A(U))}^{\top}\Big)\,,\quad R_{1}(U):=\frac{1}{2}J_{c}R^{\prime}(U)+R(U)\,,

and (
3.64
) follows recalling (
2.26
).
∎

Another consequence of Lemma
2.22
is the following.

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varrho\geq 0
.
Let
S(U)
be a matrix of spectrally localized homogeneous maps in
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{p}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
which is linearly Hamiltonian (Definition
3.2
) of the form

S(U)=J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A(U;x,\xi)\right)}+R(U)\,,

(3.66)

where
A(U;x,\xi)
is a real-to-real
matrix of symbols in
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{p}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

as in (
3.62
),
and
R(U)
is a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{p}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Then
we may write

S(U)=J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{1}(U;x,\xi)\right)}+R_{1}(U)\,

where
the matrix of symbols
A_{1}(U;x,\xi)
in
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{p}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

has the form (
3.64
)
and
R_{1}(U)
is another matrix of real-to-real smoothing operators in

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{p}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
In particular the homogeneous operator

J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{1}(U)\right)}
is linearly Hamiltonian.

Proof.

It is enough to prove that the operator

{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A(U)\right)}
is equal to
{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A(U)\right)}^{\top}
up to a
matrix of smoothing operators.
To prove this claim, recall that
S(U)
linearly Hamiltonian means that

E_{c}S(U)
is symmetric, so that by (
3.66
) one gets

{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A(U)\right)}-{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A(U)\right)}^{\top}=-R(U)^{\top}E_{c}-E_{c}R(U)\,.

Now, since
S(U)
and
{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A(U)\right)}
are spectrally localized maps, so is
R(U)
in (
3.66
).
By Remark
2.11
the transpose

R(U)^{\top}
is also a smoothing operator in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{p}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, proving the claim.
∎

4
Construction of a Darboux symplectic corrector

If
{\bf B}(U;t)
is a spectrally localized map
which is linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
,
then the associated nonlinear map

\Phi_{\leq N}(U):={\bf B}_{\leq N}(U)U
, where
{\bf B}_{\leq N}(U)=\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}({\bf B}(U;t))
,
is
not
symplectic up to homogeneity
N
. In this section we provide a systematic procedure to
construct a nearby nonlinear map which is symplectic up to homogeneity
N

according to Definition
3.11
.

Theorem 4.1
.

(Symplectic correction up to homogeneity
N
)

Let
p,N\in\mathbb{N}
with
p\leq N
. Consider a nonlinear map

\Phi_{\leq N}(U):={\bf B}_{\leq N}(U)U\,,

(4.1)

where

(i)

{\bf B}_{\leq N}(U)-{\rm Id}
is a matrix of
pluri–homogeneous spectrally localized maps in

\Sigma_{p}^{N}{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
;

(ii)

{\bf B}_{\leq N}(U)
is linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
(Definition
3.7
).

Then there exists a real-to-real map

\mathcal{C}_{\leq N}(W)=W+R_{\leq N}(W)W\quad\text{with}\quad R_{\leq N}(W)\in\Sigma_{p}^{N}{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}_{q}^{-\varrho}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\,,\ \mbox{ for any }\varrho\geq 0\,,

(4.2)

such that the Darboux correction

\mathcal{D}_{N}(U):=\big(\mathcal{C}_{\leq N}\circ\Phi_{\leq N}\big)(U)=\big({\rm Id}+R_{\leq N}(\Phi_{\leq N}(U))\big)\Phi_{\leq N}(U)

(4.3)

is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
, according to Definition
3.11
.

Remark 4.2
.

The first assumption
implies that
the operator in (
4.7
)
is smoothing for any
\varrho>0
. This fact
and the second assumption allow to deduce
that the vector field representing
the perturbed symplectic
1
-form
\theta_{\leq N}
in (
4.10
)
is a smoothing
perturbation of
E_{c}V
,
see (
4.11
).
These properties are crucial to guarantee that the vector field
Y^{\tau}(V)
solving the Darboux equation (
4.26
) is smoothing (see Lemma
4.9
), which in turn implies that the Darboux corrector
\mathcal{C}_{\leq N}(W)
in (
4.2
) is a smoothing perturbation of the identity.

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
4.1
.

In order to correct the nonlinear map
\Phi_{\leq N}

defined in (
4.1
) we develop a perturbative Darboux procedure to construct a nearby
symplectic map up to homogeneity
N
.
The map
\Phi_{{\leq N}}
induces
the nonstandard symplectic

2
-form

\Omega_{\leq N}:=\Psi_{\leq N}^{*}\ \Omega_{c}

(4.4)

where
\Psi_{{\leq N}}
is the approximate inverse of
\Phi_{{\leq N}}
defined
by Lemma
2.24
and
\Omega_{c}
is the
standard symplectic form in (
3.12
).
The next lemma describes properties of the approximate inverse
\Psi_{\leq N}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

(Approximate inverse)
The approximate inverse
up to homogeneity
N
of
the map
\Phi_{\leq N}(U)={\bf B}_{\leq N}(U)U
defined in (
4.1
)
has the form

\Psi_{\leq N}(V)={\bf A}_{\leq N}(V)V

(4.5)

where

(i)

{\bf A}_{{\leq N}}(V)-{\rm Id}
is a matrix of pluri-homogeneous spectrally localized maps in
\Sigma_{p}^{N}{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
;

(ii)

{\bf A}_{{\leq N}}(V)
is linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
(Definition
3.7
),
more precisely
(
3.33
) holds.

In addition

{\rm d}_{V}\Psi_{{\leq N}}(V)={\bf A}_{{\leq N}}(V)+{\bf G}_{{\leq N}}(V)\,,\qquad{\bf G}_{{\leq N}}(V)\widehat{V}:={\rm d}_{V}{\bf A}_{{\leq N}}(V)[\widehat{V}]V\,,

(4.6)

and

(iii)

{\bf G}_{{\leq N}}(V)

is a matrix of pluri–homogeneous operators in

\Sigma_{p}^{N}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
;

(iv)

the transposed operator

{\bf G}_{{\leq N}}^{\top}(V):=[{\bf G}_{{\leq N}}(V)]^{\top}\in\Sigma_{p}^{N}{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\varrho}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

(4.7)

is a matrix of
\varrho
-smoothing operators for arbitrary
\varrho\geq 0
.

Proof.

Items
(i)
and
(ii)
are proved in Lemma
3.8
, and

(iii)
follows by the fifth bullet after Definition
2.16
.
Finally
(iv)
follows applying
Lemma
2.21
to each spectrally localized map
\mathcal{P}_{q}({\bf A}_{\leq N}(V))
for
q=p,\ldots,N
(with
U\leadsto V
and
V\leadsto\widehat{V}
).
∎

We now compute
\Omega_{{\leq N}}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

(Non-standard symplectic form
\Omega_{\leq N}
)

The symplectic
2
-form
\Omega_{{\leq N}}=\Psi_{\leq N}^{*}\ \Omega_{c}
in (
4.4
) is represented as

\Omega_{{\leq N}}(V)=\langle E_{{\leq N}}(V)\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{r}

with symplectic tensor

E_{{\leq N}}(V)=E_{c}+{\bf A}_{\leq N}^{\top}(V)E_{c}{\bf G}_{\leq N}(V)+{\bf G}_{\leq N}^{\top}(V)E_{c}{\bf A}_{\leq N}(V)+{\bf G}_{\leq N}^{\top}(V)E_{c}{\bf G}_{\leq N}(V)+{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)

(4.8)

where

(i)

{\bf G}_{{\leq N}}^{\top}(V)E_{c}{\bf A}_{{\leq N}}(V)
and
{\bf G}_{{\leq N}}^{\top}(V)E_{c}{\bf G}_{{\leq N}}(V)
are matrices of pluri–homogeneous smoothing operators in
\Sigma_{p}^{2N}{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\varrho}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
for any
\varrho\geq 0
;

(ii)

{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V):={\bf A}_{\leq N}^{\top}(V)E_{c}{\bf A}_{\leq N}(V)-E_{c}
is a matrix of pluri–homogeneous spectrally localized maps in

\Sigma_{N+1}{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Moreover

\Omega_{{\leq N}}={\rm d}\theta_{\leq N}\,,

(4.9)

where
the
1
-form

\theta_{\leq N}:=\Psi_{\leq N}^{*}\ \theta_{c}\ ,\qquad\theta_{c}(V):=\frac{1}{2}\big\langle E_{c}V,\cdot\big\rangle_{r}\,,

(4.10)

has the form

\theta_{{\leq N}}(V)=\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle[Z_{{\leq N}}(V)+{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)]V,\cdot\Big\rangle_{r}\quad\text{with}\quad Z_{{\leq N}}(V):=E_{c}+{\bf G}_{{\leq N}}^{\top}(V)\ E_{c}\ {\bf A}_{{\leq N}}(V)\,.

(4.11)

Proof.

By (
2.161
) we have that

\Omega_{{\leq N}}(V)[X,Y]=\big\langle{\rm d}\Psi_{{\leq N}}(V)^{\top}\,E_{c}\,{\rm d}\Psi_{{\leq N}}(V)X,Y\big\rangle_{r}

which, using (
4.6
) and the fact
that
{\bf A}_{\leq N}(V)
is linearly symplectic up to homogeneity

N

(cfr. (
3.33
)), provides formula (
4.8
).
Then items (
i
)-(
ii
) follow by
(
4.6
), (
4.7
) and Proposition
2.15
.
The identity (
4.9
) follows by (
3.15
) and (
2.157
).
Finally
(
4.11
) follows similarly computing

\Psi_{\leq N}^{*}\theta_{c}
by (
2.160
),

\theta_{\leq N}(V)[X]=\frac{1}{2}\big\langle{\rm d}_{V}\Psi_{\leq N}(V)^{\top}\,\,E_{c}\,\Psi_{\leq N}(V),X\big\rangle\,,

and using (
4.5
), (
4.6
) and

{\bf A}_{\leq N}^{\top}(V)E_{c}{\bf A}_{\leq N}(V)=E_{c}+{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)
.
∎

The key step is to implement a Darboux–type procedure to transform the symplectic form
\Omega_{{\leq N}}
back to the standard symplectic form
\Omega_{c}
up to arbitrary high
degree of homogeneity. It turns out that the required transformation is a smoothing perturbation of the identity as claimed in Theorem
4.1
, see
Proposition
4.7
.
This is not at all obvious, since in the expression (
4.8
) of
E_{\leq N}(V)
the second operator

{\bf A}_{\leq N}^{\top}(V)E_{c}{\bf G}_{\leq N}(V)

is
not
smoothing.
However it has a nice structure that we now describe.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
X(V)
be a pluri-homogeneous vector field in
\Sigma_{p^{\prime}+1}\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{q}

for some
p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then

{\bf A}^{\top}_{\leq N}(V)E_{c}{\bf G}_{\leq N}(V)[X(V)]=\nabla\mathcal{W}(V)+R(V)V+M_{>N}(V)V

(4.12)

where

•

\mathcal{W}(V)
is
0
-form in
\Sigma_{p+p^{\prime}+2}\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{0}_{q}
;

•

R(V)
is matrix of pluri–homogeneous smoothing operators in
\Sigma_{p+p^{\prime}}{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\varrho}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
for any
\varrho\geq 0
;

•

M_{>N}(V)
is a matrix of pluri–homogeneous operators in
\Sigma_{N+1}{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

For simplicity of notation we set
{\bf A}(V):={\bf A}_{\leq N}(V)
and
{\bf G}(V):={\bf G}_{\leq N}(V)
.

Step 1:

For any vector
W
, the linear operator

{\bf K}_{W}(V):={\bf A}(V)^{\top}\,E_{c}\,\big[{\rm d}_{V}{\bf A}(V)[W]\big]

(4.13)

is symmetric up to homogeneity
N
,
precisely

{\bf K}_{W}(V)-{\bf K}_{W}(V)^{\top}={\rm d}_{V}{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)[W]

(4.14)

where
{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)
is the spectrally localized map in
