\Sigma_{N+1}\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
of Lemma
4.4
.

Indeed, differentiating the relation
{\bf A}(V)^{\top}E_{c}{\bf A}(V)=E_{c}+{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)
(see Lemma
4.4

(ii)
),
in direction
W
, we get

\displaystyle{\rm d}_{V}{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)[W]

\displaystyle={\bf A}(V)^{\top}E_{c}\,\big[{\rm d}_{V}{\bf A}(V)[W]\big]+\big[{\rm d}_{V}{\bf A}(V)[W]\big]^{\top}E_{c}{\bf A}(V)

\displaystyle={\bf A}(V)^{\top}E_{c}\,\big[{\rm d}_{V}{\bf A}(V)[W]\big]-\Big({\bf A}(V)^{\top}E_{c}\,\big[{\rm d}_{V}{\bf A}(V)[W]\big]\Big)^{\top}

proving, in view of (
4.13
), (
4.14
).

Step 2:

The linear operator

{\bf K}(V):={\bf K}_{X(V)}(V)={\bf A}(V)^{\top}E_{c}\big[{\rm d}_{V}{\bf A}(V)[X(V)]\big]

(4.15)

can be decomposed as

{\bf K}(V)=S(V)+\breve{R}(V)+M_{>N}(V)

(4.16)

where

\bullet

S(V)
is a symmetric matrix of spectrally localized pluri–homogeneous maps in
\Sigma_{p+p^{\prime}}{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
;

\bullet

\breve{R}(V)
is
a symmetric matrix of pluri–homogeneous smoothing operators in
\Sigma_{p+p^{\prime}}{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\varrho}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
for any
\varrho\geq 0
as well as its transpose;

\bullet

M_{>N}(V):=\frac{1}{2}{\rm d}_{V}{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)[X(V)]
is a matrix of pluri–homogeneous operators in
\Sigma_{N+1}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

We apply Lemma

2.20
to each component

{\bf A}_{q}(V):=\mathcal{P}_{q}[{\bf A}(V)]
,
q=p,\ldots,N
, each of which is a map in

\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, see Lemma
4.3

(i)
.
Lemma
2.20
(with
M(U)U\leadsto X(V)
)
gives the decomposition

{\rm d}_{V}{\bf A}(V)[X(V)]=\sum_{q=p}^{N}q{\bf A}_{q}\big(X(V),V,\dots,V\big)=S^{\prime}(V)+R^{\prime}(V)\,,

(4.17)

where
S^{\prime}(V)
is a matrix of spectrally localized pluri–homogeneous maps in
\Sigma_{p+p^{\prime}}{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
R^{\prime}(V)
is
a matrix of pluri–homogeneous smoothing operators in
\Sigma_{p+p^{\prime}}{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\varrho}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
for any
\varrho\geq 0
, as well as its transpose.
Then we obtain by (
4.14
) (with
W=X(V)
),

\displaystyle{\bf K}(V)=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}({\bf K}(V)+{\bf K}(V)^{\top})+\frac{1}{2}{\rm d}_{V}{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)[X(V)]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{KV},\eqref{decompl0}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\big[{\bf A}^{\top}E_{c}S^{\prime}-S^{\prime\top}E_{c}{\bf A}\big](V)}_{:=S(V),\ S(V)=S(V)^{\top}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\big[{\bf A}^{\top}E_{c}R^{\prime}-R^{\prime\top}E_{c}{\bf A}\big](V)}_{:=\breve{R}(V),\ \breve{R}(V)=\breve{R}(V)^{\top}}+M_{>N}(V)\,,

where

M_{>N}(V)=\frac{1}{2}{\rm d}_{V}{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)[X(V)]
is
in
\Sigma_{N+1}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
by Proposition
2.15
.
Since the maps
S^{\prime}(V)
and
{\bf A}(V)
are spectrally localized then
S(V)
belongs to
\Sigma_{p+p^{\prime}}{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
by Proposition
2.19

(ii)
and Lemma
2.18
.
The operator
\breve{R}(V)
is in
\Sigma_{p+p^{\prime}}{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\varrho}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
for any
\varrho\geq 0
by
Proposition
2.19

(i)
, Lemma
2.18
and the fact that
R^{\prime}(V)
belongs

\Sigma_{p+p^{\prime}}{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\varrho}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
for any
\varrho\geq 0

as well as its transpose.

Conclusion:

The identity (
4.12
) follows by Step 2 defining

\mathcal{W}(V):=\frac{1}{2}\big\langle S(V)V,V\big\rangle_{r}\,,\quad S(V)\mbox{ in }\eqref{decompl}\ .

Indeed by Lemma
3.18
,
\nabla\mathcal{W}(V)=S(V)V+R^{\prime\prime}(V)V
for some smoothing operator
R^{\prime\prime}(V)
belonging to
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{p+p^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
for any
\varrho>0
.
Then
we get

\displaystyle{\bf A}^{\top}_{\leq N}(V)E_{c}{\bf G}_{\leq N}(V)[X(V)]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{selfad},\eqref{sommabella2}}}{{=}}{\bf K}(V)V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{decompl}}}{{=}}S(V)V+\breve{R}(V)V+M_{>N}(V)V

\displaystyle=\nabla\mathcal{W}(V)-R^{\prime\prime}(V)V+\breve{R}(V)V+M_{>N}(V)V\ ,

proving
(
4.12
) with
R(V):=\breve{R}(V)-R^{\prime\prime}(V)
which belongs to
\Sigma_{p+p^{\prime}}{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\varrho}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
for any
\varrho\geq 0
.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

The vector field
R(V)V
in (
4.12
) depends on
X(V)
and its differential
{\rm d}_{V}X(V)
.
This is because
S(V)
depends linearly on
X(V)

(actually it is the term
S^{\prime}(V)
that depends linearly on
X(V)
,
see (
4.17
)).
Hence the smoothing vector field
R^{\prime\prime}(V)V
coming from the gradient
\nabla\mathcal{W}(V)
, given explicitly by
R^{\prime\prime}(V)V:=L(V)^{\top}V
where
L(V)W=\frac{1}{2}{\rm d}_{V}S(V)[W]V
(see (
3.60
)),
depends on the differential of
X(V)
.

Now we present the main Darboux procedure.

Proposition 4.7
.

(Darboux procedure)

There exists
a
\tau
–dependent pluri-homogeneous smoothing vector field
Y^{\tau}(V)
in

\Sigma_{p+1}\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{-\varrho}_{p+1}
, for any
\varrho\geq 0
,
defined for
\tau\in[0,1]
,
such that its approximate time
1
–flow

\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}(V)=V+R_{\leq N}(V)V\quad\text{with}\quad R_{\leq N}(V)\in\Sigma_{p}^{N}{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\varrho}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\,,\ \ \forall\varrho\geq 0\,,

(4.18)

(given by Lemma
2.26
) satisfies

\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{*}\Omega_{{\leq N}}=\Omega_{c}+\Omega_{>N}

(4.19)

where
\Omega_{>N}
is a pluri-homogeneous
2
-form in

\Sigma_{N+1}\widetilde{\Uplambda}_{q}^{2}
.

Proof.

We follow the famous deformation argument by Moser.
We define the homothety between the symplectic
2
-forms

\Omega_{c}
and
\Omega_{\leq N}
defined in (
4.4
) by setting

\Omega^{\tau}:=\Omega_{c}+\tau(\Omega_{{\leq N}}-\Omega_{c})\,,\quad\forall\tau\in[0,1]\,.

(4.20)

Equivalently
\Omega^{\tau}=\langle E^{\tau}(V)\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{r}
with
associated symplectic tensor

\displaystyle E^{\tau}(V)

\displaystyle=E_{c}+\tau(E_{{\leq N}}(V)-E_{c})

(4.21)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{EB}}}{{=}}E_{c}+\tau{\bf R}_{{\leq N}}(V)+\tau{\bf A}_{{\leq N}}^{\top}(V)\ E_{c}\ {\bf G}_{{\leq N}}(V)+\tau{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)

(4.22)

where

\bullet

{\bf R}_{{\leq N}}(V)
is the matrix of
pluri–homogeneous smoothing operators

{\bf R}_{{\leq N}}(V):={\bf G}_{{\leq N}}^{\top}(V)\ E_{c}{\bf A}_{{\leq N}}(V)+{\bf G}_{{\leq N}}^{\top}(V)\ E_{c}\ {\bf G}_{{\leq N}}(V)

belonging to

\Sigma_{p}{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\varrho}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
for any
\varrho\geq 0
;

\bullet

{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)
is the map in
\Sigma_{N+1}{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
of Lemma
4.4
.

In addition, by (
4.9
) and (
3.15
), we have

\Omega^{\tau}={\rm d}\theta^{\tau}\,,\quad\theta^{\tau}:=\theta_{c}+\tau(\theta_{\leq N}-\theta_{c})

(4.23)

where
\theta_{\leq N}
is given in (
4.11
).

We look for a
\tau
–dependent pluri-homogeneous smoothing vector field
Y^{\tau}(V)
in
\Sigma_{p+1}\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{-\varrho}_{q}
, for any
\varrho\geq 0
,
such that its approximate flow
\mathcal{F}^{\tau}_{\leq N}
up to homogeneity
N

(defined by Lemma
2.26
),
satisfies

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}(\mathcal{F}^{\tau}_{\leq N})^{*}\Omega^{\tau}={\rm d}\theta^{\tau}_{>N+1}\,,\quad\forall\tau\in[0,1]\,,

(4.24)

for a certain
1
-form
\theta^{\tau}_{>N+1}
in
\Sigma_{N+2}\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{1}_{q}
.
Then, integrating (
4.24
) and recalling (
4.20
), we deduce

(\mathcal{F}^{1}_{\leq N})^{*}\Omega_{{\leq N}}=\Omega_{c}+\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\theta^{\tau}_{>N+1}\,{\rm d}\tau\,,

which proves (
4.19
) with
\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}:=\mathcal{F}^{1}_{\leq N}
and

\Omega_{>N}:=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\theta^{\tau}_{>N+1}\,{\rm d}\tau
.

We now construct the vector field
Y^{\tau}(V)
.
Using the definition of Lie derivative and the Cartan magic formula, we derive the chain of identities

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau})^{*}\Omega^{\tau}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Omegadtheta},\eqref{commu}}}{{=}}{\rm d}\Big(\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}(\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau})^{*}\theta^{\tau}\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{deriv:pullback}}}{{=}}{\rm d}\Big((\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{\tau})^{*}(\mathcal{L}_{Y^{\tau}}\theta^{\tau}+\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}\theta^{\tau})\Big)+{\rm d}\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}_{>N+1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{cartan},\eqref{commu}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{F}^{\tau}_{\leq N})^{*}{\rm d}\big((i_{Y^{\tau}}\circ{\rm d}+{\rm d}\circ i_{Y^{\tau}})\theta^{\tau}+\theta_{{\leq N}}-\theta_{c}\big)+{\rm d}\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}_{>N+1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Omegadtheta},\eqref{d2=0}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{F}^{\tau}_{\leq N})^{*}{\rm d}\big(i_{Y^{\tau}}\Omega^{\tau}+\theta_{{\leq N}}-\theta_{c}\big)+{\rm d}\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}_{>N+1}

(4.25)

where
\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}_{>N+1}
is a
1
-form in
\Sigma_{N+2}\widetilde{\Uplambda}_{q}^{1}
by Lemma
2.33
.
We look for a vector field
Y^{\tau}(V)
and a
0
-form
\mathcal{W}^{\tau}(V)
such that

i_{Y^{\tau}}\Omega^{\tau}+\theta_{{\leq N}}-\theta_{c}=\breve{\theta}^{\tau}_{>N+1}+{\rm d}\mathcal{W}^{\tau},

(4.26)

for some pluri–homogeneous
1
–form
\breve{\theta}^{\tau}_{>N+1}
in
\Sigma_{N+2}\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{1}_{q}
.
If (
4.26
) holds, then, in view of (
4.25
), equation (
4.24
) is satisfied with

\theta_{>N+1}^{\tau}:=(\mathcal{F}^{\tau}_{\leq N})^{*}\breve{\theta}^{\tau}_{>N+1}+\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}_{>N+1}\in\Sigma_{N+2}\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{1}_{q}\,.

We turn to solve equation (
4.26
).
Using (
4.21
), (
4.11
), recalling that

\theta_{c}(V)=\frac{1}{2}\langle E_{c}V,\cdot\rangle_{r}
,
and writing
\breve{\theta}^{\tau}_{>N+1}(V)=\frac{1}{2}\langle\breve{Z}^{\tau}_{>N}(V)V,\cdot\rangle_{r}
,
we first rewrite (
4.26
) as the equation

E^{\tau}(V)Y^{\tau}(V)+\frac{1}{2}\Big(Z_{{\leq N}}(V)V-E_{c}V+{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)V\Big)=\frac{1}{2}\breve{Z}^{\tau}_{>N}(V)V+\nabla\mathcal{W}^{\tau}(V)\ .

(4.27)

Remark 4.8
.

This equation is linear in
Y^{\tau}(V)
.
In the works
[
59
,
10
,
34
,
35
,
14
,
15
]

the operator
E^{\tau}(V)
is a smoothing perturbation of
E_{c}
, so is its inverse and the vector field
Y^{\tau}(V)
is immediately a smoothing vector field.
In our case,
E^{\tau}(V)
is a (possibly) unbounded perturbation of
E_{c}
, and its (approximate) inverse is only an
m
-operator.
Hence, the composition of the (approximate) inverse of
E^{\tau}(V)
with the smoothing operator
Z_{\leq N}(V)-E_{c}
(see (
4.11
))
is only an
m
-operator, not a smoothing one (see the bullet at pag.
2.1
).
Therefore we cannot directly conclude that
Y^{\tau}(V)
is a smoothing vector field.
We proceed differently and solve the equation (
4.27
) in homogeneity, exploiting the freedom given by the function
\nabla\mathcal{W}^{\tau}(V)
to remove the non-smoothing components of the equation, thanks to
structural Lemma
4.5
.

By (
4.22
)
and (
4.11
), equation (
4.27
) becomes

\displaystyle E_{c}Y^{\tau}(V)=

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}{\bf G}_{{\leq N}}^{\top}(V)\ E_{c}\ {\bf A}_{{\leq N}}(V)V-\tau{\bf R}_{{\leq N}}(V)Y^{\tau}(V)

(4.28)

\displaystyle-\tau{\bf A}_{{\leq N}}^{\top}(V)E_{c}{\bf G}_{{\leq N}}(V)Y^{\tau}(V)+\nabla\mathcal{W}^{\tau}(V)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{2}\breve{Z}^{\tau}_{>N}(V)V-\tfrac{1}{2}{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)V-\tau{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)Y^{\tau}(V)\,.

We now solve
(
4.28
)
for a smoothing vector field
Y^{\tau}(V)
, a suitable function
\mathcal{W}^{\tau}(V)
and a high homogeneity pluri–homogeneous map
\breve{Z}_{>N}^{\tau}(V)
by an iterative procedure in increasing order
of homogeneity. Note that
{\bf G}_{{\leq N}}^{\top}(V)\ E_{c}\ {\bf A}_{{\leq N}}(V)
and
{\bf R}_{{\leq N}}(V)

are smoothing operators unlike

{\bf A}_{{\leq N}}^{\top}(V)E_{0}{\bf G}_{{\leq N}}(V)Y^{\tau}(V)

that will be canceled using
\nabla\mathcal{W}^{\tau}(V)
, thanks to the structure property explicated in Lemma
4.5
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Fix
\overline{N}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
(\overline{N}+2)p\geq N+1
.
There exist

\bullet

a pluri-homogeneous smoothing vector field
Y^{\tau}(V)=\sum_{a=0}^{\overline{N}}Y_{(a)}^{\tau}(V)
, defined for any

\tau\in[0,1]
, with
Y_{(a)}^{\tau}(V)
in
\Sigma_{(a+1)p+1}\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{-\varrho}_{q}
for any
\varrho\geq 0
, uniformly in
\tau\in[0,1]
;

\bullet
a pluri-homogeneous Hamiltonian

\mathcal{W}^{\tau}(V)=\sum_{a=0}^{\overline{N}}\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{(a)}(V)
, defined for any

\tau\in[0,1]
, with
\mathcal{W}_{(a)}^{\tau}(V)
in
\Sigma_{(a+1)p+2}\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{0}_{q}
, uniformly in
\tau\in[0,1]
;

\bullet
a pluri-homogeneous matrix of operators
\breve{Z}_{>N}^{\tau}(V)
,
defined for any

\tau\in[0,1]
, in
\Sigma_{N+1}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, uniformly in
\tau\in[0,1]
;

which solve equation (
4.28
).

Proof.

We define

\begin{cases}Y_{(0)}(V):=-\frac{1}{2}E_{c}^{-1}{\bf G}^{\top}_{{\leq N}}(V)\ E_{c}\ {\bf A}_{{\leq N}}(V)V\\
\mathcal{W}_{(0)}^{\tau}(V):=0\end{cases}

(4.29)

Note that
Y_{(0)}(V)
is smoothing vector field in

\Sigma_{p+1}\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{-\varrho}_{q}
for any
\varrho\geq 0
, since

{\bf G}^{\top}_{{\leq N}}(V)\ E_{c}\ {\bf A}_{{\leq N}}(V)
are smoothing operators in
\Sigma_{p}{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\varrho}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
for any
\varrho\geq 0
, by Lemma
4.4
-(
i
).

For
a\geq 0
, we prove the following recursive statements:
there exist a

(S1
)_{a}
pluri-homogeneous smoothing vector field
Y_{(a)}^{\tau}(V)
belonging to
\Sigma_{(a+1)p+1}\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{q}^{-\varrho}
for any
\varrho\geq 0
;

(S2
)_{a}
pluri-homogeneous Hamiltonian
\mathcal{W}_{(a)}^{\tau}(V)
in
\Sigma_{{(a+1)p+2}}\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{0}_{q}
;

(S3
)_{a}
matrix of pluri-homogeneous operators
Z_{>N,(a)}^{\tau}(V)
in
\Sigma_{N+1}{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
;

uniformly in
\tau\in[0,1]
,
with
(Y_{(0)}^{\tau}(V),\mathcal{W}_{(0)}^{\tau}(V),Z_{>N,(0)}^{\tau}(V))
defined in (
4.29
),
satisfying, for any
a\geq 1
,

\displaystyle E_{c}Y_{(a)}^{\tau}(V)

\displaystyle=-\tau{\bf R}_{{\leq N}}(V)Y_{(a-1)}^{\tau}(V)

(4.30)

\displaystyle-\tau{\bf A}_{{\leq N}}^{\top}(V)E_{c}{\bf G}_{{\leq N}}(V)[Y_{(a-1)}^{\tau}(V)]+\nabla\mathcal{W}_{(a)}^{\tau}(V)

\displaystyle+Z_{>N,(a)}^{\tau}(V)V-\tau{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)Y_{(a-1)}^{\tau}(V)\,.

Given
(Y_{(a-1)}^{\tau}(V),\mathcal{W}_{(a-1)}^{\tau}(V),Z_{>N,(a-1)}^{\tau}(V))
we now prove

(S1
)_{a}
-
(S3
)_{a}
.
Note that the first term in (
4.30
) is a smoothing vector field of homogeneity
(a+1)p+1
while the first term in the second line of (
4.30
) has homogeneity
(a+1)p+1
but it is not a smoothing vector field.
However by Lemma
4.5
we have the decomposition

{\bf A}_{{\leq N}}^{\top}(V)E_{c}{\bf G}_{{\leq N}}(V)[Y^{\tau}_{(a-1)}(V)]=\nabla\breve{\mathcal{W}}^{\tau}_{(a-1)}(V)+\breve{R}^{\tau}_{(a-1)}(V)V+\breve{M}_{>N,(a-1)}^{\tau}(V)V\,,

where

\breve{\mathcal{W}}^{\tau}_{(a-1)}(V)
is a Hamiltonian in

\Sigma_{{(a+1)p+2}}\widetilde{\Uplambda}^{0}_{q}
,

\breve{R}^{\tau}_{(a-1)}(V)
is a pluri–homogeneous smoothing operator in
\Sigma_{(a+1)p}{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\varrho}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and

\breve{M}_{>N,(a-1)}^{\tau}(V)
is a pluri–homogeneous operator in
\Sigma_{N+1}{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Then equation (
4.30
) becomes

\displaystyle E_{c}Y_{(a)}^{\tau}(V)

\displaystyle=-\tau{\bf R}_{{\leq N}}(V)Y_{(a-1)}^{\tau}(V)-\tau\breve{R}^{\tau}_{(a-1)}(V)V

\displaystyle+\nabla\big(\mathcal{W}_{(a)}^{\tau}(V)-\tau\breve{\mathcal{W}}_{(a-1)}^{\tau}(V)\big)

\displaystyle+Z_{>N,(a)}^{\tau}(V)V-\tau\breve{M}_{>N,(a-1)}^{\tau}(V)V-\tau{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)Y_{(a-1)}^{\tau}(V)\,

which is solved by

\begin{cases}Y_{(a)}^{\tau}(V):=-E_{c}^{-1}\big[\tau{\bf R}_{{\leq N}}(V)Y_{(a-1)}^{\tau}(V)+\tau\breve{R}^{\tau}_{(a-1)}(V)V\big]\\
{\mathcal{W}}_{(a)}^{\tau}(V):=\tau\breve{\mathcal{W}}_{(a-1)}^{\tau}(V)\\
Z_{>N,(a)}^{\tau}(V)V:=\tau\breve{M}_{>N,(a-1)}^{\tau}(V)V+\tau{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)Y_{(a-1)}^{\tau}(V)\,\end{cases}

proving
(S1
)_{a}
-
(S3
)_{a}
.

Summing (
4.29
) and (
4.30
) for any
a=1,\ldots,\overline{N}
we find that

Y^{\tau}(V)=\sum_{a=0}^{\overline{N}}Y_{(a)}^{\tau}(V)
and

\mathcal{W}^{\tau}(V)=\sum_{a=0}^{\overline{N}}\mathcal{W}_{(a)}^{\tau}(V)
solve (
4.28
)
with

\frac{1}{2}\breve{Z}_{>N}^{\tau}(V)V:=\frac{1}{2}{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)V+\sum_{a=1}^{\overline{N}}Z_{>N,(a)}^{\tau}(V)V+\tau\big[{\bf R}_{\leq N}(V)+{\bf A}_{\leq N}^{\top}(V)E_{c}{\bf G}_{\leq N}(V)+{{\bf S}}_{>N}(V)\big]Y_{(\overline{N})}^{\tau}(V)

which is an operator in
\Sigma_{N+1}{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, since

(\overline{N}+2)p\geq N+1
. Lemma
4.9
is proved.
∎

The approximate flow up to homogeneity
N
of
the smoothing vector field
Y^{\tau}
defined by Lemma
4.9
solves
(
4.24
). This concludes the proof of Proposition
4.7
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

The map
\mathcal{E}_{N}:=\Psi_{\leq N}\circ\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}
, where
\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}
is defined in Proposition
4.7
, fulfills

\mathcal{E}_{N}^{*}\Omega_{c}=\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{*}\Psi_{\leq N}^{*}\Omega_{c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{OmegaB}}}{{=}}\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}^{*}\Omega_{\leq N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{denew2B}}}{{=}}\Omega_{c}+\Omega_{>N}

and so
\mathcal{E}_{N}
is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
.
We define the map
\mathcal{C}_{\leq N}
in (
4.2
) as the approximate inverse (given by Lemma
2.24
)
of the nonlinear map
\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}
in (
4.18
), hence it has the claimed form.
Since
\Psi_{\leq N}
is an approximate inverse of
\Phi_{\leq N}
,
the map

\mathcal{D}_{N}:=\mathcal{C}_{\leq N}\circ\Phi_{\leq N}

is an approximate inverse of
\mathcal{E}_{N}
, and so it is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
by
Lemma
3.13
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

We write
the good-unknown of Alinach (
1.17
) in complex variables

(u,\overline{u})
induced by the transformation
\mathcal{C}
defined in (
3.11
), obtaining the real-to-real
spectrally localized, linearly symplectic map (according to Definition
3.3
)

\mathcal{G}_{c}(U):=\mathcal{C}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\big[\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
-B(\mathcal{C}U)&1\end{smallmatrix}\big]\right)}\mathcal{C}^{-1},\quad(\eta,\psi)=\mathcal{C}U\,,

where
B(\eta,\psi)
is the real function
defined in (
5.14
) which, as stated in Lemma
5.1
, belongs
to
\Sigma{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
.
Then Theorem
1.2
follows by applying
Theorem
4.1
to the pluri-homogeneous spectrally localized map

{\bf B}_{\leq N}(U)=

\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}(\mathcal{G}_{c}(U))=\mathcal{C}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\big(\big[\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}[-B(\mathcal{C}U)]&1\end{smallmatrix}\big]\big)\mathcal{C}^{-1}
.
∎

Part II
Almost global existence of water waves

We now begin the proof of
the almost global existence Theorem
1.1

for solutions of the gravity-capillary water waves equations (
1.2
)
with constant vorticity.

After further describing the Hamiltonian structure of the
water waves equations (
1.2
) and diagonalizing
the linearized system at the
equilibrium, we paralinearize
the water waves equations (
1.2
)
with constant vorticity,
written in the Zakharov-Craig-Sulem
(\eta,\psi)
variables, which are the Hamiltonian system (
1.4
) with the non–standard Poisson tensor
J_{\gamma}
.
Then we express such paralinearized system in the Wahlén coordinates
(\eta,\zeta)
in (
5.2
), which coincides with the Hamiltonian system in (
5.3
) in standard Darboux form. Finally we write such paralinearized system in the complex variable
U
defined in (
5.6
), i.e. (
5.36
).
The final system (
5.37
) is
Hamiltonian in the complex sense
, i.e. has the form (
3.18
).

5
Paralinearization of the
water waves equations
with constant vorticity and its Hamiltonian structure

From now on we consider (
1.2
) as a system
on (a dense subspace of) the
homogeneous
space
\dot{L}^{2}_{r}\times\dot{L}^{2}_{r}
, namely,
denoting
X_{\gamma}(\eta,\psi)
the right hand side in (
1.2
),
we consider

\partial_{t}(\eta,\psi)=X_{\gamma}(\Pi_{0}^{\bot}\eta,\psi)

(5.1)

where
\Pi_{0}^{\perp}
is the
L^{2}
-projector onto the space of functions with zero average.
For simplicity of notation we shall not distinguish between (
5.1
)
and (
1.2
), which are equivalent via the isometric
isomorphism
\Pi_{0}^{\bot}

between
\dot{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
and
L^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})
.
System (
5.1
) is the Hamiltonian system
as in (
1.4
)
defined on (a dense subspace of)
\dot{L}^{2}_{r}\times\dot{L}^{2}_{r}
generated by
the Hamiltonian

H_{\gamma}(\Pi_{0}^{\bot}\eta,\psi)
, with
H_{\gamma}
in (
1.5
),
computing the
\dot{L}^{2}_{r}
-gradients
(\nabla_{\eta}H_{\gamma},\nabla_{\psi}H_{\gamma})

with respect to the scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\dot{L}^{2}_{r}}
in (
2.3
)
and regarding the Poisson tensor
J_{\gamma}
in
(
1.4
) as a linear operator acting in

\dot{L}^{2}_{r}\times\dot{L}^{2}_{r}
.
We shall not insist more on this detail.

Wahlén variables.

The variables
(\eta,\psi)

are not Darboux coordinates, since the Poisson tensor
J_{\gamma}
in
(
1.4
) is not the canonical one when
\gamma\neq 0
.
Wahlén noted in
[
69
]
that, introducing the variable

\zeta:=\psi-\tfrac{\gamma}{2}\partial_{x}^{-1}\eta
,
the coordinates
(\eta,\zeta)
are canonical coordinates.
Precisely, under the linear change of variables

\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\psi\end{pmatrix}=\mathcal{W}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad\mathcal{W}:=\begin{pmatrix}{\rm Id}&0\\
\frac{\gamma}{2}\partial_{x}^{-1}&{\rm Id}\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad\mathcal{W}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}{\rm Id}&0\\
-\frac{\gamma}{2}\partial_{x}^{-1}&{\rm Id}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(5.2)

the Poisson tensor
J_{\gamma}
becomes the standard one,

\mathcal{W}^{-1}J_{\gamma}(\mathcal{W}^{-1})^{\top}=J\,,\quad J:=\begin{pmatrix}0&{\rm Id}\\
-{\rm Id}&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

and the Hamiltonian system (
1.4
) assumes the standard Darboux form

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix}=J\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{\eta}\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}(\eta,\zeta)\\
\nabla_{\zeta}\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}(\eta,\zeta)\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}(\eta,\zeta):=H_{\gamma}\big(\eta,\zeta+\frac{\gamma}{2}\partial_{x}^{-1}\eta\big)\,.

(5.3)

Note that the new Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}
is still translation invariant
so is its Hamiltonian vector field.

Linearized equation at the equilibrium.

The linearized equations (
5.3
) at the equilibrium
(\eta,\zeta)=(0,0)
are
obtained by conjugating the linearized equations (
1.2
) at
(\eta,\psi)=(0,0)
, namely

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix}\!\!=\!\mathcal{W}^{-1}\!\begin{pmatrix}0&\!\!\!\!G(0)\\
-(g+\kappa D^{2})&\!\!\!\!\gamma G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\!\!\mathcal{W}\!=\!\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}&\!\!\!G(0)\\
-(g+\kappa D^{2}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}G(0)D^{-2})&\!\!\!\frac{\gamma}{2}\partial_{x}^{-1}G(0)\end{pmatrix}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix}

(5.4)

where
D:=\frac{1}{{\rm i}}\partial_{x}
and the Dirichlet-Neumann operator

G(0)
at the flat surface
\eta=0
is the Fourier multiplier
with symbol

{\mathtt{G}}(\xi):=\begin{cases}\xi\tanh(\mathtt{h}\xi)&0<\mathtt{h}<+\infty\\
|\xi|&\mathtt{h}=+\infty\,.\end{cases}

(5.5)

We diagonalize
system (
5.4
) introducing the complex variables

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}u\\
{\overline{u}}\end{pmatrix}:=\mathcal{M}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix}\,,

(5.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}M(D)&M(D)\\
-{\rm i}M^{-1}(D)&{\rm i}M^{-1}(D)\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathcal{M}^{-1}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}M(D)^{-1}&{\rm i}M(D)\\
M^{-1}(D)&-{\rm i}M(D)\end{pmatrix}\,,

where
M(D)
is the Fourier multiplier

M(D):=\left(\frac{G(0)}{g+\kappa D^{2}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}G(0)D^{-2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}\,.

(5.7)

A direct computation (cfr. Section 2.2. in
[
19
]
),
using the identities

M(D)\big(g+\kappa D^{2}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}G(0)D^{-2}\big)M(D)=\omega(D)=M^{-1}(D)G(0)M^{-1}(D)

(5.8)

where
\omega(D)
is the Fourier multiplier with symbol

\omega(\xi):=\sqrt{{\mathtt{G}}(\xi)\Big(g+\kappa\xi^{2}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}\frac{{\mathtt{G}}(\xi)}{\xi^{2}}\Big)}

(5.9)

(with
{\mathtt{G}}(\xi)
defined in (
5.5
))
shows that the variables
(u,\overline{u})
in
(
5.6
)
solve the diagonal linear system

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}u\\
{\overline{u}}\end{pmatrix}=-{\rm i}{\bf\Omega}(D)\begin{pmatrix}u\\
{\overline{u}}\end{pmatrix},\quad{\bf\Omega}(D):=\begin{pmatrix}\Omega(D)&0\\
0&-\overline{\Omega}(D)\end{pmatrix}

(5.10)

where

\Omega(D):=\omega(D)+{\rm i}\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1},\quad\overline{\Omega}(D):=\omega(D)-{\rm i}\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}\,.

(5.11)

The real-to-real
system (
5.10
) amounts to the scalar equation

\partial_{t}u=-{\rm i}\Omega(D)u\,,\quad u(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}u_{j}\,e^{{\rm i}jx}\ ,

which, written in Fourier basis, decouples in infinitely many harmonic oscillators

\partial_{t}u_{j}=-{\rm i}\Omega_{j}(\kappa)u_{j}\,,\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\,,

where

\Omega_{j}(\kappa):=\omega_{j}(\kappa)+\frac{\gamma}{2}\frac{{\mathtt{G}}(j)}{j}\,,\qquad\omega_{j}(\kappa):=\sqrt{{\mathtt{G}}(j)\Big(g+\kappa j^{2}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}\frac{{\mathtt{G}}(j)}{j^{2}}\Big)}\,.

(5.12)

Note that the map
j\rightarrow\Omega_{j}(\kappa)
is not even because of the vorticity term
\frac{\gamma}{2}\frac{{\mathtt{G}}(j)}{j}
which is odd.

A fundamental property that we prove in Appendix
B
is that
the linear frequencies
\{\Omega_{j}(\kappa)\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}

satisfy the non-resonance conditions of Theorem
B.1
.
Thus one can think to implement a Birkhoff normal form procedure.
Since the water waves equations (
1.2
) are a quasilinear system
we first paralinearize them.

Paralinearization of the water waves equations with constant vorticity.

We denote the horizontal and vertical components of the velocity field
at the free interface by

\displaystyle V=V(\eta,\psi):=(\partial_{x}\Phi)(x,\eta(x))=\psi_{x}-\eta_{x}B\,,

(5.13)

\displaystyle B=B(\eta,\psi):=(\partial_{y}\Phi)(x,\eta(x))=\frac{G(\eta)\psi+\eta_{x}\psi_{x}}{1+\eta_{x}^{2}}\,.

(5.14)

Lemma 5.1
.

(Water-waves equations in Zakharov-Craig-Sulem
variables)

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\varrho\geq 0
.
For any
K\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
there exist
s_{0},r>0
such that,
if
(\eta,\psi)\in B^{K}_{s_{0}}(I;r)
solves (
1.2
), then

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\eta

\displaystyle=G(0)\psi+{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-B(\eta,\psi;x)|\xi|-{\rm i}V_{\gamma}(\eta,\psi;x)\xi+a_{0}(\eta,\psi;x,\xi)\right)}\eta+{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(b_{-1}(\eta;x,\xi)\right)}\psi

\displaystyle\quad+R_{1}(\eta)\psi+R^{\prime}_{1}(\eta,\psi)\eta\,,

(5.15)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi

\displaystyle=-(g+\kappa D^{2})\eta+\gamma G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}\psi+{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-\kappa{\mathtt{f}}(\eta;x)\xi^{2}-B^{2}(\eta,\psi;x)|\xi|+c_{0}(\eta,\psi;x,\xi)\right)}\eta

\displaystyle\quad+{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(B(\eta,\psi;x)|\xi|-{\rm i}V_{\gamma}(\eta,\psi;x)\xi+d_{0}(\eta,\psi;x,\xi)\right)}\psi+R_{2}(\eta,\psi)\psi+R^{\prime}_{2}(\eta,\psi)\eta

(5.16)

where

•

V_{\gamma}(\eta,\psi;x):=V(\eta,\psi;x)-\gamma\eta(x)
is a function in
\Sigma{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
as well as the functions

V,B
defined in (
5.13
)-(
5.14
);

•

a_{0},\,c_{0},\,d_{0}
are
symbols in
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
and

b_{-1}(\eta;x,\xi)
is a symbol in
\Sigma\Gamma^{-1}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
satisfying (
2.27
);

•

the function
{\mathtt{f}}(\eta;x):=(1+\eta_{x}^{2}(x))^{-\frac{3}{2}}-1
belongs to
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,2}[r,N]
;

•

R_{1}
,
R_{1}^{\prime}
,
R_{2}
,
R_{2}^{\prime}
are real smoothing operators in

\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
.

Moreover (
5.15
)–(
5.16
) are the
Hamiltonian
system (
1.4
).

Proof.

By Proposition 7.4 of
[
16
]
, the function

B
defined in (
5.14
) belongs to
\Sigma{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
, as well as
the function
V
in (
5.13
) and
V_{\gamma}=V-\gamma\eta
.

Paralinearization of the first equation in (
1.2
).

We use the paralinearization of the Dirichlet-Neumann operator
G(\eta)\psi
proved in
[
16
]
.
By Propositions 7.5 and 8.3 in
[
16
]
where
\omega:=\psi-{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(B)\eta

is the “good unknown” of Alinhac, using
Propositions
2.14
and
2.19
-(
i
),
the second bullet below (
2.53
),
and noting that
\xi\tanh(\mathtt{h}\xi)-|\xi|\in\widetilde{\Gamma}^{-\varrho}_{0}
, for any
\varrho>0
,
we get

\displaystyle G(\eta)\psi

\displaystyle=G(0)\big(\psi-{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(B)\eta\big)+{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\big(-{\rm i}V\xi+\breve{a}_{0}\big)\eta+{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(b_{-1})\big(\psi-{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(B)\eta\big)

\displaystyle\quad+R^{\prime}(\eta,\psi)\eta+R(\eta)\psi

\displaystyle=G(0)\psi+{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-B|\xi|-{\rm i}V\xi+a_{0}^{\prime}\right)}\eta+{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(b_{-1})\psi+R(\eta)\psi+R^{\prime}(\eta,\psi)\eta

(5.17)

where
\breve{a}_{0},a_{0}^{\prime}
are symbols in
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
,

b_{-1}
is a symbol in
\Gamma^{-1}_{K,0,1}[r,N]

depending only on
\eta
, and

R(\eta)
,
R^{\prime}(\eta,\psi)
are smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
.

We now paralinearize the term with the vorticity. Using Lemma
2.12
,
Proposition
2.14
, the identity
\eta_{x}={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\xi\right)}\eta
and
(i)
of Proposition
2.15
we get

\eta\eta_{x}={{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(\eta){{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}({\rm i}\xi)\eta+{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(\eta_{x})\eta+R(\eta)\eta={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\eta\xi+\tfrac{1}{2}\eta_{x}\right)}\eta+R(\eta)\eta

(5.18)

where
R(\eta)
is a homogeneous smoothing operator in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{1}
. Then
(
5.17
) and (
5.18
) imply (
5.15
)
with symbol
a_{0}:=a_{0}^{\prime}+\frac{\gamma}{2}\eta_{x}
in
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
. Furthermore, since (
5.15
) is a real equation we may assume that
a_{0}
and
b_{-1}
satisfy (
2.27
) eventually replacing them with
\tfrac{1}{2}(a_{0}+\overline{a}_{0}^{\vee})
and
\tfrac{1}{2}(b_{-1}+\overline{b}_{-1}^{\vee})
and replacing the smoothing remainders with
\tfrac{1}{2}(R_{1}+\overline{R}_{1})
and
\tfrac{1}{2}(R^{\prime}_{1}+\overline{R}^{\prime}_{1})
.

Paralinearization of the second equation in (
1.2
).

By Lemma
2.12
and Proposition
2.14
we get

-\frac{1}{2}\psi_{x}^{2}=-{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\psi_{x}\xi-\tfrac{1}{2}\psi_{xx}\right)}\psi+R(\psi)\psi

(5.19)

where
R(\psi)
is a
smoothing operator in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{1}
.
Next, recalling (
5.14
)
and using Lemma
2.12
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{(G(\eta)\psi+\eta_{x}\psi_{x})^{2}}{(1+\eta_{x}^{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(1+\eta_{x}^{2})B^{2}

(5.20)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left((1+\eta_{x}^{2})B\right)}B+\frac{1}{2}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(B\right)}\big[(1+\eta_{x}^{2})B\big]+R(\eta,\psi)\psi+R^{\prime}(\eta,\psi)\eta

where
R(\eta,\psi)
,
R^{\prime}(\eta,\psi)
are smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
.
Consider the second term in the right hand side of (
5.20
).
Applying Lemma
2.12
, Propositions
2.14

and
2.15
(and since
{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(B\right)}[1]
is a constant which we neglect because we consider (
1.2
) posed in homogeneous spaces) we get

\tfrac{1}{2}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(B)\big[(1+\eta_{x}^{2})B\big]=\tfrac{1}{2}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left((1+\eta_{x}^{2})B\right)}B+{\rm i}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(B^{2}\eta_{x}\xi+\breve{c}_{0}\right)}\eta+R(\eta,\psi)\psi+R^{\prime}(\eta,\psi)\eta

(5.21)

where

\breve{c}_{0}
is a symbol in
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
and

R(\eta,\psi)
,
R^{\prime}(\eta,\psi)

are smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
.
Then by (
5.20
)-(
5.21
) and (
5.14
) we deduce that

\frac{1}{2}(1+\eta_{x}^{2})B^{2}={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(B\right)}[G(\eta)\psi+\eta_{x}\psi_{x}]-{\rm i}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(B^{2}\eta_{x}\xi+\breve{c}_{0}\right)}\eta+R(\eta,\psi)\psi+R^{\prime}(\eta,\psi)\eta\,.

(5.22)

In order to expand this term
we first write

\eta_{x}\psi_{x}={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\eta_{x}\xi-\tfrac{1}{2}\eta_{xx}\right)}\psi+{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\psi_{x}\xi-\tfrac{1}{2}\psi_{xx}\right)}\eta+R(\eta)\psi+R^{\prime}(\psi)\eta

(5.23)

where
R(\eta),R^{\prime}(\psi)
are smoothing homogeneous operators in

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{1}
.
Finally,
using (
5.17
), (
5.23
), Proposition
2.15
,
and exploiting the explicit form (
5.13
) of the function
V
,
we conclude that (
5.22
) is equal to

(
5.20
)

\displaystyle={{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(-B^{2}|\xi|+\breve{c}_{0})\eta+{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(B|\xi|+{\rm i}B\eta_{x}\xi+\breve{d}_{0})\psi+R(\eta,\psi)\psi+R^{\prime}(\eta,\psi)\eta\,

(5.24)

where
\breve{c}_{0},\breve{d}_{0}
are symbols in
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
and

R(\eta,\psi),R^{\prime}(\eta,\psi)
are smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
.

Next we paralinearize the capillary term

\kappa\partial_{x}\Big[\frac{\eta_{x}}{(1+\eta_{x}^{2})^{1/2}}\Big]=\kappa\partial_{x}F(\eta_{x})\,,\quad F(t):=\frac{t}{(1+t^{2})^{1/2}}\,.

The Bony paralinearization formula for the composition (Lemma 3.19 in
[
16
]
) and Proposition
2.14
imply

\displaystyle\partial_{x}F(\eta_{x})

\displaystyle={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\xi\right)}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(F^{\prime}(\eta_{x})\right)}\eta_{x}+R(\eta)\eta={{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\Big(-(1+\eta_{x}^{2})^{-\frac{3}{2}}\xi^{2}+c_{0}^{\prime}\Big)\eta+R(\eta)\eta

(5.25)

\displaystyle=-D^{2}\eta-{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\mathtt{f}(\eta;x)\xi^{2}+c_{0}^{\prime}\right)}\eta+R(\eta)\eta

where
c_{0}^{\prime}
is symbol in
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
,
the function
{\mathtt{f}}(\eta;x):=(1+\eta_{x}^{2}(x))^{-\frac{3}{2}}-1
belongs to
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,2}[r,N]

and
R(\eta)
is a smoothing operator in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
.

Next, by Lemma
2.12
and Proposition
2.14
we get

\eta\psi_{x}={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\eta\xi-\tfrac{1}{2}\eta_{x}\right)}\psi+{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\psi_{x}\right)}\eta+R(\eta)\psi+R^{\prime}(\psi)\eta

(5.26)

where
R(\eta)
,
R^{\prime}(\psi)
are homogeneous smoothing operators in

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{1}
.

Finally using (
5.17
), Propositions
2.14
and
2.15
-
(i)
, and that
\partial_{x}^{-1}={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\frac{1}{{\rm i}\xi}\right)}
we get

\partial_{x}^{-1}G(\eta)\psi=G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}\psi+{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(c_{-2}\right)}\psi+{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(d_{0}^{\prime}\right)}\eta+R(\eta,\psi)\eta+R^{\prime}(\eta,\psi)\psi

(5.27)

where
c_{-2}
is a symbol in
\Sigma\Gamma^{-2}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
,
the symbol
d_{0}^{\prime}
is in
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
and
R,R^{\prime}
are smoothing operators in

\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
.

In conclusion, collecting (
5.19
), (
5.24
), (
5.25
), (
5.26
), (
5.27
) and using the explicit form of
V
in (
5.13
)
we deduce the second equation (
5.16
) with symbols

c_{0}:=\breve{c}_{0}-\kappa c_{0}^{\prime}+\gamma\psi_{x}+\gamma d_{0}^{\prime}
and

d_{0}:=\frac{1}{2}\psi_{xx}+\breve{d}_{0}-\frac{\gamma}{2}\eta_{x}+\gamma c_{-2}
in

\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
. Since (
5.16
) is a real equation we may assume that
c_{0}
and
d_{0}
satisfy (
2.27
) arguing as for the first equation.
∎

Remark 5.2
.

The symbols
a_{0},\,c_{0},\,d_{0}
in (
5.15
)–(
5.16
) can be explicitly computed in terms of
V
and
B
(e.g. see
[
18
]
). On the other hand the symbol
b_{-1}(\eta;x,\xi)
is expected to be of order
-\infty
but in
[
16
]

it has been estimated as a symbol of order
-1
only.

We write (
5.15
)-(
5.16
) as the system

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\psi\end{pmatrix}=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&G(0)\\
-(g+\kappa D^{2})&\gamma G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\psi\end{pmatrix}

(5.28)

\displaystyle+{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}-B|\xi|-{\rm i}V_{\gamma}\xi&0\\
-\kappa{\mathtt{f}}\xi^{2}-B^{2}|\xi|&B|\xi|-{\rm i}V_{\gamma}\xi\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}a_{0}&b_{-1}\\
c_{0}&d_{0}\end{bmatrix}\right)}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\psi\end{pmatrix}+R(\eta,\psi)\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\psi\end{pmatrix}.

Wahlén coordinates.

We now transform system (
5.28
) in the Wahlén coordinates
(\eta,\zeta)
defined in (
5.2
).

Lemma 5.3
.

(Water-waves equations in Wahlén variables)

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\varrho\geq 0
.
For any
K\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
there exist
s_{0},r>0
such that,
if
(\eta,\psi)\in B^{K}_{s_{0}}(I;r)
solves (
1.2
)
then
(\eta,\zeta)=\mathcal{W}^{-1}(\eta,\psi)
defined in (
5.2
) solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix}=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}&G(0)\\
-(g+\kappa D^{2}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}G(0)D^{-2})&\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle+{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}-B^{(1)}(\eta,\zeta;x)|\xi|-{\rm i}V^{(1)}(\eta,\zeta;x)\xi&0\\
-\kappa{\mathtt{f}}(\eta;x)\xi^{2}-[B^{(1)}(\eta,\zeta;x)]^{2}|\xi|&B^{(1)}(\eta,\zeta;x)|\xi|-{\rm i}V^{(1)}(\eta,\zeta;x)\xi\end{bmatrix}\right)}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle+{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}a_{0}^{(1)}(\eta,\zeta;x,\xi)&b_{-1}(\eta;x,\xi)\\
c_{0}^{(1)}(\eta,\zeta;x,\xi)&d^{(1)}_{0}(\eta,\zeta;x,\xi)\end{bmatrix}\right)}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix}+R(\eta,\zeta)\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix}

(5.29)

where

•

B^{(1)}(\eta,\zeta;x):=B(\mathcal{W}(\eta,\zeta);x)
and
V^{(1)}(\eta,\zeta;x):=V_{\gamma}(\mathcal{W}(\eta,\zeta);x)
are functions in
\Sigma{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
,

{\mathtt{f}}(\eta;x)
is the function in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,2}[r,N]
defined in Lemma
5.1
;

•

a_{0}^{(1)}(\eta,\zeta;x,\xi)
,
c_{0}^{(1)}(\eta,\zeta;x,\xi)
,

d_{0}^{(1)}(\eta,\zeta;x,\xi)
are symbols in
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]

satisfying (
2.27
), and
the symbol
b_{-1}(\eta;x,\xi)
in
\Sigma\Gamma^{-1}_{K,0,1}[r,N]

is defined in Lemma
5.1
;

•

R(\eta,\zeta)

is a matrix of real
smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Moreover system (
5.29
) is the
Hamiltonian
system (
5.3
).

Proof.

By (
5.2
) and (
5.28
) one has

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}0&G(0)\\
-(g+\kappa D^{2})&\gamma G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{W}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix}

(5.30)

\displaystyle\ \ +\mathcal{W}^{-1}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}-B|\xi|-{\rm i}V_{\gamma}\xi&0\\
-\kappa{\mathtt{f}}(\eta)\xi^{2}-B^{2}|\xi|&B|\xi|-{\rm i}V_{\gamma}\xi\end{bmatrix}\right)}\mathcal{W}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix}

(5.31)

\displaystyle\ \ +\mathcal{W}^{-1}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}a_{0}&b_{-1}\\
c_{0}&d_{0}\end{bmatrix}\right)}\mathcal{W}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix}+R(\eta,\zeta)\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix}

(5.32)

where
R(\eta,\zeta)
is a matrix of smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
We now compute the above conjugated operators
applying the transformation rule

\mathcal{W}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{A}&\mathcal{B}\\
\mathcal{C}&\mathcal{D}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{W}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{A}+\frac{\gamma}{2}\mathcal{B}\partial_{x}^{-1}&\mathcal{B}\\
\mathcal{C}-\frac{\gamma}{2}\partial_{x}^{-1}\mathcal{A}-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}\partial_{x}^{-1}\mathcal{B}\partial_{x}^{-1}+\frac{\gamma}{2}\mathcal{D}\partial_{x}^{-1}&\mathcal{D}-\frac{\gamma}{2}\partial_{x}^{-1}\mathcal{B}\end{pmatrix}.

(5.33)

The operator in the right hand side of (
5.30
) is given in (
5.4
).
Then by (
5.33
) and Proposition
2.14
,

\displaystyle\eqref{ilprimo2}={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}-B|\xi|-{\rm i}V_{\gamma}\xi&0\\
-\kappa{\mathtt{f}}(\eta)\xi^{2}-B^{2}|\xi|+\breve{c}_{0}&B|\xi|-{\rm i}V_{\gamma}\xi\end{bmatrix}\right)}+R(\eta,\zeta)\,,

(5.34)

where the symbol

\breve{c}_{0}:=\frac{\gamma}{2}\frac{1}{{\rm i}\xi}\#_{\varrho}[B|\xi|+{\rm i}V_{\gamma}\xi]+\frac{\gamma}{2}[B|\xi|-{\rm i}V_{\gamma}\xi]\#_{\varrho}\frac{1}{{\rm i}\xi}

belongs to
\Sigma\Gamma_{K,0,1}^{0}[r,N]
and

R(\eta,\zeta)
is matrix of smoothing operators in

\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Finally, by (
5.33
) and Proposition
2.14
, we deduce that

\eqref{ilprimo3}={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}a_{0}^{\prime}&b_{-1}\\
c^{\prime}_{0}&d_{0}^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}\right)}+R(\eta,\zeta)\,,

(5.35)

where
a_{0}^{\prime},c^{\prime}_{0},d_{0}^{\prime}
are symbols in
\Sigma\Gamma_{K,0,1}^{0}[r,N]
and

R(\eta,\zeta)
are smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
In conclusion, by (
5.4
), (
5.34
), (
5.35
), we deduce that
system (
5.30
)-(
5.32
) has the form (
5.29
) with
symbols
a_{0}^{(1)}:=a_{0}^{\prime}
,

c_{0}^{(1)}:=\breve{c}_{0}+c_{0}^{\prime}
and
d_{0}^{(1)}:=d_{0}^{\prime}
evaluated at
(\eta,\psi)=\mathcal{W}(\eta,\zeta)
which belong to
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
.
Since the Whalen transformation is a real map,
we may assume that
a_{0}^{(1)},c_{0}^{(1)},d_{0}^{(1)}
satisfy (
2.27
) arguing as in the previous lemma.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

The first two matrices of paradifferential operators in (
5.29
) have the linear Hamiltonian structure (
3.9
)-(
3.10
). We do not claim that the third matrix of paradifferential operators in (
5.29
) has the linear Hamiltonian structure (
3.9
)-(
3.10
).
Nevertheless in Lemma
5.5
we shall recover the complex
linear Hamiltonian structure of
J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A_{0}^{(2)})
, up to homogeneity
N
, thanks to the abstract Lemma
3.20
.

Complex coordinates.

We now diagonalize the linear part of the system (
5.29
)
at
(\eta,\zeta)=(0,0)
introducing the complex variables

U:=\begin{pmatrix}u\\
{\overline{u}}\end{pmatrix}=\mathcal{M}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
{\zeta}\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad\mathcal{M}^{-1}:\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{4}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\times\dot{H}^{s-\frac{1}{4}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\to\dot{H}^{s}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})\,,\ \forall s\in\mathbb{R}\,,

(5.36)

where
\mathcal{M}
is the matrix of Fourier multipliers defined in (
5.6
).

Lemma 5.5
.

(Hamiltonian formulation of the water waves in complex coordinates)

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\varrho\geq 0
.
For any
K\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
there exist
s_{0},r>0
such that,
if
(\eta,\psi)\in B^{K}_{s_{0}}(I;r)
is a solution of (
5.29
) then
U
defined in (
5.36
) solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}U

\displaystyle=J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{\frac{3}{2}}(U;x)\omega(\xi)\right)}U+\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}U

(5.37)

\displaystyle+J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{1}(U;x,\xi)+A_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;x,\xi)+A^{(2)}_{0}(U;x,\xi)\right)}U+R(U)U

where
J_{c}
is the Poisson tensor defined in (
1.22
) and

•

\omega(\xi)\in\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{0}
is the symbol in
(
5.9
);

•

A_{\frac{3}{2}}(U;x)\in\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,0}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
is the matrix of real functions

A_{\frac{3}{2}}(U;x):=\begin{pmatrix}f(U;x)&1+f(U;x)\\
1+f(U;x)&f(U;x)\end{pmatrix},

(5.38)

where
f(U;x):=\frac{1}{2}\mathtt{f}(\mathcal{M}U;x)
belongs to
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,2}[r,N]
. Note that

J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A_{\frac{3}{2}}\omega(\xi))
is linearly Hamiltonian according to
Definition
3.2
;

•

A_{1}(U;x,\xi)\in\Sigma\Gamma^{1}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

is the matrix of symbols

A_{1}(U;x,\xi):=\begin{pmatrix}{\rm i}B^{(2)}(U;x)|\xi|&-V^{(2)}(U;x)\xi\\
V^{(2)}(U;x)\xi&-{\rm i}B^{(2)}(U;x)|\xi|\end{pmatrix}

(5.39)

where
B^{(2)}(U;x):=B^{(1)}(\mathcal{M}U;x)
and
V^{(2)}(U;x):=V^{(1)}(\mathcal{M}U;x)
are real functions in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
.
Note that

J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A_{1})
is linearly Hamiltonian;

•

A_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;x,\xi)\in\Sigma\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}_{K,0,2}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

is the symmetric matrix of symbols

A_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;x,\xi)=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}[B^{(2)}(U;x)]^{2}|\xi|M^{2}(\xi)

(5.40)

where
M(\xi)\in\widetilde{\Gamma}^{-\frac{1}{4}}_{0}
is the symbol of the Fourier multiplier
M(D)
in (
5.7
).
Note that
J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A_{\frac{1}{2}})
is linearly Hamiltonian;

•

A^{(2)}_{0}(U;x,\xi)
is a matrix of symbols in
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and the operator
J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A^{(2)}_{0})
is linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
according to Definition
3.6
;

•

R(U)
is a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Moreover system (
5.37
) is
Hamiltonian in the complex sense
, i.e. has the form (
3.18
).

Proof.

We begin by noting that the operator
\mathcal{M}
in the change of coordinates (
5.36
) has the form (cfr. (
5.6
))

\mathcal{M}=\breve{\mathcal{M}}\circ\mathcal{C},\qquad\breve{\mathcal{M}}:=\begin{pmatrix}M(D)&0\\
0&M(D)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

where
M(D)
is the Fourier multiplier in (
5.7
) and
\mathcal{C}
the matrix in (
3.11
).
The operator
\breve{\mathcal{M}}
is symplectic whereas under the change of variables
\mathcal{C}

a real
Hamiltonian system in standard Darboux form (
5.3
) assumes the standard complex form (
3.18
), see the paragraph at page
3.1
.
Therefore
U
solves a system which is Hamiltonian in the complex sense.

Since
(\eta,\zeta)
solves (
5.29
), the complex variable
U
in (
5.36
) solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}U

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}&G(0)\\
-(g+\kappa D^{2}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}G(0)D^{-2})&\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{M}U

(5.41)

\displaystyle\ \ +\mathcal{M}^{-1}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}-B^{(1)}|\xi|-{\rm i}V^{(1)}\xi&0\\
-\kappa{\mathtt{f}}(\eta)\xi^{2}-[B^{(1)}]^{2}|\xi|&B^{(1)}|\xi|-{\rm i}V^{(1)}\xi\end{bmatrix}\right)}\mathcal{M}U

(5.42)

\displaystyle\ \ +\mathcal{M}^{-1}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}a^{(1)}_{0}&b_{-1}\\
c^{(1)}_{0}&d^{(1)}_{0}\end{bmatrix}\right)}\mathcal{M}U+R(U)U

(5.43)

where
R(U)
is a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

The operator in the right hand side of (
5.41
) is computed in
(
5.10
)-(
5.11
).
In order to compute the conjugated operators in (
5.42
)-(
5.43
), we apply the following transformation rule,
where we denote by
M:=M(D)
the Fourier multiplier in (
5.7
) (which
satisfies
M(D)=\overline{M}(D)
),

\displaystyle\footnotesize\mathcal{M}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{A}_{1}&\mathcal{A}_{2}\\
\mathcal{A}_{3}&\mathcal{A}_{4}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{M}

(5.44)

\displaystyle\footnotesize\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{defMM-1}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}M^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{1}M+M\mathcal{A}_{4}M^{-1}+{\rm i}M\mathcal{A}_{3}M-{\rm i}M^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{2}M^{-1}&M^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{1}M-M\mathcal{A}_{4}M^{-1}+{\rm i}M\mathcal{A}_{3}M+{\rm i}M^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{2}M^{-1}\\
M^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{1}M-M\mathcal{A}_{4}M^{-1}-{\rm i}M\mathcal{A}_{3}M-{\rm i}M^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{2}M^{-1}&M^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{1}M+M\mathcal{A}_{4}M^{-1}-{\rm i}M\mathcal{A}_{3}M+{\rm i}M^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{2}M^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

Using (
5.44
) and Proposition
2.14
we get that

\eqref{coniucomplessa1}=\frac{1}{2}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}a_{1}&b_{1}\\
\overline{b}_{1}^{\vee}&\overline{a}_{1}^{\vee}\end{bmatrix}\right)}+R(U)

(5.45)

where
a_{1},b_{1}
are the symbols

\displaystyle\footnotesize a_{1}

\displaystyle:=\footnotesize M^{-1}(\xi)\#_{\varrho}\big(-B^{(1)}|\xi|-{\rm i}V^{(1)}\xi\big)\#_{\varrho}M(\xi)+M(\xi)\#_{\varrho}\big(B^{(1)}|\xi|-{\rm i}V^{(1)}\xi\big)\#_{\varrho}M^{-1}(\xi)

\displaystyle\quad+{\rm i}M(\xi)\#_{\varrho}\big(-\kappa{\mathtt{f}}(\eta)\xi^{2}-[B^{(1)}]^{2}|\xi|\big)\#_{\varrho}M(\xi)

\displaystyle b_{1}

\displaystyle:=\footnotesize M^{-1}(\xi)\#_{\varrho}\big(-B^{(1)}|\xi|-{\rm i}V^{(1)}\xi\big)\#_{\varrho}M(\xi)-M(\xi)\#_{\varrho}\big(B^{(1)}|\xi|-{\rm i}V^{(1)}\xi\big)\#_{\varrho}M^{-1}(\xi)

\displaystyle\quad+{\rm i}M(\xi)\#_{\varrho}\big(-\kappa{\mathtt{f}}(\eta)\xi^{2}-[B^{(1)}]^{2}|\xi|\big)\#_{\varrho}M(\xi)

and
R(U)
is a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators in

\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Noting that

\omega(\xi)-\sqrt{\kappa}|\xi|^{\frac{3}{2}}\in\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,,\quad M(\xi)-\kappa^{-\frac{1}{4}}|\xi|^{-\frac{1}{4}}\in\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}^{-\frac{9}{4}}\,,\quad M^{-1}(\xi)-\kappa^{\frac{1}{4}}|\xi|^{\frac{1}{4}}\in\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}^{-\frac{7}{4}}\,,

(5.46)

so that
\kappa\xi^{2}M^{2}(\xi)-\omega(\xi)\in\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
,
we deduce, using also the remarks after Definition
2.13
, that

\displaystyle a_{1}

\displaystyle=-2{\rm i}V^{(1)}\xi-{\rm i}{\mathtt{f}}(\eta)\omega(\xi)-{\rm i}[B^{(1)}]^{2}|\xi|M^{2}(\xi)+a_{0}^{\prime}\quad\text{with}\quad a_{0}^{\prime}\in\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\,,

(5.47)

\displaystyle b_{1}

\displaystyle=-2B^{(1)}|\xi|-{\rm i}{\mathtt{f}}(\eta)\omega(\xi)-{\rm i}[B^{(1)}]^{2}|\xi|M^{2}(\xi)+b_{0}^{\prime}\quad\text{with}\quad b_{0}^{\prime}\in\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\,.

(5.48)

Finally, noting that

M^{-1}(\xi)\#_{\varrho}b_{-1}\#_{\varrho}M^{-1}(\xi)
belongs to
\Sigma\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
,
we deduce that

\eqref{coniucomplessa2}={{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A^{\prime}_{0})+R(U)

(5.49)

where
A^{\prime}_{0}
is a real-to-real matrix of symbols in
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
R(U)
is a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators in

\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

In conclusion, by (
5.10
)-(
5.11
), (
5.45
), (
5.47
), (
5.48
), (
5.49
),
computing the symbols at
(\eta,\zeta)=\mathcal{M}U
,
we deduce that system
(
5.41
)-(
5.43
) has the form
(
5.37
).
Note that the matrices of paradifferential operators
J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A_{\frac{3}{2}}\omega(\xi))
,

J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A_{1})
,
J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A_{\frac{1}{2}})
in (
5.38
), (
5.39
), (
5.40
) are linearly Hamiltonian according to (
3.22
), whereas
J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A^{(2)}_{0})
might not be. Thanks to Lemma
3.20
we
replace each homogeneous component of

A_{\frac{3}{2}}\omega(\xi)+A_{1}+A_{\frac{1}{2}}+A_{0}^{(2)}
with its symmetrized version, by adding another smoothing operator. Since the symbols with positive orders are unchanged we obtain a new operator
J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A^{(2)}_{0})
(that we denote in the same way) which
is linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
.
∎

6
Block-diagonalization and reduction to constant coefficients

In this section we perform several transformations
in order to
symmetrize and reduce system (
5.37
) to constant coefficients up to smoothing remainders.
In particular we will prove the following:

Proposition 6.1
.

(Reduction to constant coefficients up to smoothing operators)

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\varrho>3(N+1)
.
Then there exists
\underline{K}^{\prime}:=\underline{K}^{\prime}(\varrho)>0
such that for any
K\geq\underline{K}^{\prime}
there are
s_{0}>0
,
r>0
such that
for any solution
U\in B_{s_{0},\mathbb{R}}^{K}(I;r)
of (
5.37
), there exists a
real-to-real invertible matrix of spectrally localized maps
{\bf B}(U;t)
such that
{\bf B}(U;t)-{\rm Id}\in\Sigma\mathcal{S}^{\frac{3}{2}(N+1)}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime}-1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and the following holds true:

(i)

Boundedness:

{\bf B}(U;t)
and
{\bf B}(U;t)^{-1}

are non–homogeneous maps in
\mathcal{S}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime}-1,0}^{0}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, cfr. (
2.72
) with
m=N=0
.

(ii)

Linear symplecticity:
The map
{\bf B}(U;t)
is linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
, according to Definition
3.7
.

(iii)

Conjugation:
If
U
solves (
5.37
) then
W:={\bf B}(U;t)U
solves

\partial_{t}W={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}}(U;t,\xi)\right)}W+R(U;t)W

(6.1)

(recall notation (
2.24
)) where

\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}}(U;t,\xi):=-\left[(1+\zeta(U))\omega(\xi)+\frac{\gamma}{2}\frac{{\mathtt{G}}(\xi)}{\xi}+\mathtt{V}(U;t)\xi+\mathtt{b}_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;t)|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}+\mathtt{b}_{0}(U;t,\xi)\right]

(6.2)

and

•

\omega(\xi)\in\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{0}
is the Fourier multiplier defined in (
5.9
);

•

\zeta(U)
is a real function in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,2}[r,N]
independent of
x
;

•

\mathtt{V}(U;t)
is a real function in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,1,2}[r,N]
independent of
x
;

•

\mathtt{b}_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;t)
is a real function in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,2,2}[r,N]
independent of
x
;

•

\mathtt{b}_{0}(U;t,\xi)
is a symbol in
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime},2}[r,N]
independent of
x
and its imaginary part
\mathrm{Im}\,\mathtt{b}_{0}(U;t,\xi)
is in
\Gamma^{0}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime},N+1}[r]
;

•

R(U;t)
is a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+3(N+1)}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime},1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Remark 6.2
.

The symbol
\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}}(U;t,\xi)
in (
6.2
) is real valued except for the term
\mathtt{b}_{0}(U;t,\xi)
whose imaginary part has order 0 and homogeneity at least
N+1
. Hence system (
6.1
) fulfills energy estimates in
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
, of the type
(
8.15
) with
N=0
.

Remark 6.3
.

One can choose
\underline{K}^{\prime}(\varrho)\geq 3\varrho-8(N+1)+1
.

The rest of Section
6
is devoted to
the proof of Proposition
6.1
.
We shall use constantly the identities

\omega(\xi)=\sqrt{\kappa}|\xi|^{\frac{3}{2}}+\widetilde{\Gamma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{0},\quad\omega^{\prime}(\xi)=\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{\kappa}|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}\textup{ sign }\xi+\widetilde{\Gamma}^{-\frac{3}{2}}_{0}\ .

(6.3)

6.1
A complex good unknown of Alinhac

In this section we introduce
a complex version of the good unknown of Alinhac,
whose goal is to diagonalize the matrix of paradifferential
operators of order
1
in (
5.37
) and remove the paradifferential operators of order
\frac{1}{2}
.
The complex good unknown that we use coincides at principal order with

\mathcal{M}^{-1}\mathcal{G}_{A}\mathcal{M}
where
\mathcal{G}_{A}
is the classical good unknown of Alinhac in (
1.17
) and
\mathcal{M}
is the change of variables in (
5.6
).

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\varrho>0
. Then for any
K\in\mathbb{N}
there
are

s_{0}>0
,
r>0
such that for any solution
U\in B_{s_{0},\mathbb{R}}^{K}(I;r)
of (
5.37
), there exists a
real-to-real invertible matrix of spectrally localized maps
\mathcal{G}(U)
satisfying

\mathcal{G}(U)-{\rm Id}\in\Sigma\mathcal{S}^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and the following holds true:

(i)

Boundedness:

\mathcal{G}(U)
and its inverse
are non–homogeneous maps in
\mathcal{S}_{K,0,0}^{0}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

(ii)

Linear symplecticity:
The map
\mathcal{G}(U)
is linearly symplectic according to Definition
3.3
;

(iii)

Conjugation:

If
U
solves (
5.37
) then
V_{0}:=\mathcal{G}(U)U
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V_{0}

\displaystyle=J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{\frac{3}{2}}(U;x)\omega(\xi)\right)}V_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}V_{0}

(6.4)

\displaystyle+{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}V^{(2)}(U;x)\xi\right)}V_{0}+J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A^{(3)}_{0}(U;t,x,\xi)\right)}V_{0}+R(U)V_{0}

where

•

the matrix of real functions
A_{\frac{3}{2}}(U;x)\in\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,0}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and the real function
V^{(2)}(U;x)\in\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
are defined in Lemma
5.5
;

•

\omega(\xi)\in\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{0}
is the symbol defined in (
5.9
);

•

The matrix of symbols
A^{(3)}_{0}(U;t,x,\xi)
belongs to
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and the operator
J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A^{(3)}_{0})
is linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
according to Definition
3.6
;

•

R(U)
is a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

We define
\mathcal{G}(U)
to be the real-to-real map

\mathcal{G}(U):={\rm Id}-\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
-1&-1\end{pmatrix}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(B^{(2)}(U;x)M^{2}(\xi)\right)}

(6.5)

where
B^{(2)}(U;x)
is the function in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]

defined in (
5.39
) and

M(\xi)\in\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}^{-\frac{1}{4}}
is the symbol of
the Fourier multiplier
M(D)
defined in (
5.7
).
Its inverse and transpose are given by

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(U)^{-1}={\rm Id}+\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
-1&-1\end{pmatrix}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(B^{(2)}(U;x)M^{2}(\xi)\right)}\,,

(6.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(U)^{\top}={\rm Id}-\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(B^{(2)}(U;x)M^{2}(\xi)\right)}\,.

By the fourth bullet below Definition
2.16
the
matrices of paradifferential operators

\mathcal{G}(U)^{\pm 1}-{\rm Id}
belong
to
\Sigma\mathcal{S}_{K,0,1}^{0}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
item
(i)
follows. Also
(ii)
follows by a direct computation using the explicit expressions
in (
6.5
) and (
6.6
).

Let us prove item
(iii)
.
Since
U
solves (
5.37
) the variable
V_{0}:=\mathcal{G}(U)U
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{G}(U)\left[J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{\frac{3}{2}}\omega(\xi)+A_{1}+A_{\frac{1}{2}}+A_{0}^{(2)}\right)}+\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}\right]\mathcal{G}(U)^{-1}V_{0}+(\partial_{t}\mathcal{G}(U))\mathcal{G}(U)^{-1}V_{0}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathcal{G}(U)R(U)\mathcal{G}(U)^{-1}V_{0}\,.

(6.7)

We now expand each of the above operators.
By (
6.5
), the form of
J_{c}
in (
1.22
),
(
6.6
),
the symbolic calculus Proposition
2.14
,
writing

J_{c}A_{\frac{3}{2}}\omega(\xi)=-{\rm i}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\omega(\xi)-{\rm i}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
-1&-1\end{pmatrix}f(U)\omega(\xi)
(see (
5.38
)),
and since

\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
-1&-1\end{pmatrix}^{2}=0
, after a lengthy computation we obtain that
the first term in (
6.7
) is

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(U)J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{\frac{3}{2}}\,\omega(\xi)\right)}\mathcal{G}^{-1}(U)=J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{\frac{3}{2}}\,\omega(\xi)\right)}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\omega(\xi)\#_{\varrho}B^{(2)}M^{2}(\xi)-B^{(2)}M^{2}(\xi)\#_{\varrho}\omega(\xi)&\omega(\xi)\#_{\varrho}B^{(2)}M^{2}(\xi)+B^{(2)}M^{2}(\xi)\#_{\varrho}\omega(\xi)\\
\omega(\xi)\#_{\varrho}B^{(2)}M^{2}(\xi)+B^{(2)}M^{2}(\xi)\#_{\varrho}\omega(\xi)&\omega(\xi)\#_{\varrho}B^{(2)}M^{2}(\xi)-B^{(2)}M^{2}(\xi)\#_{\varrho}\omega(\xi)\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
-1&-1\end{pmatrix}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(B^{(2)}M^{2}(\xi)\#_{\varrho}\omega(\xi)\#_{\varrho}B^{(2)}M^{2}(\xi)\right)}+R(U)

\displaystyle=J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{\frac{3}{2}}\,\omega(\xi)\right)}+{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}0&\,B^{(2)}|\xi|\\
\ \,B^{(2)}|\xi|&0\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
-1&-1\end{pmatrix}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left([B^{(2)}]^{2}|\xi|M^{2}(\xi)\right)}+J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A_{0})+R(U)\,,

(6.8)

where
A_{0}
is a matrix of symbols in
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and

R(U)
is a matrix of smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. In the last passage to get (
6.8
) we also used that

M^{2}(\xi)\omega(\xi)={\mathtt{G}}(\xi)=|\xi|+\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}^{-\varrho}
, for any
\varrho\geq 0
,
cfr. (
5.8
), (
5.5
).

Next using the explicit form (
5.39
) of
A_{1}
we get, arguing similarly,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(U)

\displaystyle J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A_{1})\mathcal{G}(U)^{-1}

\displaystyle=J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A_{1})+{\rm i}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
-1&-1\end{pmatrix}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left([B^{(2)}]^{2}|\xi|M^{2}(\xi)\right)}+J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{0}^{\prime}\right)}+R(U)

(6.9)

where
A_{0}^{\prime}
is a matrix of symbols in

\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

and
R(U)
is a matrix of smoothing operators in

\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Moreover, using the form (
5.40
) of
A_{\frac{1}{2}}
we get

\mathcal{G}(U)J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{\frac{1}{2}}\right)}\mathcal{G}(U)^{-1}=J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{\frac{1}{2}}\right)}=-\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
-1&-1\end{pmatrix}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left([B^{(2)}]^{2}|\xi|M^{2}(\xi)\right)}\,.

(6.10)

Then, since
A_{0}^{(2)}
is a matrix of symbols of order zero, by Proposition
2.14
we have

\mathcal{G}(U)\left[J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{0}^{(2)}\right)}+\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}\right]\,\mathcal{G}(U)^{-1}=\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}+J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A_{0}^{\prime\prime})+R(U)\,,

(6.11)

for a matrix of symbols
A_{0}^{\prime\prime}
in

\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and smoothing operators
R(U)
in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Next by (
6.5
)-(
6.6
)

(\partial_{t}\mathcal{G}(U))\mathcal{G}(U)^{-1}=J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)}+R(U)\,,\quad A_{-\frac{1}{2}}:=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\partial_{t}B^{(2)}M^{2}(\xi)\right)

(6.12)

where, in view of the last bullets at the end of Section
2.1
and Proposition
2.15
-
(iv)
,
A_{-\frac{1}{2}}
is a matrix of symbols in
\Sigma\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{K,1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

and
R(U)
is a matrix of smoothing operator in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Finally by Proposition
2.19
we have that

\mathcal{G}(U)R(U)\mathcal{G}(U)^{-1}

is a matrix of smoothing operators in

\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, being
\mathcal{G}(U)
a spectrally localized map.

Note that, using the expression (
5.39
), the sum of the terms of order 1 which are in
(
6.8
) and (
6.9
) is

J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{1}\right)}+{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}0&\,B^{(2)}|\xi|\\
\ \,B^{(2)}|\xi|&0\end{bmatrix}\right)={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}V^{(2)}(U;x)\xi\right)}\,.

(6.13)

Note also that the sum of terms which are of order
\frac{1}{2}
in
(
6.8
), (
6.9
) and (
6.10
) equals zero.

In conclusion, by (
6.8
), (
6.9
) (
6.10
),
(
6.11
), (
6.12
) and (
6.13
) we obtain that system (
6.7
) has the form (
6.4
)
with
A^{(3)}_{0}:=A_{0}+A_{0}^{\prime}+A_{0}^{\prime\prime}+A_{-\frac{1}{2}}
in

\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Note that the paradifferential operators of positive order in (
6.4
) are linearly Hamiltonian, whereas
J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}{(A_{0}^{(3)})}
might not be.
However the operator in the first line of (
6.7
)
is a spectrally localized map which is linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
by Lemma
3.9
, with a paradifferential structure as in
(
3.66
).
Then by Lemma
3.21

we
replace each homogeneous component of

A_{\frac{3}{2}}\omega(\xi)+\begin{bmatrix}0&-V^{(2)}\xi\\
V^{(2)}\xi&0\end{bmatrix}+A_{0}^{(3)}
with its symmetrized version, by adding another smoothing operator. Since the symbols with positive orders are unchanged we obtain a new operator
J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A^{(3)}_{0})
(that we denote in the same way) which
is linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
.
∎

6.2
Block-Diagonalization at highest order

In this section we diagonalize the operator

J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\big(A_{\frac{3}{2}}(U;x)\omega(\xi)\big)
in (
6.4
)
where
A_{\frac{3}{2}}
is the matrix defined in (
5.38
).
Note that the eigenvalues of the matrix

J_{c}A_{\frac{3}{2}}(U;x)={\rm i}\begin{bmatrix}-(1+f(U;x))&-f(U;x)\\
f(U;x)&1+f(U;x)\end{bmatrix}\,,

(6.14)

where
f(U;x)
is the real function defined in (
5.38
), are

\pm{\rm i}\lambda(U;x)
with

\lambda(U;x):=\sqrt{(1+f(U;x))^{2}-f(U;x)^{2}}=\sqrt{1+2f(U;x)}\,.

(6.15)

Since the function
f(U;x)
is in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,2}[r,N]
,
for any
U\in B_{s_{0},\mathbb{R}}^{K}(I;r)
with
r>0
small enough it results that

|f(U;x)|\leq\frac{1}{4}
,
the function
\lambda(U;x)-1

belongs to
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,2}[r,N]
and

\lambda(U;x)\geq\tfrac{\sqrt{2}}{2}>0\,,\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{T}\,.

Actually the function
\lambda(U;x)
is real valued also for not small
U
, see
Remark
6.6
.

A matrix which diagonalizes (
6.14
) is

\displaystyle F(U;x):=\begin{pmatrix}h(U;x)&g(U;x)\\
g(U;x)&h(U;x)\end{pmatrix}

(6.16)

\displaystyle h:={\frac{1+f+\lambda}{\sqrt{(1+f+\lambda)^{2}-f^{2}}}}\,,\quad g:={\frac{-f}{\sqrt{(1+f+\lambda)^{2}-f^{2}}}}\,.

Note that
F(U;x)
is well defined since

(1+f+\lambda)^{2}-f^{2}=(1+2f+\lambda)(1+\lambda)\geq\frac{1}{2}
.
Moreover the matrix
F(U;x)
is symplectic, i.e.

\det F=h^{2}-g^{2}=1\,.

(6.17)

The inverse of
F(U;x)
is the symplectic and symmetric matrix

F(U;x)^{-1}:=\begin{pmatrix}h(U;x)&-g(U;x)\\
-g(U;x)&h(U;x)\end{pmatrix}\,.

(6.18)

Moreover
F(U;x)-{\rm Id}
is a matrix of real functions in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and

F(U;x)^{-1}\,J_{c}A_{\frac{3}{2}}(U;x)F(U;x)=\begin{bmatrix}-{\rm i}\lambda(U;x)&0\\
0&{\rm i}\lambda(U;x)\end{bmatrix}\,,

(6.19)

which amounts to
(h^{2}+g^{2})\,(1+f)+2hgf=\lambda
and

2hg(1+f)+(h^{2}+g^{2})f=0
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\varrho>0
.
Then for any
K\in\mathbb{N}
there are
s_{0}>0
,
r>0
such that for any solution

U\in B_{s_{0},\mathbb{R}}^{K}(I;r)
of (
5.37
), there exists a real-to-real invertible matrix of spectrally localized maps
\Psi_{1}(U)
satisfying

\Psi_{1}(U)-{\rm Id}\in\Sigma\mathcal{S}^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and the following holds true:

(i)

Boundedness:

The operator
\Psi_{1}(U)
and its inverse
are non–homogeneous maps in
\mathcal{S}_{K,0,0}^{0}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
;

(ii)

Linear symplecticity:
The map
\Psi_{1}(U)
is linearly symplectic according to Definition
3.3
;

(iii)

Conjugation:
If
V_{0}
solves (
6.4
) then
V_{1}:=\Psi_{1}(U)V_{0}
solves the system

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V_{1}

\displaystyle={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}\lambda(U;x)\omega(\xi)-{\rm i}V^{(2)}(U;x)\xi\right)}V_{1}+\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}V_{1}

(6.20)

\displaystyle+J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A^{(4)}_{0}(U;t,x,\xi)\right)}V_{1}+R(U;t)V_{1}

where

•

the function
\lambda(U;x)\in\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,0}[r,N]
, defined in (
6.15
), fulfills
\lambda(U;x)-1\in\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,2}[r,N]
;

•

the Fourier multiplier
\omega(\xi)\in\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{0}
is defined in (
5.9
);

•

the real function
V^{(2)}(U;x)\in\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
is defined in Lemma
5.5
;

•

the matrix of symbols
A^{(4)}_{0}(U;t,x,\xi)
belongs to
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and the operator

J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A^{(4)}_{0})

is linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
;

•

R(U;t)
is a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

By Lemma 3.11 of
[
18
]
, there exists a real valued function
m(U;x)\in\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]

(actually

m(U;x):=-\log(h(U;x)+g(U;x))
)
such that the time 1 flow
\Psi_{1}(U):=\Psi^{\tau}(U)|_{\tau=1}
of

\begin{cases}\partial_{\tau}\Psi^{\tau}(U)=J_{c}\,{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(M(U;x)\right)}\Psi^{\tau}(U)\\
\Psi^{0}(U)={\rm Id}\,,\end{cases}\qquad M(U;x):=\begin{bmatrix}-{\rm i}m(U;x)&0\\
0&{\rm i}m(U;x)\end{bmatrix}\,,

fulfills

\Psi_{1}(U)={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(F^{-1}(U;x)\right)}+R(U)\,,\quad\Psi_{1}(U)^{-1}={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(F(U;x)\right)}+R^{\prime}(U)\,,

(6.21)

where the matrix of functions
F(U;x)
is defined in (
6.16
)
and
R(U),R^{\prime}(U)
are matrices of smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Since the operator
J_{c}\,{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(M(U;x)\right)}
is linearly Hamiltonian
according to Definition
3.2
,
Lemma
3.16
guarantees that
\Psi_{1}(U)
is invertible, linearly symplectic and
\Psi_{1}(U)^{\pm 1}-{\rm Id}
belong to
\Sigma\mathcal{S}^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Since
V_{0}
solves (
6.4
) then the variable
V_{1}=\Psi_{1}(U)V_{0}
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V_{1}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{1}(U)\,\Big[J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{\frac{3}{2}}\omega(\xi)\right)}+\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}+{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}V^{(2)}\xi\right)}+J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A^{(3)}_{0}\right)}\Big]\Psi_{1}(U)^{-1}\,V_{1}

\displaystyle\ \ +(\partial_{t}\Psi_{1}(U))\Psi_{1}(U)^{-1}\,V_{1}+\Psi_{1}(U)R(U)\Psi_{1}(U)^{-1}V_{1}\ .

(6.22)

Next we compute each term in (
6.22
). We begin with

J_{c}\,{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A_{\frac{3}{2}}\omega(\xi))
.
Using (
6.21
), Proposition
2.19
-
(i)
,
Proposition
2.15
-(
i
) and the explicit form of
A_{\frac{3}{2}}
in (
5.38
), one computes

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}(U)J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{\frac{3}{2}}\omega(\xi)\right)}\Psi_{1}(U)^{-1}

\displaystyle={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(F^{-1}\right)}J_{c}\,{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{\frac{3}{2}}\omega(\xi)\right)}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(F\right)}+R(U)

\displaystyle={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}a_{\frac{3}{2}}&b_{-\frac{1}{2}}\\
\overline{b_{-\frac{1}{2}}}^{\vee}&\overline{a_{\frac{3}{2}}}^{\vee}\end{bmatrix}\right)}+R(U)

(6.23)

where
R(U)
is a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
using (
6.14
), (
6.16
), (
6.18
), Proposition
2.14
,
we have

\displaystyle a_{\frac{3}{2}}:=-{\rm i}\big[h\#_{\varrho}(1+f)\omega\#_{\varrho}h+g\#_{\varrho}(1+f)\omega\#_{\varrho}g+h\#_{\varrho}f\omega\#_{\varrho}g+g\#_{\varrho}f\omega\#_{\varrho}h\big]

\displaystyle b_{-\frac{1}{2}}:=-{\rm i}\big[h\#_{\varrho}(1+f)\omega\#_{\varrho}g+g\#_{\varrho}(1+f)\omega\#_{\varrho}h+h\#_{\varrho}f\omega\#_{\varrho}h+g\#_{\varrho}f\omega\#_{\varrho}g\big]\,.

The real-to-real structure of the symbols in (
6.23
) follows also by the last bullet after Definition
2.13
.
By the symbolic calculus rule (
2.53
), the second and third bullets after Definition

2.13
and
(
6.19
)
one has

\displaystyle a_{\frac{3}{2}}=-{\rm i}\big[(h^{2}+g^{2})\,(1+f)+2hgf\big]\,\omega+a_{-\frac{1}{2}}=-{\rm i}\lambda\omega+\breve{a}_{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle b_{-\frac{1}{2}}=-{\rm i}\big[2hg(1+f)+(h^{2}+g^{2})f\big]\omega+\breve{b}_{-\frac{1}{2}}=\breve{b}_{-\frac{1}{2}}

with symbols
\breve{a}_{-\frac{1}{2}},\breve{b}_{-\frac{1}{2}}
in

\Sigma\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
.
Then we obtain

{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}a_{\frac{3}{2}}&b_{-\frac{1}{2}}\\
\overline{b_{-\frac{1}{2}}}^{\vee}&\overline{a_{\frac{3}{2}}}^{\vee}\end{bmatrix}\right)}={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}\lambda\omega\right)}+J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)}

(6.24)

where
A_{-\frac{1}{2}}
is a real-to-real
matrix of symbols in
\Sigma\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proceeding similarly one finds that

\Psi_{1}(U){{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}V^{(2)}\xi\right)}\Psi_{1}(U)^{-1}={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}a_{1}&b_{0}\\
\overline{b}_{0}^{\vee}&\overline{a}_{1}^{\vee}\end{bmatrix}\right)}+R(U)

(6.25)

where
R(U)
is a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and

\displaystyle a_{1}

\displaystyle:=h\#_{\varrho}(-{\rm i}V^{(2)}\xi)\#_{\varrho}h-g\#_{\varrho}(-{\rm i}V^{(2)}\xi)\#_{\varrho}g=(h^{2}-g^{2})(-{\rm i}V^{(2)}\xi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{detF=1}}}{{=}}-{\rm i}V^{(2)}\xi

(6.26)

\displaystyle b_{0}

\displaystyle:=h\#_{\varrho}(-{\rm i}V^{(2)}\xi)\#_{\varrho}g-g\#_{\varrho}(-{\rm i}V^{(2)}\xi)\#_{\varrho}h\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\,.

In addition, using (
6.21
),
the last bullets at the end of Section
2.1
, Proposition
2.15
-(
iv
)
we obtain that

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}(U)\left[\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}+J_{c}\,{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{0}^{(3)}\right)}\right]\Psi_{1}(U)^{-1}+(\partial_{t}\Psi_{1}(U))\Psi_{1}(U)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}+J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{0}^{\prime}\right)}+R(U;t)

(6.27)

where
A_{0}^{\prime}
is a real-to-real matrix of symbols in
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
R(U;t)
is
a matrix of real-to-real smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Finally, by Proposition
2.19
,

\Psi_{1}(U)R(U)\Psi_{1}(U)^{-1}

is a matrix of smoothing operators in

\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

In conclusion, by (
6.23
)-(
6.24
),
(
6.25
)-(
6.26
) and (
6.27
) we
deduce that system (
6.22
) has the form
(
6.20
)
with a matrix of symbols
A_{0}^{(4)}:=A_{-\frac{1}{2}}+J_{c}\begin{bmatrix}0&b_{0}\\
b_{0}&0\end{bmatrix}+A_{0}^{\prime}
in
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Note that the paradifferential operators of positive order in (
6.20
) are linearly Hamiltonian, whereas
J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}{(A_{0}^{(4)})}
might not be.
However the sum of the operators in the first line of (
6.22
) plus

(\partial_{t}\Psi_{1}(U))\Psi_{1}(U)^{-1}

is a spectrally localized map which is a linearly Hamiltonian operator
up to homogeneity
N
by Lemma
3.9
, with a paradifferential structure as in
(
3.66
).
Then by Lemma
3.21

we can replace each homogeneous component of
A_{0}^{(4)}
with its symmetrized version obtaining that
J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}{(A_{0}^{(4)})}
is linearly Hamiltonian
up to homogeneity
N
, by adding another smoothing operator.
∎

Remark 6.6
.

In view of Lemmata
5.5
and
5.1
the function
\lambda(U;x)
in (
6.15
) is equal to

(1+\eta_{x}^{2})^{-\frac{3}{4}}
. Therefore
the symbols
\pm{\rm i}\lambda(U;x)\omega(\xi)

are elliptic also for not small data and system
(
6.20
) is hyperbolic at order
\frac{3}{2}
.
This is the well known fact that, in presence of capillarity,
there is no need of the Taylor sign condition for the local well-posedness.

6.3
Reduction to constant coefficients of the highest order

In this section we perform a linearly symplectic change of variable which reduces the highest order paradifferential operator

\textup{Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}_{\tt vec}(-{\rm i}\lambda(U;x)\omega(\xi))
in (
6.20
) to constant coefficients.

Lemma 6.7

(
Reduction of the highest order).

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\varrho>2(N+1)
.
Then for any
K\in\mathbb{N}
there are
s_{0}>0
,
r>0
such that for
any solution
U\in B_{s_{0},\mathbb{R}}^{K}(I;r)
of (
5.37
), there exists a real-to-real invertible matrix of spectrally localized maps
\Psi_{2}(U)
satisfying
\Psi_{2}(U)-{\rm Id}\in\Sigma\mathcal{S}^{N+1}_{K,0,2}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and the following holds true:

(i)

Boundedness:
The linear map
\Psi_{2}(U)
and its inverse
are non–homogeneous maps in
\mathcal{S}_{K,0,0}^{0}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
;

(ii)

Linear symplecticity:
The map
\Psi_{2}(U)
is linearly symplectic according to Definition
3.3
;

(iii)

Conjugation:

If
V_{1}
solves (
6.20
) then
V_{2}:=\Psi_{2}(U)V_{1}
solves the system

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V_{2}

\displaystyle={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}(1+\zeta(U))\omega(\xi)-{\rm i}V^{(3)}(U;t,x)\xi\right)}V_{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}V_{2}

(6.28)

\displaystyle+J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A^{(5)}_{0}(U;t,x,\xi)\right)}V_{2}+R(U;t)V_{2}

where

•

\zeta(U)
is a
x
-independent function in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,2}[r,N]
and
\omega(\xi)
is defined in (
5.9
);

•

V^{(3)}(U;t,x)
is a real valued function in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,1,1}[r,N]
;

•

The matrix of symbols
A^{(5)}_{0}(U;t,x,\xi)
belongs to
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

and the paradifferential operator

J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A^{(5)}_{0})
is
linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
;

•

R(U;t)
is a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators
in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+2(N+1)}_{K,1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

We define the map
\Psi_{2}(U)
as the time 1 flow
\Psi_{2}(U):=\Psi^{\tau}(U)|_{\tau=1}
of
