\partial_{\tau}\Psi^{\tau}(U)=J_{c}\,{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(B(\tau,U;x,\xi)\right)}\Psi^{\tau}(U),\qquad\Psi^{0}(U)={\rm Id}\,,

where

B(\tau,U;x,\xi):=\begin{pmatrix}0&b(\tau,U;x,\xi)\\
b(\tau,U;x,-\xi)&0\end{pmatrix},\quad b(\tau,U;x,\xi):=\frac{\beta(U;x)}{1+\tau\beta_{x}(U;x)}\xi\,,

and the function
\beta(U;x)
in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,2}[r,N]
has to be determined.
As
\beta(U;x)
is real valued, the operator
J_{c}\,{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(B(\tau,U;\cdot)\right)}
is linearly Hamiltonian.
Thus Lemma
3.16
, applied with
m=1
, guarantees that

\Psi_{2}(U)
is a spectrally localized map in
\mathcal{S}^{0}_{K,0,0}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, it is linearly symplectic and
\Psi_{2}(U)^{\pm}-{\rm Id}
belongs to

\Sigma\mathcal{S}^{N+1}_{K,0,2}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Note that the diagonal operator

J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(B\right)}={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}b(\tau,U;x,\xi)\right)}{\rm Id}
is a multiple of the identity and
hence the flow
\Psi_{2}(U)
acts as a scalar operator.

Since
V_{1}
solves (
6.20
), then the variable
V_{2}=\Psi_{2}(U)V_{1}
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V_{2}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{2}(U)\left[{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}\lambda\omega(\xi)-{\rm i}V^{(2)}\xi\right)}+\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}+J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{0}^{(4)}\right)}\right]\Psi_{2}(U)^{-1}V_{2}

(6.29)

\displaystyle+(\partial_{t}\Psi_{2}(U))\circ\Psi_{2}(U)^{-1}V_{2}+\Psi_{2}(U)R(U;t)\Psi_{2}(U)^{-1}V_{2}\,.

We now compute each term in (
6.29
).
By Lemma 3.21 of
[
16
]
,
the diffeomorphism
\Phi_{U}:x\mapsto x+\beta(U;x)
of
\mathbb{T}

is invertible with inverse

\Phi_{U}^{-1}\colon y\mapsto y+\breve{\beta}(U;y)
and

\breve{\beta}(U;y)
belongs to
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,2}[r,N]
.
By Theorem 3.27 of
[
16
]

one has

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}(U)\,{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}\,\lambda(U;x)\omega(\xi)\right)}\,\Psi_{2}(U)^{-1}

(6.30)

\displaystyle={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\left.\left[-{\rm i}\,\lambda(U;y)\,\omega\Big(\xi\big(1+\breve{\beta}_{y}(U;y)\big)\Big)\right]\right|_{y=\Phi_{U}(x)}\right)}+J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)}+R(U)

with a diagonal matrix of symbols
A_{-\frac{1}{2}}
in

\Sigma\Gamma_{K,0,1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
,
and a diagonal matrix of smoothing operators

R(U)
in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+\frac{3}{2}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Note that

\omega\big(\xi(1+\breve{\beta}_{y}(U;y))\big)

is a symbol in
\Sigma\Gamma^{\frac{3}{2}}_{K,0,0}[r,N]
by Lemma 3.23 of
[
16
]
.

Now we choose
\breve{\beta}
in such a way that the principal symbol
in (
6.30
) is
x
-independent.
Since,
by (
5.46
),

\omega_{-\frac{1}{2}}(\xi):=\omega(\xi)-\sqrt{\kappa}|\xi|^{\frac{3}{2}}
is a Fourier multiplier in
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{0}
we get

\displaystyle\lambda(U;y)\omega\Big(\xi(1+\breve{\beta}_{y}(U;y))\Big)

\displaystyle=\lambda(U;y)\sqrt{\kappa}|\xi|^{\frac{3}{2}}\big|1+\breve{\beta}_{y}(U;y)\big|^{\frac{3}{2}}+\lambda(U;y)\omega_{-\frac{1}{2}}\big(\xi(1+\breve{\beta}_{y}(U;y))\big)

(6.31)

and we select
\breve{\beta}(U;\cdot)
so that

\lambda(U;y)\,\big|1+\breve{\beta}_{y}(U;y)\big|^{\frac{3}{2}}=1+\zeta(U)

(6.32)

with a
y
-independent function
\zeta(U)
. In order to fulfill (
6.32
) we define
the functions

\zeta(U):=\Big(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\lambda(U;y)^{-\frac{2}{3}}{\rm d}y\Big)^{-\frac{3}{2}}-1\,,\quad\breve{\beta}(U;y):=\partial_{y}^{-1}\left[\Big(\frac{1+\zeta(U)}{\lambda(U;y)}\Big)^{\frac{2}{3}}-1\right]

which belong to
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,2}[r,N]
.
By (
6.32
) and since
\omega(\xi)-\sqrt{\kappa}|\xi|^{\frac{3}{2}}\in\widetilde{\Gamma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{0}
,
the expression (
6.31
) becomes

\displaystyle\lambda(U;y)\omega\Big(\xi(1+\breve{\beta}_{y}(U;y))\Big)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\kappa}|\xi|^{\frac{3}{2}}\,(1+\zeta(U))+\lambda(U;y)\omega_{-\frac{1}{2}}\big(\xi(1+\breve{\beta}_{y}(U;y))\big)

\displaystyle=\omega(\xi)(1+\zeta(U))+a_{-\frac{1}{2}}

where
a_{-\frac{1}{2}}
is a real valued symbol in

\Sigma\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
.
Note that we used that

\omega_{-\frac{1}{2}}\big(\xi(1+\breve{\beta}_{y}(U;y))\big)\lambda(U;y)-\omega_{-\frac{1}{2}}(\xi)
is a symbol in
\Sigma\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
.
In conclusion (
6.30
) is

\Psi_{2}(U){{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}\lambda\omega(\xi)\right)}\Psi_{2}(U)^{-1}={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}(1+\zeta(U))\omega(\xi)\right)}+J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)}+R(U)

(6.33)

where
A_{-\frac{1}{2}}
is a diagonal matrix of symbols
in
\Sigma\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and

R(U)
is a diagonal matrix of smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+\frac{3}{2}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

We now compute the other terms in (
6.29
).
Again by Theorem 3.27 of
[
16
]
(and Lemma A.4 of
[
18
]
)

\Psi_{2}(U)\,{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}V^{(2)}\xi\right)}\,\Psi_{2}(U)^{-1}={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}\breve{V}(U;x)\xi\right)}+R(U)

(6.34)

where
\breve{V}(U;x)
is a real function in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
,
and
R(U)
is a diagonal matrix of smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+1}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. In addition,
again by Theorem 3.27 of
[
16
]

\Psi_{2}(U)\,\left[\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}+J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{0}^{(4)}\right)}\right]\,\Psi_{2}(U)^{-1}=\frac{\gamma}{2}G(0)\partial_{x}^{-1}+J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{0}\right)}+R(U)

(6.35)

where
A_{0}
is a real-to-real matrix of symbols in
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
R(U)\in\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,0,1}[r,N]
.
Moreover, by Lemma A.5 of
[
18
]

(\partial_{t}\Psi_{2}(U))\circ\Psi_{2}(U)^{-1}={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}g(U;t,x)\xi\right)}+R(U;t)

(6.36)

where
g(U;t,x)
is a real function in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,1,1}[r,N]
and
R(U;t)

is a matrix of real-to-real
smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+1}_{K,1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Finally
\Psi_{2}(U)R(U;t)\Psi_{2}(U)^{-1}
in (
6.29
) is a matrix of
real-to-real smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+2(N+1)}_{K,1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, by Proposition
2.19
.

In conclusion,
by
(
6.33
), (
6.34
), (
6.35
),
(
6.36
),
we
deduce that system (
6.29
) has the form
(
6.28
)
with

V^{(3)}:=\breve{V}+g

and

A_{0}^{(5)}:=A_{-\frac{1}{2}}+A_{0}
.
Note that the paradifferential operators of positive order in (
6.28
) are linearly Hamiltonian, whereas
J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}{(A_{0}^{(5)})}
might not be.
However the sum of the operators in the first line of (
6.29
) and
(\partial_{t}\Psi_{2}(U))\Psi_{2}(U)^{-1}

is a spectrally localized map which is linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
by Lemma
3.9
, with a paradifferential structure as in
(
3.66
).
Then by Lemma
3.21

we can replace each homogeneous component of
A_{0}^{(5)}
with its symmetrized version obtaining that
J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}{(A_{0}^{(5)})}
is linearly Hamiltonian
up to homogeneity
N
, by adding another smoothing operator.
∎

6.4
Block-Diagonalization up to smoothing operators

The goal of this section is to block-diagonalize system (
6.28
) up to smoothing remainders.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\varrho>2(N+1)
.
Then for any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
there is
K^{\prime}:=K^{\prime}(n)\geq 0
(one can choose
K^{\prime}=n
) such that for all
K\geq K^{\prime}+1
there are
s_{0}>0
,
r>0
such that for any solution

U\in B_{s_{0},\mathbb{R}}^{K}(I;r)
of (
5.37
), there exists a real-to-real invertible matrix of spectrally localized maps
\Phi_{n}(U)
satisfying
\Phi_{n}(U)-{\rm Id}\in\Sigma\mathcal{S}_{K,K^{\prime},1}^{0}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and the following holds true:

(i)

Boundedness:
Each
\Phi_{n}(U)
and its inverse
are non–homogeneous maps in
\mathcal{S}_{K,K^{\prime},0}^{0}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
;

(ii)

Linear symplecticity:
The map
\Phi_{n}(U)
is linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
according to Definition
3.7
;

(iii)

Conjugation:

If
V_{2}
solves (
6.28
) then
V_{n+2}:=\Phi_{n}(U)V_{2}
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V_{n+2}

\displaystyle={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}\left[(1+\zeta(U))\omega(\xi)+\frac{\gamma}{2}\frac{{\mathtt{G}}(\xi)}{\xi}+V^{(3)}(U;t,x)\xi+a_{0}^{(n)}(U;t,x,\xi)\right]\right)}V_{n+2}

(6.37)

\displaystyle+J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{-n}(U;t,x,\xi)\right)}V_{n+2}+R(U;t)V_{n+2}

where

•

the Fourier multiplier
\omega(\xi)
is defined in
(
5.9
), the
x
-independent real function

\zeta(U)\in\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,2}[r,N]
and
the real function
V^{(3)}(U;t,x)\in\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,1,1}[r,N]
are defined in Lemma
6.7
;

•

a_{0}^{(n)}(U;t,x,\xi)
is a symbol in
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},1}[r,N]
and
\mathrm{Im}\,a_{0}^{(n)}(U;t,x,\xi)
belongs to
\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[r]
;

•

The matrix of symbols
A_{-n}(U;t,x,\xi)

belongs to

\Sigma\Gamma^{-n}_{K,K^{\prime}+1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

and

J_{c}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A_{-n})
is a linearly Hamiltonian operator up to homogeneity
N
;

•

R(U;t)
is a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators
in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+2(N+1)}_{K,K^{\prime}+1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

We prove the thesis by induction on
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Case
n=0
.
It follows by (
6.28
) with

a_{0}^{(0)}:=0
,
A_{0}:=A_{0}^{(5)}
,
K^{\prime}=0
and

\Phi_{0}(U):={\rm Id}
.

Case
n\leadsto n+1
.

Suppose (
6.37
) holds. We perform a transformation to push the off diagonal part of
J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{-n}\right)}
to lower order.
We write the real-to-real matrix
J_{c}A_{-n}
as

J_{c}A_{-n}=J_{c}\begin{pmatrix}-{\rm i}\overline{b}_{-n}^{\vee}&-\overline{a}_{-n}^{\vee}\\
-a_{-n}&{\rm i}b_{-n}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}{\rm i}a_{-n}&b_{-n}\\
\overline{b}_{-n}^{\vee}&-{\rm i}\overline{a}_{-n}^{\vee}\end{bmatrix},\qquad a_{-n},b_{-n}\in\Sigma\Gamma^{-n}_{K,K^{\prime}+1,1}[r,N]

(6.38)

where, since
J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{-n}\right)}
is linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
, by (
3.31
) we have

\mathrm{Im}\,a_{-n}\in\Gamma^{-n}_{K,K^{\prime}+1,N+1}[r]\,,\qquad b_{-n}-b_{-n}^{\vee}\in\Gamma^{-n}_{K,K^{\prime}+1,N+1}[r]\,.

(6.39)

Denote by
\Phi_{F^{(n)}}(U):=\Phi^{\tau}_{F^{(n)}}(U;t)|_{\tau=1}
the time
1
-flow
of

\begin{cases}\partial_{\tau}\Phi_{F^{(n)}}^{\tau}(U)={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(F^{(n)}(U)\right)}\Phi_{F^{(n)}}^{\tau}(U)\\
\Phi_{F^{(n)}}^{0}(U)={\rm Id}\,,\end{cases}\qquad F^{(n)}(U):=\begin{bmatrix}0&f_{-n-\frac{3}{2}}\\
\overline{f}_{-n-\frac{3}{2}}^{\vee}&0\end{bmatrix}\ ,

(6.40)

where, see (
6.38
),

\qquad f_{-n-\frac{3}{2}}(U;t,x,\xi):=-\frac{b_{-n}(U;t,x,\xi)}{2{\rm i}\omega(\xi)(1+\zeta(U))}\in\Sigma\Gamma^{-n-\frac{3}{2}}_{K,K^{\prime}+1,1}[r,N]\,.

(6.41)

By (
6.39
), (
6.41
), the symbol
f_{-n-\frac{3}{2}}-f_{-n-\frac{3}{2}}^{\vee}
is in
\Gamma^{-n-\frac{3}{2}}_{K,K^{\prime}+1,N+1}[r]

and therefore

{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(F^{(n)}(U)\right)}
is a linearly Hamiltonian operator up to homogeneity
N
.
Lemma

3.16
implies that
\Phi_{F^{(n)}}(U)

is invertible,
linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
and

\Phi_{F^{(n)}}(U)^{\pm 1}-{\rm Id}
belong to
\Sigma\mathcal{S}_{K,K^{\prime}+1,1}^{0}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

If
V_{n+2}
fulfills (
6.37
), the variable
V_{n+3}:=\Phi_{F^{(n)}}(U)V_{n+2}
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V_{n+3}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{F^{(n)}}(U)\,\left[{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\mathtt{d}}^{(n)}_{\frac{3}{2}}\right)}+J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{-n}\right)}\right]\Phi_{F^{(n)}}(U)^{-1}V_{n+3}

(6.42)

\displaystyle\ \ +(\partial_{t}\Phi_{F^{(n)}}(U))\Phi_{F^{(n)}}(U)^{-1}V_{n+3}+\Phi_{F^{(n)}}(U)R(U;t)\Phi_{F^{(n)}}(U)^{-1}V_{n+3}\,

(6.43)

where, to shorten notation, we denoted

{\mathtt{d}}^{(n)}_{\frac{3}{2}}(U;t,x,\xi):=-{\rm i}\Big((1+\zeta(U))\omega(\xi)+\frac{\gamma}{2}\frac{{\mathtt{G}}(\xi)}{\xi}+V^{(3)}(U;t,x)\xi+a_{0}^{(n)}(U;t,x,\xi)\Big)\ .

(6.44)

We first expand (
6.42
).
The Lie expansion formula (see e.g. Lemma A.1 of
[
18
]
)
says that for any operator
M(U)
, setting

{\bf\Phi}(U):=\Phi_{F^{(n)}}(U)
,

{\bf F}:={{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(F^{(n)}(U)\right)}

and
{\rm Ad}_{{\bf F}}[M]:=[{\bf F},M]
, one has

{\bf\Phi}(U)M(U)({\bf\Phi}(U))^{-1}=M+\sum_{q=1}^{L}\frac{1}{q!}{\rm Ad}_{{\bf F}}^{q}[M]+\frac{1}{L!}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\tau)^{L}{\bf\Phi}^{\tau}(U){\rm Ad}_{{\bf F}}^{L+1}[M]({\bf\Phi}^{\tau}(U))^{-1}{\rm d}\tau\,.

(6.45)

We apply this formula with
L:=L(\varrho)\geq(\varrho-n)/(n+\frac{3}{2})
(in this way the integral remainder above is a smoothing operator in
\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,K^{\prime}+1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
), and by
symbolic calculus in Proposition
2.14
,
(
6.38
), (
6.40
), (
6.41
) and formula (
6.3
)
we find

\displaystyle\eqref{pheq0o}={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\mathtt{d}}^{(n)}_{\frac{3}{2}}+{\rm i}a_{-n}\right)}V_{n+3}

\displaystyle\ \ \ +{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}0&\big[(\overline{{\mathtt{d}}}^{(n)}_{\frac{3}{2}})^{\vee}-{\mathtt{d}}^{(n)}_{\frac{3}{2}}\big]f_{-n-\frac{3}{2}}+b_{-n}\\
\big[{\mathtt{d}}^{(n)}_{\frac{3}{2}}-(\overline{{\mathtt{d}}}^{(n)}_{\frac{3}{2}})^{\vee}\big]\overline{f}_{-n-\frac{3}{2}}^{\vee}+\overline{b_{-n}}^{\vee}&0\end{bmatrix}\right)}V_{n+3}

\displaystyle\ \ \ +J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A^{\prime}_{-(n+1)}\right)}V_{n+3}+R(U;t)\,V_{n+3}

(6.46)

with a real-to-real matrix of symbols

A_{-(n+1)}^{\prime}
in
\Sigma\Gamma^{-n-1}_{K,K^{\prime}+1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and a matrix of smoothing operators
R(U;t)
in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,K^{\prime}+1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
(we also used Lemma

3.16
and Proposition
2.19
to estimate the Taylor remainder in the Lie expansion formula).
By (
6.44
),
(
6.41
)
and since
a_{0}^{(n)}
is of order 0,
we have

\big[(\overline{{\mathtt{d}}}^{(n)}_{\frac{3}{2}})^{\vee}-{\mathtt{d}}^{(n)}_{\frac{3}{2}}\big]f_{-n-\frac{3}{2}}+b_{-n}=:b_{-n-\frac{3}{2}}\in\Sigma\Gamma^{-n-\frac{3}{2}}_{K,K^{\prime}+1,1}[r,N]\,.

(6.47)

We pass to the first term in (
6.43
).
Using the Lie expansion (cfr. Lemma A.1 of
[
18
]
)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}{\bf\Phi}(U))({\bf\Phi}(U))^{-1}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}{\bf F}+\sum_{q=2}^{L}\frac{1}{q!}{\rm Ad}_{{\bf F}}^{q-1}[\partial_{t}{\bf F}]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{L!}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\tau)^{L}{\bf\Phi}^{\tau}(U){\rm Ad}_{{\bf F}}^{L}[\partial_{t}{\bf F}]({\bf\Phi}^{\tau}(U))^{-1}{\rm d}\tau\,

(6.48)

with the same
L
as above, the last bullets at the end of Section
2.1
, Proposition
2.15
-
(iv)
and (
6.41
) we get

(\partial_{t}\Phi_{F^{(n)}}(U))\Phi_{F^{(n)}}(U)^{-1}=J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(Q(U;t,x,\xi)\right)}+R(U;t)

(6.49)

with a real-to-real matrix of symbols

Q(U;t,x,\xi)
in
\Sigma\Gamma^{-n-\frac{3}{2}}_{K,K^{\prime}+2,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and a matrix of smoothing operators
R(U;t)

in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,K^{\prime}+2,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Thanks to
(i)
and
(ii)
of Proposition
2.19
, the operator
\Phi_{F^{(n)}}(U)R(U;t)\Phi_{F^{(n)}}(U)^{-1}
in (
6.43
) is a smoothing operator in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+2(N+1)}_{K,K^{\prime}+1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
as well as
R(U;t)
. In conclusion,
by (
6.46
), (
6.47
), (
6.49
), the system in
(
6.42
)–(
6.43
) has the form

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V_{n+3}

\displaystyle={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\mathtt{d}}^{(n)}_{\frac{3}{2}}+{\rm i}a_{-n}\right)}V_{n+3}+J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{-n-1}\right)}V_{n+3}+R(U;t)V_{n+3}

(6.50)

where
the matrix of symbols
A_{-n-1}
in
\Sigma\Gamma^{-n-1}_{K,K^{\prime}+2,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
is given by

A_{-n-1}:=A^{\prime}_{-(n+1)}+J_{c}\begin{bmatrix}0&b_{-n-\frac{3}{2}}\\
\overline{b}_{-n-\frac{3}{2}}^{\vee}&0\end{bmatrix}+Q
and
R(U;t)
a matrix of smoothing operators in

\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+2(N+1)}_{K,K^{\prime}+2,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
By Lemma
3.21
,
we replace each homogeneous component of
A_{-n-1}
with its symmetrized version obtaining that
J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{-n-1}\right)}
is linearly Hamiltonian
up to homogeneity
N
, by adding another smoothing operator.

In conclusion, by (
6.44
), system (
6.50
) has the form (
6.37
) at step
n+1

with
a_{0}^{(n+1)}:=a_{0}^{(n)}-a_{-n}
and
K^{\prime}(n+1):=K^{\prime}(n)+1
. Note that the imaginary part

\mathrm{Im}\,a_{0}^{(n+1)}

is in
\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[r]
by the inductive assumption and (
6.39
).

Finally we define
\Phi_{n+1}(U):=\Phi_{F^{(n)}}(U)\circ\Phi_{n}(U)
. The claimed properties of
\Phi_{n}(U)
follow by the analogous ones of
each
\Phi_{F^{(n)}}(U)
and Proposition
2.19
.
∎

For any
\varrho>2(N+1)
we now choose in Lemma
6.8
a number of iterative steps
n:=n_{1}(\varrho)
such that
n_{1}\geq\varrho-2(N+1)
,
so that we can incorporate
J_{c}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(A_{-n}(U)\right)}
in the smoothing remainder
R(U)
in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+2(N+1)}_{K,K^{\prime}+1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Thus, denoting
Z:=V_{n+2}
, we write system (
6.37
)
as

\!\!\!\partial_{t}Z={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}\left[(1+\zeta(U))\omega(\xi)+\frac{\gamma}{2}\frac{{\mathtt{G}}(\xi)}{\xi}+V^{(3)}(U;t,x)\xi+a_{0}(U;t,x,\xi)\right]\right)}Z+R(U;t)Z

(6.51)

where the symbol
a_{0}(U;t,x,\xi):=a_{0}^{(n_{1})}(U;t,x,\xi)
is given in (
6.37
) with
n=n_{1}(\varrho)
.
Thus
a_{0}(U;t,x,\xi)
belongs to
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},1}[r,N]
and its imaginary part

\mathrm{Im}\,a_{0}(U;t,x,\xi)
in
\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[r]
with
K^{\prime}=n_{1}(\varrho)
.

6.5
Reduction to constant coefficients up to smoothing operators

The goal of this section is to reduce the symbol in the paradifferential operator
in (
6.51
) to an
x
-independent one, up to smoothing operators.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\varrho>3(N+1)
.
Then for any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
there is
K^{\prime\prime}:=K^{\prime\prime}(\varrho,n)>0
(one can choose
K^{\prime\prime}=K^{\prime}(n_{1}(\varrho))+n
) such that for all
K\geq K^{\prime\prime}+1
there are
s_{0}>0
,
r>0
such that for any solution

U\in B_{s_{0},\mathbb{R}}^{K}(I;r)
of (
5.37
), there exists a real-to-real invertible matrix of spectrally localized maps
\mathcal{F}_{n}(U)
satisfying
\mathcal{F}_{n}(U)-{\rm Id}\in\Sigma\mathcal{S}^{(N+1)/2}_{K,K^{\prime\prime},1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and the following holds true:

(i)

Boundedness:
Each
\mathcal{F}_{n}(U)
and its inverse
are non–homogeneous maps in
\mathcal{S}_{K,K^{\prime\prime},0}^{0}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
;

(ii)

Linear symplecticity:
The map
\mathcal{F}_{n}(U)
is linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
according to Definition
3.7
.

(iii)

Conjugation:
If
Z
solves (
6.51
) then

Z_{n}:=\mathcal{F}_{n}(U)Z
solves

\partial_{t}Z_{n}={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\,\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}}^{(n)}(U;t,\xi)+{\rm i}a_{-\frac{n}{2}}(U;t,x,\xi)\right)}Z_{n}+R(U;t)Z_{n}

(6.52)

with the
x
–independent symbol

\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}}^{(n)}(U;t,\xi):=-\left[(1+\zeta(U))\omega(\xi)+\frac{\gamma}{2}\frac{{\mathtt{G}}(\xi)}{\xi}+\mathtt{V}(U;t)\xi+\mathtt{b}_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;t)|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}\right]+\mathtt{b}_{0}^{(n)}(U;t,\xi)

(6.53)

where

•

the
x
–independent function
\zeta(U)\in\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,2}[r,N]
is defined in Lemma
6.7
and
\omega(\xi)

in (
5.9
);

•

the function
\mathtt{V}(U;t)\in\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,1,2}[r,N]
is

x
-independent;

•

the function
\mathtt{b}_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;t)\in\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,2,2}[r,N]
is

x
-independent;

•

the symbol
\mathtt{b}_{0}^{(n)}(U;t,\xi)\in\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime\prime},2}[r,N]

is
x
–independent and its imaginary part
\mathrm{Im}\,\mathtt{b}_{0}^{(n)}(U;t,\xi)
is in
\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime\prime},N+1}[r]
;

•

the symbol
a_{-\frac{n}{2}}(U;t,x,\xi)
belongs to
\Sigma\Gamma^{-\frac{n}{2}}_{K,K^{\prime\prime}+1,1}[r,N]
and its imaginary part
\mathrm{Im}\,a_{-\frac{n}{2}}(U;t,x,\xi)
is in
\Gamma^{-\frac{n}{2}}_{K,K^{\prime\prime}+1,N+1}[r]
;

•

R(U;t)
is a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators in

\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+3(N+1)}_{K,K^{\prime\prime}+1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

We transform the equation (
6.51
) for the variable
Z
.

Case
n=0
.

Reduction to constant coefficients of order 1.

We first reduce to constant coefficients the transport term of order
1
in (
6.51
).
Let

\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}(U):=\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}^{\tau}(U;t)|_{\tau=1}
be
the time
1
-flow
of

\partial_{\tau}\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}^{\tau}(U)={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;t,x)|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)}\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}^{\tau}(U),\qquad\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}^{0}(U)={\rm Id}\,,

where
\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}
is the
real function
in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,1,1}[r,N]
defined by

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;t,x):=\frac{2}{3\sqrt{\kappa}(1+\zeta(U))}\,\partial_{x}^{-1}\left[\mathtt{V}(U;t)-V^{(3)}(U;t,x)\right]\,

(6.54)

\displaystyle\mathtt{V}(U;t):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}V^{(3)}(U;t,x)\,{\rm d}x\,.

Note that the real
x
-independent function

\mathtt{V}(U;t)
is in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,1,2}[r,N]
thanks to Remark
2.2

(it could be also directly verified that the linear component in
U
of the space average of
V^{(3)}
vanishes).

By (
3.22
)
the operator
{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)}

is linearly Hamiltonian.
By Lemma

3.16
, the flow
\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}(U)
is a diagonal matrix of
spectrally localized maps in

\mathcal{S}^{0}_{K,1,0}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
with its inverse,
it is linearly symplectic and

\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}(U)^{\pm 1}-{\rm Id}
belong to
\Sigma\mathcal{S}_{K,1,1}^{(N+1)/2}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

If
Z
solves equation (
6.51
), then the variable
\breve{Z}:=\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}(U)Z
satisfies

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\breve{Z}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}(U)\,{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}\left[(1+\zeta(U))\omega(\xi)+\frac{\gamma}{2}\frac{{\mathtt{G}}(\xi)}{\xi}+V^{(3)}\xi+a_{0}\right]\right)}\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}(U)^{-1}\,\breve{Z}

\displaystyle+(\partial_{t}\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}(U))\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}(U)^{-1}\breve{Z}+\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}(U)R(U;t)\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}(U)^{-1}\breve{Z}\,.

Using the Lie expansions in (
6.45
), (
6.4
) with

{\bf\Phi}:=\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}
,
{\bf F}:={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)}
,

L:=L(\varrho)\geq 2(\varrho+1)
(so the integral remainders in the Lie expansions are smoothing operators in
\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,K^{\prime},1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
), Proposition
2.13
and (
6.3
)
we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\breve{Z}={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}\left[(1+\zeta(U))\omega(\xi)+\frac{\gamma}{2}\frac{{\mathtt{G}}(\xi)}{\xi}\right]\right)}\,\breve{Z}

\displaystyle+{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}\left[V^{(3)}+\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{\kappa}(\beta_{\frac{1}{2}})_{x}(1+\zeta(U))\right]\xi\right)}\,\breve{Z}

(6.55)

\displaystyle+{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}b_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;t,x)|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}-{\rm i}a_{0}^{(1)}(U;t,x,\xi)\right)}\breve{Z}+R^{\prime}(U;t)\breve{Z}+\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}(U)R(U;t)\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}(U)^{-1}\breve{Z}

where
R^{\prime}(U;t)
belongs to
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,K^{\prime},1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and

b_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;t,x):=-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}V^{(3)}_{x}+(\beta_{\frac{1}{2}})_{x}V^{(3)}-\frac{3}{4}\sqrt{\kappa}(1+\zeta(U))\left(\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}(\beta_{\frac{1}{2}})_{xx}+(\beta_{\frac{1}{2}})_{x}^{2}\right)+\partial_{t}\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}

(6.56)

is a real valued function in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,2,1}[r,N]

(use also the last bullets at the end of Section
2.1
and Proposition
2.15
-
(iv)
),
and we collect in
a_{0}^{(1)}(U;t,x,\xi)
all the symbols in
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},1}[r,N]
. Finally
by Proposition
2.19
we deduce that

\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}(U)R(U;t)\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}(U)^{-1}
is
in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+3(N+1)}_{K,K^{\prime},1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
By (
6.54
) the first order term in (
6.55
) is constant coefficient, namely

V^{(3)}(U;t,x)+\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{\kappa}(\beta_{\frac{1}{2}})_{x}(U;t,x)(1+\zeta(U))=\mathtt{V}(U;t)\,,

and (
6.55
) reduces to

\partial_{t}\breve{Z}={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}\left[(1+\zeta(U))\omega(\xi)+\frac{\gamma}{2}\frac{{\mathtt{G}}(\xi)}{\xi}+\mathtt{V}(U;t)\xi+b_{\frac{1}{2}}|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}+a_{0}^{(1)}\right]\right)}\breve{Z}+R(U;t)\breve{Z}\,.

(6.57)

The paradifferential operators of positive order in (
6.57
) are linearly Hamiltonian, whereas
\textup{Op}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm BW}}_{\tt vec}(-{\rm i}a_{0}^{(1)})
might not be.
By the usual argument,
we replace each homogeneous component of
a_{0}^{(1)}
with its symmetrized version obtaining that
\textup{Op}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm BW}}_{\tt vec}(-{\rm i}a_{0}^{(1)})
is linearly Hamiltonian
up to homogeneity
N
, by adding another smoothing operator.

Reduction to constant coefficients of order
\frac{1}{2}
.

The next step is to put to constant coefficients the symbol

-{\rm i}b_{\frac{1}{2}}|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}

in system (
6.57
).
Let

\Phi_{\beta_{0}}(U):=\Phi_{\beta_{0}}^{\tau}(U;t)|_{\tau=1}
be the time

1
-flow
of

\partial_{\tau}\Phi_{\beta_{0}}^{\tau}(U)={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\beta_{0}(U;t,x)\,{\rm sign}\,\xi\right)}\Phi_{\beta_{0}}^{\tau}(U),\qquad\Phi_{\beta_{0}}^{0}(U)={\rm Id}\,,

where the
real function
\beta_{0}
in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,2,1}[r,N]
is

\displaystyle\beta_{0}(U;t,x)=\frac{2}{3\sqrt{\kappa}(1+\zeta(U))}\partial_{x}^{-1}\left(b_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;t,x)-\mathtt{b}_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;t)\right)\,,

(6.58)

\displaystyle\mathtt{b}_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;t):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}b_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;t,x)\,{\rm d}x\,.

Note that the real
x
-independent function
\mathtt{b}_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;t)
is in

\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,2,2}[r,N]
thanks to Remark
2.2
(it also follows by (
6.56
) since its linear component in
U
comes from
\partial_{t}\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}
which has zero average, see (
6.54
)).

By (
3.22
), the operator
{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\beta_{0}\,\textup{sign}\,\xi\right)}
is linearly Hamiltonian.
Hence by Lemma

3.16
,
\Phi_{\beta_{0}}(U)
is a diagonal matrix of spectrally localized maps in

\mathcal{S}^{0}_{K,2,0}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
with its inverse,
it is linearly symplectic and

\Phi_{\beta_{0}}(U)^{\pm 1}-{\rm Id}
belong to
\Sigma\mathcal{S}^{0}_{K,2,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

If
\breve{Z}
solves (
6.57
) then the variable
Z_{0}:=\Phi_{\beta_{0}}(U)\breve{Z}
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}Z_{0}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{\beta_{0}}(U)\,{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}\left[(1+\zeta(U))\omega(\xi)+\frac{\gamma}{2}\frac{{\mathtt{G}}(\xi)}{\xi}+\mathtt{V}(U;t)\xi\right]\right)}\Phi_{\beta_{0}}(U)^{-1}\,Z_{0}

\displaystyle+\Phi_{\beta_{0}(U)}\,{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}b_{\frac{1}{2}}|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}-{\rm i}a_{0}^{(1)}\right)}\Phi_{\beta_{0}}(U)^{-1}\,Z_{0}

\displaystyle+(\partial_{t}\Phi_{\beta_{0}}(U))\Phi^{-1}_{\beta_{0}}(U)Z_{0}+\Phi_{\beta_{0}}(U)R(U;t)\Phi_{\beta_{0}}(U)^{-1}Z_{0}\,.

Using the Lie expansions in (
6.45
), (
6.4
) with

{\bf\Phi}:=\Phi_{\beta_{0}}
,
{\bf F}:={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\beta_{0}{\rm sign}(\xi)\right)}
,

L:=L(\varrho)
large enough so that the integral remainders in the Lie expansions are
\varrho
-smoothing operators, and (
6.3
)
we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}Z_{0}

\displaystyle={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}\left[(1+\zeta(U))\omega(\xi)+\frac{\gamma}{2}\frac{{\mathtt{G}}(\xi)}{\xi}+\mathtt{V}(U;t)\xi\right]\right)}Z_{0}

(6.59)

\displaystyle+{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\left(\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{\kappa}(\beta_{0})_{x}(1+\zeta(U))-b_{\frac{1}{2}}\right)|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}+{\rm i}a_{0}^{(2)}\right)}\,Z_{0}+R(U;t)Z_{0}

where

a_{0}^{(2)}
is a symbol
in
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},1}[r,N]
and
R(U;t)
is a real-to-real matrix of
smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+3(N+1)}_{K,K^{\prime},1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
By
(
6.58
)
the symbol of order
\frac{1}{2}
in (
6.59
) is constant coefficient, namely

\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{\kappa}(\beta_{0})_{x}(1+\zeta(U))-b_{\frac{1}{2}}=-\mathtt{b}_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;t)\,,

and
system (
6.5
) reduces to

\displaystyle\partial_{t}Z_{0}

\displaystyle={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}\Big[(1+\zeta(U))\omega(\xi)+\frac{\gamma}{2}\frac{{\mathtt{G}}(\xi)}{\xi}+\mathtt{V}(U;t)\xi+\mathtt{b}_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;t)|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big]+{\rm i}a_{0}^{(2)}\right)}Z_{0}

(6.60)

\displaystyle+R(U;t)Z_{0}\,.

By Lemma
3.21
,
we replace each homogeneous component
of
\textup{Op}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm BW}}_{\tt vec}{({\rm i}a_{0}^{(2)})}
so that it becomes linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
, i.e.
by (
3.31
), it results that Im
a_{0}^{(2)}
belongs to

\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[r,N]
.

So far we have shown
that (
6.60
) becomes (
6.52
) with
n=0
, putting

a_{0}:=a_{0}^{(2)}

(which we consider as a symbol in
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime\prime}+1,1}[r,N]
)
and
\mathtt{b}_{0}^{(0)}:=0
.
We put
\mathcal{F}_{0}(U):=\Phi_{\beta_{0}}(U)\Phi_{\beta_{\frac{1}{2}}}(U)
.

Case
n\leadsto n+1
.
The proof is by induction on
n
.
Suppose that
Z_{n}
is a solution of system (
6.52
).
Let
\Phi_{F_{n}}(U):=\Phi_{F_{n}}^{\tau}(U;t)|_{\tau=1}
be the time
1
-flow

\partial_{\tau}\Phi_{F_{n}}^{\tau}(U)={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\beta_{-\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}(U;t,x,\xi)\right)}\Phi_{F_{n}}^{\tau}(U)\,,\quad\Phi_{F_{n}}^{0}(U)={\rm Id}\,,

where

\displaystyle\beta_{-\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}(U;t,x,\xi):=-\frac{2\,{\rm sign}\,\xi}{3\sqrt{\kappa}(1+\zeta(U))|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\partial_{x}^{-1}\left(a_{-\frac{n}{2}}(U;t,x,\xi)-\mathtt{a}_{-\frac{n}{2}}(U;t)\right)\,,

(6.61)

\displaystyle\mathtt{a}_{-\frac{n}{2}}(U;t):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}a_{-\frac{n}{2}}(U;t,x,\xi){\rm d}x\,.

By the inductive assumption, the symbol
a_{-\frac{n}{2}}
belongs to

\Sigma\Gamma^{-\frac{n}{2}}_{K,K^{\prime\prime}+1,1}[r,N]
and has imaginary part in
\Gamma^{-\frac{n}{2}}_{K,K^{\prime\prime}+1,N+1}[r]
.
Then the

x
-independent symbol
\mathtt{a}_{-\frac{n}{2}}
belongs to

\Sigma\Gamma^{-\frac{n}{2}}_{K,K^{\prime\prime}+1,2}[r,N]
thanks to Remark
2.2

and
\textup{Im}\,\mathtt{a}_{-\frac{n}{2}}\in\Gamma^{-\frac{n}{2}}_{K,K^{\prime\prime}+1,N+1}[r]
.
It follows that the symbol

\beta_{-\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}

belongs to
\Sigma\Gamma^{-\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}_{K,K^{\prime\prime}+1,1}[r,N]
and has
imaginary part in
\Gamma^{-\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}_{K,K^{\prime\prime}+1,N+1}[r]
.

Therefore by (
3.31
) the operator

\textup{Op}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm BW}}_{\tt vec}({\rm i}\beta_{-\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}})
is linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
.
By Lemma
3.16
, the flow
\Phi_{F_{n}}(U)
is invertible,
linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
and

\Phi_{{F_{n}}}(U)^{\pm 1}-{\rm Id}
belong to

\Sigma\mathcal{S}_{K,K^{\prime\prime}+1,1}^{0}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

If
Z_{n}
solves (
6.52
) then the variable
Z_{n+1}:=\Phi_{F_{n}}(U)Z_{n}
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}Z_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{F_{n}}(U)\,{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\,\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}}^{(n)}(U;t,\xi)+{\rm i}a_{-\frac{n}{2}}\right)}\Phi_{F_{n}}(U)^{-1}Z_{n+1}

\displaystyle+(\partial_{t}\Phi_{F_{n}}(U))\Phi_{F_{n}}(U)^{-1}Z_{n}+\Phi_{F_{n}}(U)R(U;t)\Phi_{F_{n}}(U)^{-1}Z_{n+1}\,.

Using the Lie expansions in (
6.45
), (
6.4
) with

{\bf\Phi}:=\Phi_{F_{n}}
,
{\bf F}:={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\beta_{-\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}\right)}

with (
L:=L(\varrho)
large enough),
the last bullets at the end of Section
2.1
and Proposition
2.15
-
(iv)
,
(
6.53
), (
6.3
),
we obtain that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}Z_{n+1}

\displaystyle={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\,\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}}^{(n)}+{\rm i}\left[\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{\kappa}(\beta_{-\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}})_{x}(1+\zeta(U))|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}{\rm sign}\,{\xi}+a_{-\frac{n}{2}}\right]\right)}Z_{n+1}

\displaystyle\quad+{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}a_{-\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}\right)}Z_{n+1}+R(U;t)Z_{n+1}

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{betan12}}}{{=}}{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\,\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}}^{(n)}+{\rm i}\mathtt{a}_{-\frac{n}{2}}+{\rm i}a_{-\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}\right)}Z_{n+1}+R(U;t)Z_{n+1}

(6.62)

where we collect in
a_{-\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}
all the symbols in
\Sigma\Gamma^{-\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}_{K,K^{\prime\prime}+2,1}[r,N]
,
and
R(U;t)
is a smoothing operator in

\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+3(N+1)}_{K,K^{\prime\prime}+2,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
By Lemma
3.21
, we replace each homogeneous component of

\textup{Op}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm BW}}_{\tt vec}{({\rm i}a_{-\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}})}
so that it is linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
; which,
by (
3.31
), is equivalent to assume that the imaginary part

\mathrm{Im}\,a_{-\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}
is a symbol in

\Gamma^{-\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}_{K,K^{\prime\prime}+2,N+1}[r]
.

System (
6.62
) has the form (
6.52
) at step
n+1
with

\mathtt{b}_{0}^{(n+1)}:=\mathtt{b}_{0}^{(n)}+\mathtt{a}_{-\frac{n}{2}}

(hence

\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}}^{(n+1)}:=\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}}^{(n)}+\mathtt{a}_{-\frac{n}{2}}
) and
K^{\prime\prime}(n+1):=K^{\prime\prime}(n)+1
.

The thesis follows with
\mathcal{F}_{n+1}(U):=\Phi_{F_{n}}(U)\mathcal{F}_{n}(U)
. The proof of Lemma
6.9
is complete.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.1
.

We now choose in Lemma
6.9
a number
n:=n_{2}(\varrho)

of iterative steps satisfying
n_{2}(\varrho)\geq 2(\varrho-3(N+1))

so that we incorporate
\textup{Op}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm BW}}_{\tt vec}{({\rm i}a_{-\frac{n}{2}})}
in the smoothing remainder
R(U;t)
, which belongs to
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+3(N+1)}_{K,K^{\prime\prime}+1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

with
K^{\prime\prime}=n_{1}(\varrho)+n_{2}(\varrho)\geq 3\varrho-8(N+1)
, with
n_{1}(\varrho)

fixed above (
6.51
).
Denoting
W:=Z_{n}
, system (
6.52
) has the form
(
6.1
)
with
\mathtt{b}_{0}(U;t,\xi):=-\mathtt{b}_{0}^{(n_{2})}(U;t,\xi)
in
(
6.2
)
and taking as
\underline{K}^{\prime}:=K^{\prime\prime}+1=n_{1}(\varrho)+n_{2}(\varrho)+1

(this proves Remark
6.3
).
The variable
W
can be written as
W={\bf B}(U;t)U
where

{\bf B}(U;t):=\mathcal{F}_{n_{2}}(U)\circ\Phi_{n_{1}}(U)\circ\Psi_{2}(U)\circ\Psi_{1}(U)\circ\mathcal{G}(U)

and
\mathcal{G}(U)
is the map of Lemma
6.4
,
\Psi_{1}(U)
is the map of Lemma
6.5
,
\Psi_{2}(U)
is the map of Lemma
6.7
,
\Phi_{n_{1}}(U)

is the map of Lemma
6.8
with number of steps

n_{1}:=n_{1}(\varrho)
and

\mathcal{F}_{n_{2}}(U)
is the map of Lemma
6.9
with number of steps

n_{2}:=n_{2}(\varrho)
.
Since

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(U)-{\rm Id}\,,\ \ \ \Psi_{1}(U)-{\rm Id}\in\Sigma\mathcal{S}^{0}_{K,0,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\,,\ \ \Phi_{n_{1}}(U)-{\rm Id}\in\Sigma\mathcal{S}_{K,K^{\prime},1}^{0}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}(U)-{\rm Id}\in\Sigma\mathcal{S}^{N+1}_{K,0,2}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\,,\quad\mathcal{F}_{n_{2}}(U)-{\rm Id}\in\Sigma\mathcal{S}^{(N+1)/2}_{K,K^{\prime\prime},1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\,,

we deduce by Proposition
2.19
that
{\bf B}(U;t)-{\rm Id}
is a
real-to-real matrix of spectrally
localized maps in

\Sigma\mathcal{S}^{\frac{3}{2}(N+1)}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime}-1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
In addition
{\bf B}(U;t)

is a spectrally localized map in

\mathcal{S}^{0}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime}-1,0}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
with its inverse,
as each map

\mathcal{F}_{n_{2}}(U)
,
\Phi_{n_{1}}(U)
,
\Psi_{2}(U)
,
\Psi_{1}(U)
,
\mathcal{G}(U)
separately.
Finally
{\bf B}(U;t)
is linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
, being the composition of linearly symplectic maps up to homogeneity
N
. This completes the proof of
Proposition
6.1
.

7
Hamiltonian Birkhoff normal form

The main result of this section is Proposition
7.12
which transforms
the water waves equations in Hamiltonian Birkhoff normal form.
This is required to ensure that the life span of the solutions is of order
\varepsilon^{-N-1}
with
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
So
from now on we take
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

In Proposition
6.1
we have conjugated
the water waves Hamiltonian system (
5.37
) into (
6.1
), by applying the transformation

W={\bf B}(U;t)U
which is just linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
. Thus the transformed system (
6.1
) is not Hamiltonian anymore.
The first goal of this section is to
construct a nearby transformation which is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
, according to Definition
3.11
, thus obtaining a Hamiltonian system up to homogeneity
N
,
according to Definition
3.10
.

7.1
Hamiltonian correction up to homogeneity
N

We first prove the following abstract result, which is a
direct consequence of Theorem
4.1
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
p,N\in\mathbb{N}
with
p\leq N
,
K,K^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with

K^{\prime}+1\leq K
,
r>0
.
Let

Z={\bf M}_{0}(U;t)U
with
{\bf M}_{0}(U;t)\in\mathcal{M}^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},0}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

as in (
3.35
). Assume
that
Z(t)
solves a Hamiltonian system up to homogeneity
N
,
according to Definition
3.10
. Consider

\Phi(Z):={\bf B}(Z;t)Z

(7.1)

where

•

{\bf B}(Z;t)-{\rm Id}
is a matrix of spectrally localized maps in

{\bf B}(Z;t)-{\rm Id}\in\begin{cases}\Sigma\mathcal{S}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\qquad\qquad\text{if}\quad{\bf M}_{0}(U;t)={\rm Id}\,,\\
\Sigma\mathcal{S}_{K,0,p}[\breve{r},N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\,,\,\forall\breve{r}>0\quad\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

(7.2)

•

{\bf B}(Z;t)
is linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
,
according to Definition
3.7
.

Then there exists a real-to-real matrix of pluri–homogeneous smoothing operators
R_{\leq N}(\cdot)
in
\Sigma_{p}^{N}{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\varrho}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
,
for any
\varrho>0
, such that the non-linear map

Z_{+}:=\big({\rm Id}+R_{\leq N}(\Phi(Z)\big)\Phi(Z)

is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
(Definition
3.11
) and thus
Z_{+}

solves a system which is Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
.

Proof.

We decompose

{\bf B}(Z;t)={\bf B}_{\leq N}(Z)+{\bf B}_{>N}(Z;t)
where

{\bf B}_{\leq N}(Z):=\mathcal{P}_{\leq N}[{\bf B}(Z;t)]
.
Note that

{\bf B}_{\leq N}(Z)-{\rm Id}
is in

\Sigma_{p}^{N}{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

and
{\bf B}_{>N}(Z;t)

is in

\mathcal{S}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Since
{\bf B}(Z;t)
is linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
,
its pluri-homogeneous component

{\bf B}_{\leq N}(Z)
is linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
as well. Then
Theorem
4.1
applied to
\Phi_{\leq N}(Z):={\bf B}_{\leq N}(Z)Z
implies the existence of pluri-homogeneous smoothing operators
R_{\leq N}(\cdot)
in
\Sigma_{p}^{N}{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\varrho}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
for any
\varrho\geq 0
,
such that the nonlinear map

\mathcal{D}_{N}(Z):=\big({\rm Id}+R_{\leq N}(\Phi_{\leq N}(Z))\big)\Phi_{\leq N}(Z)

is
symplectic up to homogeneity

N
.
We then write

\mathcal{D}(Z;t):=\big({\rm Id}+R_{\leq N}(\Phi(Z))\big)\Phi(Z)=\mathcal{D}_{N}(Z)+M_{>N}(Z;t)Z

where, using Proposition
2.15
-
(ii)
and the first bullet after Definition
2.5
,

M_{>N}(Z;t)\in\begin{cases}\mathcal{M}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\qquad\qquad\text{if}\quad{\bf M}_{0}(U;t)={\rm Id}\,,\\
\mathcal{M}_{K,0,N+1}[\breve{r}]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\,,\,\forall\breve{r}>0\quad\text{otherwise}\,,\end{cases}

showing that
\mathcal{D}(Z;t)
is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
as well.
Then Lemma
3.15
implies the thesis.
∎

The first application of Theorem
7.1
is to
provide a symplectic correction of the map

\Phi(U):={\bf B}(U;t)U
of Proposition
6.1

and to conjugate the
Hamiltonian system (
5.37
) into system
(
7.4
), which is
Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
.

Proposition 7.2

(
Hamiltonian reduction up to smoothing operators).

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varrho>c(N):=3(N+1)+\frac{3}{2}(N+1)^{3}
.
Then for any
K\geq\underline{K}^{\prime}
(fixed in Proposition
6.1
)
there is
s_{0}>0,r>0
, such that
for any solution

of (
5.37
), there exists a real-to-real matrix of pluri–homogeneous smoothing operators
R(U)
in
\Sigma_{1}^{N}{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\varrho^{\prime}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

for any
\varrho^{\prime}\geq 0
, such that defining

Z_{0}:=\big({\rm Id}+R(\Phi(U))\big)\Phi(U),\quad\Phi(U):={\bf B}(U;t)U\,,

(7.3)

where
{\bf B}(U;t)
is the
real-to-real matrix of spectrally localized maps defined in Proposition

,
the following holds true:

(i)

Symplecticity:
The non-linear map
in (

)

according to Definition

.

(ii)

Conjugation:

the variable

N
(cfr. Definition
3.10
)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}Z_{0}

\displaystyle=-{\rm i}{\bf\Omega}(D)Z_{0}+{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}(\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{\leq N}(Z_{0};\xi)-{\rm i}(\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}(U;t,\xi)\right)}Z_{0}

(7.4)

\displaystyle+R_{\leq N}(Z_{0})Z_{0}+R_{>N}(U;t)U

where

•

is the diagonal matrix of Fourier multipliers defined in (

);

•

(\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{\leq N}(Z_{0};\xi)

;

with imaginary part

;

•

is a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators in

\Sigma_{1}^{N}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho+c(N)}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
;

•

R_{>N}(U;t)
is a real-to-real matrix of non–homogeneous smoothing operators in
\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+c(N)}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime},N+1}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

(iii)

Boundedness:

The variable
Z_{0}={\bf M}_{0}(U;t)U
with
{\bf M}_{0}(U;t)\in\mathcal{M}^{0}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime}-1,0}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
for any
s\geq s_{0}
, for all
0<r<r_{0}(s)
small enough,
for any
U\in B_{s_{0}}^{K}(I;r)\cap C_{*\mathbb{R}}^{K}(I;\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}))
,
there is a constant
C:=C_{s,K}>0
such that, for all
k=0,\dots,K-\underline{K}^{\prime}
,

C^{-1}\|U\|_{k,s}\leq\|Z_{0}\|_{k,s}\leq C\|U\|_{k,s}\,.

(7.5)

Proof.

We construct the symplectic corrector to the map

W:=\Phi(U)={\bf B}(U;t)U
of Proposition
6.1
by Theorem
7.1
.
Let us check its assumptions.
By Lemma
5.5
, the function
U
solves the Hamiltonian system (
5.37
).
By Proposition

,
{\bf B}(U;t)-{\rm Id}
is a spectrally localized map in
\Sigma\mathcal{S}^{\frac{3}{2}(N+1)}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime}-1,1}[r,N]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
{\bf B}(U;t)
is linearly symplectic
up to homogeneity
N
.
So Theorem
7.1
(in the case
{\bf M}_{0}(U;t)={\rm Id}
) implies the existence of
a matrix of pluri–homogeneous smoothing operators

R(W)
in
\Sigma_{1}^{N}{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\varrho^{\prime}}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, for any
\varrho^{\prime}\geq 0
,
such that the variable

Z_{0}:=({\rm Id}+R(\Phi(U)))\Phi(U)=({\rm Id}+R(W))W\

(7.6)

solves a system which is Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
. We now prove that such system has the form (
7.4
).
We will compute it by transforming system
(

) solved by
W(t)
under the change of variable

, exploiting that

is a pluri-homogeneous smoothing operator.
We first substitute the variable
U
with the variable
W

in the homogeneous components up to degree

of both the symbols and the smoothing operator in (
6.1
).
We first use Lemma
A.1
(with

and

) to construct an approximate inverse of

, getting

U=\Psi_{\leq N}(W)+M_{>N}(U;t)U\,,\qquad\Psi_{\leq N}(W)=W+\breve{S}_{\leq N}(W)W\,,

(7.7)

where
\breve{S}_{\leq N}(W)\in\Sigma_{1}^{N}\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{q}^{\frac{3}{2}(N+1)N}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and

is a matrix of operators in

\mathcal{M}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime},N+1}^{\frac{3}{2}(N+1)^{2}}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Next we substitute (

) in the homogeneous components of order
\leq N

in system (

) of

{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}}(U;t,\xi)\right)}={{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\rm i}(\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{\leq N}(U)+{\rm i}(\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}(U;t,\xi)\right)}\,,\ \ \ R(U;t)=R_{\leq N}(U)+R_{>N}(U;t)\,,

and substitute

in the term

.
By (

), (

), Lemma

(with

,

,

and

) and Proposition

(i)
we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}W

\displaystyle=-{\rm i}{\bf\Omega}(D)W+{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}(\widetilde{\mathtt{m}}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{\leq N}(W;\xi)-{\rm i}(\widetilde{\mathtt{m}}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}(U;t,\xi)\right)}W

(7.8)

\displaystyle+\widetilde{R}_{\leq N}(W)W+\widetilde{R}_{>N}(U;t)U

where

\bullet

(\widetilde{\mathtt{m}}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{\leq N}(W;\xi)
is a real valued symbol, independent of
x
, in

\Sigma_{2}^{N}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{q}
;

\bullet

is a non–homogeneous symbol, independent of
x
, in
\Gamma^{\frac{3}{2}}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime},N+1}[r]

given by the sum of the
old non-homogeneous symbol

in (

)-(

) and a purely real correction coming from formula (

) (cfr.

) hence its imaginary part

is in

;

\bullet

is a matrix of pluri-homogeneous smoothing operators in

with

;

is a matrix of non–homogeneous smoothing operators in

.

We finally conjugate system (

)
under the change of variable

defined in (

).
Note that system (

) fulfills Assumption

at pag
A
with

, with

replacing

,

and

.

with the

) (choosing also

) and we deduce

).
Item

follows from (

),

), the fact that

for any

(by Lemma

.

) and the estimate below (

) for

7.2
Super action preserving symbols and Hamiltonians

In this section we define the special class of “super–action preserving”

\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}

homogeneous symbols and Hamiltonians
which will appear in the Birkhoff normal form reduction of the next
Section
7.3
.

Definition 7.3
.

(
\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}
multi-index)

A multi-index

(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}

is super action preserving if

\alpha_{n}+\alpha_{-n}=\beta_{n}+\beta_{-n}\ ,\qquad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\,.

(7.9)

A super action preserving multi-index
(\alpha,\beta)
satisfies
|\alpha|=|\beta|
where

|\alpha|:=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\alpha_{j}
.
If a multi-index
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}
is not super action preserving, then the set

\mathfrak{N}(\alpha,\beta):=\Big\{n\in\mathbb{N}\,\colon\,\alpha_{n}+\alpha_{-n}-\beta_{n}-\beta_{-n}\neq 0\Big\}

(7.10)

is not empty
and, since
\mathfrak{N}(\alpha,\beta)\subset\{n\in\mathbb{N}\,:\,\alpha_{n}+\alpha_{-n}+\beta_{n}+\beta_{-n}\neq 0\}
, its cardinality satisfies

|\mathfrak{N}(\alpha,\beta)|\leq|\alpha+\beta|=|\alpha|+|\beta|\,.

(7.11)

Definition 7.4
.

(
\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}
monomial)

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}
. Given
(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma})=(j_{a},\sigma_{a})_{a=1,\dots,p}\in(\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\})^{p}\times\{\pm\}^{p}
we define the multi-index
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}
with components, for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
,

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma}):=\#\big\{a=1,\ldots,p\,:\,(j_{a},\sigma_{a})=(k,+)\big\}\,,

(7.12)

\displaystyle\beta_{k}(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma}):=\#\big\{a=1,\ldots,p\,:\,(j_{a},\sigma_{a})=(k,-)\big\}\,.

We say that a monomial of the form

z_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}=z_{j_{1}}^{\sigma_{1}}\dots z_{j_{p}}^{\sigma_{p}}
is super-action preserving if the associated multi-index
(\alpha,\beta)=(\alpha(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma}),\beta(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma}))
is super-action preserving according to Definition
7.3
.

We now introduce the subset
\mathfrak{S}_{p}
of the indexes of
{\mathfrak{T}}_{p}
defined in (
2.10
)
composed by super-action preserving indexes

\mathfrak{S}_{p}:=\Big\{(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma})\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p}\,:\,(\alpha(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma}),\beta(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma}))\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\ \text{in}\ \eqref{defalbe}\ \text{are super action preserving}\Big\}\,.

(7.13)

We remark that the multi-index
(\alpha,\beta)
associated to

(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma})\in(\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\times\{\pm\})^{p}
as in (
7.12
) satisfies
|\alpha+\beta|=p
and

z_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}=z^{\alpha}\overline{z}^{\beta}:=\prod_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}z_{j}^{\alpha_{j}}{\overline{z_{j}}}^{\beta_{j}}=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}z_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}z_{-n}^{\alpha_{-n}}{\overline{z_{n}}}^{\beta_{n}}\overline{z_{-n}}^{\beta_{-n}}\,\,.

(7.14)

It turns out

\vec{\sigma}\cdot\Omega_{\vec{\jmath}}\,(\kappa)=\sigma_{1}\Omega_{j_{1}}(\kappa)+\dots+\sigma_{p}\Omega_{j_{p}}(\kappa)=(\alpha-\beta)\cdot\vec{\Omega}(\kappa)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}(\alpha_{k}-\beta_{k})\Omega_{k}(\kappa)\,,

(7.15)

where we denote

\vec{\Omega}(\kappa):=\{\Omega_{j}(\kappa)\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}},\quad\Omega_{\vec{\jmath}}\,(\kappa):=(\Omega_{j_{1}}(\kappa),\dots,\Omega_{j_{p}}(\kappa))\,.

(7.16)

Remark 7.5
.

In view of (
7.14
) and (
7.9
) a super action monomial has either the
integrable form
|z_{j_{1}}|^{2}\ldots|z_{j_{m}}|^{2}
or
the one described in (
1.14
) (with not necessarily distinct indexes
j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}
).

Remark 7.6
.

If the monomial
z_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}
is super–action preserving then, for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, the monomial
z_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}z_{j}\overline{z}_{j}
is super-action preserving as well.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we define the
super action

J_{n}:=|z_{n}|^{2}+|z_{-n}|^{2}\,.

(7.17)

Lemma 7.7
.

The Poisson bracket between a monomial
z_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}

and a super-action
J_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, defined
in (
7.17
), is

\{z_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}},J_{n}\}={\rm i}\big(\beta_{n}+\beta_{-n}-\alpha_{n}-\alpha_{-n}\big)\,z_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}\,,

(7.18)

where
(\alpha,\beta)=(\alpha(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma}),\beta(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma}))
is the multi-index defined in (
7.12
).
In particular a super action preserving
monomial
z_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}

(according to Definition
7.4
) Poisson commutes with any super action
J_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

We write the monomial
z_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}=z^{\alpha}\overline{z}^{\beta}

as in (
7.14
). Then,
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
j,k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, one has

\displaystyle\partial_{z_{j}}(z_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}})=\alpha_{j}z_{j}^{\alpha_{j}-1}{\overline{z_{j}}^{\beta_{j}}}\prod_{k\not=j}z_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}\overline{z_{k}}^{\beta_{k}},\quad\partial_{\overline{z_{j}}}(z_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}})=\beta_{j}z_{j}^{\alpha_{j}}\overline{z_{j}}^{\beta_{j}-1}\prod_{k\not=j}z_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}\overline{z_{k}}^{\beta_{k}}\,,

(7.19)

\displaystyle\partial_{z_{j}}J_{n}=\begin{cases}\overline{z_{j}}&j=\pm n\\
0&j\not=\pm n\,,\end{cases}\quad\partial_{\overline{z_{j}}}J_{n}=\begin{cases}z_{j}&j=\pm n\\
0&j\not=\pm n\,.\end{cases}

Then by (
3.29
) and (
7.19
) we deduce
(
7.18
).
∎

We now define a super action preserving Hamiltonian.

Definition 7.8
.

(
\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}
Hamiltonian)
Let
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
A
(p+2)
–homogeneous super action preserving Hamiltonian
H^{(\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P})}_{p+2}(Z)
is a real function of the form

H^{(\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P})}_{p+2}(Z)=\frac{1}{p+2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\vec{\jmath}_{p+2},\vec{\sigma}_{p+2})\in\mathfrak{S}_{p+2}\end{subarray}}H_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+2}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+2}}z_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+2}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+2}}

where
\mathfrak{S}_{p+2}
is defined as in (
7.13
).
A pluri-homogeneous super action preserving Hamiltonian is a finite sum of
homogeneous super action preserving Hamiltonians.
A Hamiltonian vector field is super action preserving if it is generated by a
super action preserving Hamiltonian.

We now define a super action preserving symbol.

Definition 7.9
.

(
\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}
symbol)

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
m\in\mathbb{R}
.
For
p\geq 1
a real valued,
p
–homogeneous super action preserving symbol of order
m
is a symbol
\mathtt{m}_{p}^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}(Z;\xi)
in
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{p}
, independent of
x
, of the form

\mathtt{m}_{p}^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}(Z;\xi)=\sum_{({\vec{\jmath}_{p}},{\vec{\sigma}_{p}})\in\mathfrak{S}_{p}}M_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}(\xi)z_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}\,.

(7.20)

For
p=0
we say that any symbol in
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{0}
is
super action preserving.
A pluri-homogeneous super action preserving symbol is a finite sum of
homogeneous super action preserving symbols.

Remark 7.10
.

A super action preserving symbol has even degree
p
of homogeneity.
Indeed, if
z_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}
is super-
action preserving then
(\alpha,\beta)
defined in (
7.12
) satisfies

|\alpha|=|\beta|
and
p=|\alpha+\beta|=2|\alpha|
is even.

Given a super action preserving symbol we associate a
super action preserving Hamiltonian according to the following lemma.

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
m\in\mathbb{R}
.
If
(\mathtt{m}^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})})_{p}(Z;\xi)
is a
p
–homogeneous super action preserving symbol
in
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{p}
according to Definition
7.9
then

H^{(\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P})}_{p+2}(Z):=\mathrm{Re}\,\Big\langle{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left((\mathtt{m}^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})})_{p}(Z;\xi)\right)}z,\overline{z}\Big\rangle_{\dot{L}^{2}_{r}}

is a
(p+2)
–homogeneous super action preserving Hamiltonian according to Definition
7.8
.

Proof.

By the expression (
7.20
) and (
2.23
) we have

\int_{\mathbb{T}}{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(\mathtt{m}_{p}^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}(Z;\xi)\right)}\Pi_{0}^{\bot}z\cdot\Pi_{0}^{\bot}\overline{z}\,{\rm d}x=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\sum_{(\vec{\jmath}_{p},\vec{\sigma}_{p})\in\mathfrak{S}_{p}}\chi_{p}(\vec{\jmath}_{p},j)M_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}(j)z_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}z_{j}\overline{z_{j}}

where
\mathfrak{S}_{p}
is defined in
(
7.13
).
Then Remark
7.6
implies the thesis.
For
p=0
the
Hamiltonian
H^{(\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P})}_{2}(Z)

is a series of integrable monomials
z_{j}\overline{z_{j}}
. The proof of the lemma is complete.
∎

7.3
Birkhoff normal form reduction

In this section we finally
transform system (
7.4
) into its
Hamiltonian Birkhoff normal form, up to homogeneity
N
.

Proposition 7.12
.

(Hamiltonian Birkhoff normal form)

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Assume that, for any value of the gravity
g>0
,
vorticity
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
and depth
\mathtt{h}\in(0,+\infty]
,
the surface tension coefficient
\kappa
is outside the zero measure set

{\mathcal{K}}\subset(0,+\infty)
defined in Theorem
B.1
.

Then there exists
\underline{\varrho}
(depending on
N
) such that, for any
\varrho\geq\underline{\varrho}
, for any
K\geq\underline{K}^{\prime}(\varrho)
(defined in Proposition
6.1
),
there exists
\underline{s}_{0}>0
such that, for any
s\geq\underline{s}_{0}
there is
\underline{r}_{0}:=\underline{r}_{0}(s)>0
such that for all
0<r<\underline{r}_{0}(s)
small enough, and any solution
U\in B_{\underline{s}_{0}}^{K}(I;r)\cap C^{K}_{*\mathbb{R}}(I;\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}^{2}))
of the water waves system (
5.37
),
there exists a
non–linear map
\mathcal{F}_{\mathtt{nf}}(Z_{0})

such that:

(i)

Simpleticity:

\mathcal{F}_{\mathtt{nf}}(Z_{0})
is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
(Definition
3.11
);

(ii)

Conjugation:
If
Z_{0}
solves the system (
7.4
) then the variable
Z:=\mathcal{F}_{\mathtt{nf}}(Z_{0})
solves the Hamiltonian system up to homogeneity
N
(cfr. Definition
3.10
)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}Z

\displaystyle=-{\rm i}{\bf\Omega}(D)Z+J_{c}\nabla H^{(\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P})}_{\frac{3}{2}}(Z)+J_{c}\nabla H^{(\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P})}_{-\varrho}(Z)

(7.21)

\displaystyle+{{{\rm Op}_{\mathtt{vec}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left(-{\rm i}(\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}(U;t,\xi)\right)}Z+R_{>N}(U;t)U

where

•

H^{(\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P})}_{\frac{3}{2}}(Z)
is the super action preserving Hamiltonian

\mathrm{Re}\,\Big\langle{{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left((\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}}^{(\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P})})_{\leq N}(Z;\xi)\right)}z,\overline{z}\Big\rangle_{\dot{L}^{2}_{r}}

with a pluri homogeneous super action preserving symbol

(\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}}^{(\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P})})_{\leq N}(Z;\xi)
in
\Sigma_{2}^{N}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{q}^{\frac{3}{2}}
,
according to Definition
7.9
;

•

J_{c}\nabla H^{(\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P})}_{-\varrho}(Z)

is a super action preserving, Hamiltonian, smoothing vector field in
\Sigma_{3}^{N+1}\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{-\varrho+\underline{\varrho}}_{q}
(see Definitions

and
7.8
);

•

(\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}(U;t,\xi)
is a non–homogeneous symbol in
\Gamma^{\frac{3}{2}}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime},N+1}[r]
with imaginary part
\mathrm{Im}\,(\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}(U;t,\xi)
in
\Gamma^{0}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime},N+1}[r]
;

•

R_{>N}(U;t)
is a real-to-real matrix of non–homogeneous smoothing operators
in
\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+\underline{\varrho}}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime},N+1}[r]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

(iii)

Boundedness:
there exists
C:=C_{s,K}>0
such that for all
0\leq k\leq K
and any
Z_{0}\in B_{\underline{s}_{0}}^{K}(I;r)\cap C^{K}_{*\mathbb{R}}(I;\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}))
one has

C^{-1}\|Z_{0}\|_{k,s}\leq\|\mathcal{F}_{\mathtt{nf}}(Z_{0})\|_{k,s}\leq C\|Z_{0}\|_{k,s}\,.

(7.22)

and

{C}^{-1}\|U(t)\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq\|Z(t)\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq C\|U(t)\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\,,\quad\forall t\in I\,.

(7.23)

Proof.

We divide the proof in
N
steps. At the
p
-th step,
1\leq p\leq N
,
we reduce the
p
–homogeneous component of the Hamiltonian vector field which appears in the equation to its super action preserving part, up to higher homogeneity terms.

Step
1
: Elimination of the quadratic smoothing remainder in equation (
7.4
).

The
x
-independent symbol
(\mathtt{m}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{\leq N}(Z_{0};\xi)
in (
7.4
) belongs to
\Sigma_{2}^{N}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{q}
and the only quadratic component of the vector field in (

) is

R_{1}(Z_{0})Z_{0}
where

R_{1}(Z_{0}):=\mathcal{P}_{1}[R_{\leq N}(Z_{0})]\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{-\varrho+c(N)}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\ .

(7.24)

Since system (
7.4
) is Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
,
R_{1}(Z_{0})Z_{0}
is a Hamiltonian vector field in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{-\varrho+c(N)}_{2}

that we expand in Fourier coordinates
as in (
2.139
)

\big(R_{1}(Z_{0})Z_{0}\big)_{k}^{\sigma}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(j_{1},j_{2},k,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},-\sigma)\in\mathfrak{T}_{3}\end{subarray}}X_{j_{1},j_{2},k}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma}(z_{0})_{j_{1}}^{\sigma_{1}}(z_{0})_{j_{2}}^{\sigma_{2}}\,.

(7.25)

In order to remove
R_{1}(Z_{0})Z_{0}
from equation (
7.4
)
we perform the change of variable

Z_{1}=\mathtt{F}^{(1)}_{\leq N}(Z_{0})

where
\mathtt{F}^{(1)}_{\leq N}(Z_{0})

is the time
1
-approximate flow,
given by Lemma
2.26
, generated by
the smoothing vector field

\big(G_{1}(Z_{0})Z_{0}\big)_{k}^{\sigma}=\sum_{(j_{1},j_{2},k,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},-\sigma)\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{3}}G_{j_{1},j_{2},k}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma}(z_{0})_{j_{1}}^{\sigma_{1}}(z_{0})_{j_{2}}^{\sigma_{2}}

(7.26)

with

G_{j_{1},j_{2},k}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma}:=\begin{cases}0&\mbox{ if }\ (j_{1},j_{2},k,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},-\sigma)\notin{\mathfrak{T}}_{3}\\
\displaystyle{\frac{X_{j_{1},j_{2},k}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma}}{{\rm i}\big(\sigma_{1}\Omega_{j_{1}}(\kappa)+\sigma_{2}\Omega_{j_{2}}(\kappa)-\sigma\Omega_{k}(\kappa)\big)}}&\mbox{ if }(j_{1},j_{2},k,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},-\sigma)\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{3}\,.\end{cases}

(7.27)

Lemma 7.13
.

Let
\kappa\in(0,+\infty)\setminus\mathcal{K}
.
Then the vector field

G_{1}(Z_{0})Z_{0}
in (
7.26
), (
7.27
) is a well defined Hamiltonian vector field in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{-\varrho^{\prime}}_{2}
with
\varrho^{\prime}:=\varrho-c(N)-\tau
and where
\tau
is defined in Theorem
B.1
.

Proof.

We claim that for any
\kappa\in(0,+\infty)\setminus\mathcal{K}

there exist
\tau,\nu>0
such that

|\sigma_{1}\Omega_{j_{1}}(\kappa)+\sigma_{2}\Omega_{j_{2}}(\kappa)-\sigma\Omega_{k}(\kappa)|>\frac{\nu}{\max\{|j_{1}|,|j_{2}|,|k|\}^{\tau}}\,,\quad\forall(j_{1},j_{2},k,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},-\sigma)\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{3}\,.

(7.28)

Indeed,
to any
(j_{1},j_{2},k,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},-\sigma)

we associate the multi–index

(\alpha,\beta)
as in (
7.12
) whose length is
|\alpha+\beta|=3
and satisfies

\sigma_{1}\Omega_{j_{1}}(\kappa)+\sigma_{2}\Omega_{j_{2}}(\kappa)-\sigma\Omega_{k}(\kappa)=(\alpha-\beta)\cdot\vec{\Omega}(\kappa)
by (
7.15
).
Having length 3, by Remark
7.10
, the multi-index
(\alpha,\beta)
is not super–action preserving and
therefore
Theorem
B.1
implies (
7.28
).
In view of (
7.28
) the coefficients in (
7.27
) are well defined.

Next we show that
G_{1}(Z_{0})Z_{0}
is a vector field in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{-\varrho^{\prime}}_{2}
.
As
R_{1}(Z_{0})Z_{0}
belongs to
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{-\varrho+c(N)}_{2}
,
by Lemma
2.29
the coefficients

X_{j_{1},j_{2},k}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma}
in (
7.25
)
satisfy the
symmetric and reality
properties (
2.141
), (
2.142
) and the estimate:
for some
\mu\geq 0
,
C>0
,

|X_{j_{1},j_{2},k}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma}|\leq C\frac{\max_{2}\{|j_{1}|,|j_{2}|\}^{\mu}}{\max\{|j_{1}|,|j_{2}|\}^{\varrho-c(N)}}\,,\qquad\forall(j_{1},j_{2},k,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},-\sigma)\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{3}\,.

(7.29)

Hence also the
coefficients
G_{j_{1},j_{2},k}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma}

in (
7.27
)
fulfill the
symmetric, reality
properties (
2.141
), (
2.142
) as well as
X_{j_{1},j_{2},k}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma}
.
Moreover, using (
7.29
), (
7.28
)
and the momentum relation
\sigma k=\sigma_{1}j_{1}+\sigma_{2}j_{2}
, they also satisfy

|G_{j_{1},j_{2},k}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma}|\leq C\frac{\max_{2}\{|j_{1}|,|j_{2}|\}^{\mu}}{\max\{|j_{1}|,|j_{2}|\}^{\varrho-c(N)-\tau}}

for a new constant
C>0
(depending on
\nu
).
Then Lemma
2.29
implies that
G_{1}(Z_{0})Z_{0}
belongs to
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{-\varrho^{\prime}}_{2}
with
\varrho^{\prime}:=\varrho-c(N)-\tau
.

Finally we show that

G_{1}(Z_{0})Z_{0}
is Hamiltonian.
Recall that
R_{1}(Z_{0})Z_{0}
in (
7.25
) is a Hamiltonian vector field whose Hamiltonian function

H_{R_{1}}(Z_{0})
is, thanks to Lemma
3.5
,

H_{R_{1}}(Z_{0})=\tfrac{1}{3}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{3}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\![H_{R_{1}}]_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}(z_{0})_{j_{1}}^{\sigma_{1}}(z_{0})_{j_{2}}^{\sigma_{2}}(z_{0})_{j_{3}}^{\sigma_{3}}\,,\qquad[H_{R_{1}}]_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}:=-{\rm i}\sigma_{3}X_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},-\sigma_{3}}\,.

(7.30)

Then
the coefficients defined for
\sigma_{1}j_{1}+\sigma_{2}j_{2}+\sigma_{3}j_{3}=0
by

[H_{G_{1}}]_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}:=-{\rm i}\sigma_{3}G_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},-\sigma_{3}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{G1+.coeff},\eqref{HR1}}}{{=}}\frac{[H_{R_{1}}]_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}}{{\rm i}\big(\sigma_{1}\Omega_{j_{1}}(\kappa)+\sigma_{2}\Omega_{j_{2}}(\kappa)+\sigma_{3}\Omega_{j_{3}}(\kappa)\big)}

(7.31)

satisfy the symmetric, reality properties (
2.130
), (
2.129
) as well as
the coefficients

.
Then Lemma
3.5
implies that

G_{1}(Z_{0})Z_{0}
is the Hamiltonian vector field generated by the Hamiltonian
H_{G_{1}}
with coefficients defined in (
7.31
).
∎

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
\kappa\in(0,+\infty)\setminus\mathcal{K}

∎

Lemma 7.15
.

Let
\kappa\in(0,+\infty)\setminus\mathcal{K}
.
The vector field
G_{p}(V)V
in (
7.59
), (
7.60
) is a well defined Hamiltonian vector field in

with
\varrho^{\prime}:=\varrho-\underline{\varrho}(p)-c(N,p)-\tau
and where
\tau
is defined in Theorem
B.1
.

Proof.

We claim that for any
\kappa\in(0,+\infty)\setminus\mathcal{K}

there exist
\tau,\nu>0
such that

|\vec{\sigma}_{p+1}\cdot{\Omega}_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1}}(\kappa)-\sigma\Omega_{k}(\kappa)|>\frac{\nu}{\max(|j_{1}|,\ldots,|j_{p+1}|,|k|)^{\tau}}\,,\qquad\forall(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k,\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},-\sigma)\not\in\mathfrak{S}_{p+2}\ .

Indeed,
to any
(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k,\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},-\sigma)

we associate the multi–index

(\alpha,\beta)
as in (
7.12
) whose length is
|\alpha+\beta|=p+2
and satisfies

\vec{\sigma}_{p+1}\cdot{\Omega}_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1}}(\kappa)-\sigma\Omega_{k}(\kappa)=(\alpha-\beta)\cdot\vec{\Omega}(\kappa)
by (

).
Recalling (
7.13
), the vector

(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k,\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},-\sigma)\not\in\mathfrak{S}_{p+2}
if and only if
(\alpha,\beta)
is
not super action-preserving and
therefore

B.1
implies (
7.61
). Note also that, by Remark
7.10
, if
p
is odd, there are not super-action preserving indexes, i.e.
\mathfrak{S}_{p+2}=\emptyset
.
In view of (
7.61
) the coefficients in (
7.60
) are well defined.

Next we show that
G_{p}(V)V
is a vector field in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{p+1}^{-\varrho^{\prime}}
. As
\widetilde{R}_{p}(V)V
belongs to
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{-\varrho+\underline{\varrho}(p)+c(N,p)}_{p+1}
,

2.29

∎
